--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page acts as a secondary title page and the beginning of the author's preface "To the Reader." Elias Ashmole introduces the work as a sequel of sorts to his famous 1652 collection of alchemical poetry, "Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum."</meta>

<header>TO THE Reader</header>

# THE
## Way to Bliss.
---
->IN THREE BOOKS.<-
---
->TO THE<-
->Reader<-
->Published <note>original: "Made Publick"</note><-
## BY
## ELIAS ASHMOLE Esquire.
---
<image-desc>Decorative floral ornament featuring symmetrical scrolling foliage and stylized floral motifs.</image-desc>
---

Having formerly published my *British Chemical Theater* <note>original: "THEATRUM CHEMICUM BRITANNICUM," Ashmole's renowned 1652 collection of English alchemical literature</note>, it was immediately afterward the first of the things I received from the hands of God—who has done so much to support my many burdens <note>original: "to endure the multiplied"</note> and to sweeten my long and laborious life—that He was pleased to meet with me in a still and peaceful voice.

And because frequent questions have been asked of me concerning this work while it was still in manuscript <note>original: "in its non-perishable state," likely referring to the work's existence as a durable handwritten copy before being set in print</note>, it is now at last brought to light in the following manner.

<summary>Elias Ashmole introduces "The Way to Bliss" to his readers, explaining that he decided to publish the manuscript following the success of his previous work and in response to public interest.</summary>
<keywords>Elias Ashmole, The Way to Bliss, Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum, alchemy, 17th-century literature, Hermeticism, preface</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface to the reader. Elias Ashmole provides a personal update on the years following his 1652 publication of the "Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum."</meta>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut headpiece featuring three crowned heraldic emblems: a Tudor rose on the left, a fleur-de-lis in the center, and a thistle on the right, all set amidst ornate foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>

## TO THE
# R E A D E R .

It has now been a little over five years since I published the first *part* of my *British Chemical Theater* <note>original: "THEATRUM CHEMICUM BRITANNICUM," Ashmole's famous 1652 collection of English alchemical poetry</note>. Immediately afterward, my *studies* of that *nature* suffered most unfortunate *interruptions* due to the *start* of several troublesome <note>original: "vexatious"</note> *lawsuits* against me. But GOD not only enabled me to endure those violent <note>original: "impetuous"</note>, repeated *storms*, but a few *months* ago, He was pleased to reward <note>original: "sweeten"</note> my long suffering with a fair and peaceful *outcome* <note>original: "issue"</note>.

And because my *studies* in HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY <note>term: a tradition of spiritual and scientific inquiry based on writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, often associated with alchemy</note> could not withstand those aforementioned troublesome *obstacles* <note>original: "rubs"</note>—as *she* <note>note: Philosophy is often personified as a woman in this period</note> requires a serene *mind*, quiet *thoughts*, tireless *efforts*, and indeed the attention of the *whole man*—I was very reluctantly forced to set them aside. Yet, so that I might not completely leave the company of *Minerva* <note>note: the Roman goddess of wisdom and the arts, symbolizing scholarly pursuits</note>, who had nurtured me so kindly, I turned to other *studies*. The *nature* of these subjects was better suited to *disturbances* and allowed them—when unfortunately interrupted—to be taken up again with less difficulty; and their *variety* might also inspire a certain *interest* <note>original: "appetite"</note> and *delight*.

<meta>catchword: All</meta>

<summary>Elias Ashmole explains that his alchemical and Hermetic studies were interrupted for five years by difficult lawsuits, forcing him to pivot to other scholarly subjects that were less demanding of his constant focus.</summary>
<keywords>Elias Ashmole, Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum, Hermetic Philosophy, lawsuits, Minerva, alchemy, 17th-century scholarship, autobiography</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues Elias Ashmole's preface. He discusses his search for a collaborator to continue his alchemical anthology and his decision to publish this specific text to protect it from a plagiarized edition.</meta>

<header>To the Reader.</header>

Throughout all this time, I truly hoped to meet with *one person or another* who (being inclined to advance the honor of HERMES'S <note>original: "HERMES his"; referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy</note> *family*) would have taken the trouble to add a *second volume* to my aforementioned THEATER <note>original: "THEATRUM," referencing his 1652 collection of alchemical works, the *Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum*</note>. I hoped for this because those successive *troubles* (hanging so long and heavily upon me) had denied me the *leisure* to do it myself. I did not lack *invitations* for others to join me, nor did I withhold the free *contribution* of whatever I had so painstakingly *collected*. My *design* was to let the *world* see what excellent *men* we once had in our own *nation*—men famous for that kind of *philosophy* as well as any other *learning*, and *masters* of such a transcendent *secret*—and I had hoped this goal might have been furthered. Despite this, I have heard of nothing (so far) being done, and nothing even attempted.

<margin>John Heydon's</margin>
But instead, I lately came across a pretended *copy* of the following *discourse*, prepared for the *press*. Upon reading it, I found it mutilated with many *imperfections* and much damaged by several inconsistent *additions*. It was confessed that these were only made up of some scattered *shreds* and *fragments* collected from the whole *work*. Furthermore, it was intended that the *world* should mistake it for the *child* of one *Eugenius Theodidactus* <note>term: "God-taught nobleman"; a pseudonym used by John Heydon (1629–c. 1667), an author known for plagiarizing the works of alchemists like Thomas Vaughan</note>, being (by *re-baptism*) called *The Wise Man’s Crown*, or *Rosicrucian Medicine* <note>original: "Rosie-Crucian Physick"</note>. Under these *titles*, notice has been given of its upcoming release by other *books* published since then. 

Considering all of this, together with the *zeal* I have for this noble *science* and my *regret* to see so able a *champion* of it thus boldly and wickedly <note>original: "nefariously"</note> robbed and despoiled of his *honor*, I was loath to keep my *perfect copy* to myself any longer. Therefore, I resolved to venture it abroad (though unaccompanied) to prevent the *injury* that would otherwise be done to our dead *author* and the *world*. (I say "unaccompanied" because my past and present *engagements*—finishing the *productions* of those *hours* I snatched from the intervals of my recent *disturbances*—will not allow me time to pair it with the *associates* I formerly intended should complete one of the later *parts* of my above-mentioned THEATER.) However, (considering the *nature* of this *piece*,) it will appear appropriately enough by itself and will serve very well as a large *preface* to usher forth the remaining *volumes* (or any

<meta>catchword: thing</meta>

<summary>Elias Ashmole explains that he is publishing this text to preempt a corrupted and plagiarized version by John Heydon. He hopes it will serve as a preface to future volumes of his alchemical collection that he was unable to complete due to legal troubles.</summary>

<keywords>Elias Ashmole, John Heydon, Eugenius Theodidactus, Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum, alchemy, plagiarism, Rosicrucianism, Hermetic philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Elias Ashmole's preface. He has just explained his decision to publish this specific text to prevent a flawed and plagiarized version from circulating.</meta>

<header>To the Reader.</header>

...anything else) that shall be published on this *subject*.

As for our *author*, he was undoubtedly an Englishman, but until now he has been counted among the *anonymous* <note>original: "Anonymi"; Latin for nameless authors</note>. The book (his *offspring*) shows itself to be sufficiently *legitimate*, even though its true *father* remains unknown. <margin>X</margin> I have heard some remarkable *stories*, supported by *persuasive circumstances*, which might lead a trusting person to think that *Providence* was very clearly at work. It not only provided a diligent seeker of this *mysterious learning* <note>referring to alchemy</note> with the *original* manuscript itself, but most fortunately directed him to three *grains* of the *Powder*, tucked between two of its *leaves*, with which he performed a *projection* <term>the final stage of alchemy, where the "Powder of Projection" is used to transmute base metals into gold</term>. However, I do not wish to *fill the ears* <note>original: "Fly-blow the Ears"; a metaphor for contaminating or spoiling something with falsehoods, as flies lay eggs in meat</note> of my readers with tall tales. I can only modestly state that my copy was a transcript of that *original*.

The work seems to have been written around the beginning of the last (or the end of the previous) *century* <note>likely referring to the late 1500s or early 1600s</note>. The *author's* main goal is to use weighty and serious *arguments* and *examples* to prove the *possibility* of such a thing as the PHILOSOPHER'S STONE. Through this, he extensively demonstrates that Nature has shown greater *wonders* to the *world*, and that just as great things have been (and consequently can be) achieved through other weaker and smaller *means*, provided there is a proper, harmonious, and *philosophical combination* of *art* and *nature*. Yet, because such wonders are familiar and common among us, we often fail to notice them. This is a *discourse* filled with a variety of excellent, rational *content*, suited for both the learned and those of *simpler minds*. Indeed, I am bold enough to believe it will fully satisfy *both*, more so than anything else currently available of this *nature*. I believe many of the *deceptive arguments* <note>original: "captious Arguments"</note> previously used against the *truth* of this infallible *science* will find satisfactory *solutions* here, and will no longer have a place in any *discussion* guided by *sober reason*.

I must also inform my *reader* that this piece was so highly valued by the industrious *Dr. Everard* <note>John Everard (c. 1584–1641), a prominent preacher and translator of Hermetic and alchemical texts</note> that he was inspired to devote his efforts to the *marginal notes*. In these (like a skilled *philosopher* whose first task is to make *hidden* things *manifest*), he drew out and revealed what our *author's masterly pen* chose to conceal; and having

<meta>catchword: obtained</meta>

<summary>Elias Ashmole discusses the unknown English authorship of the text and claims its authenticity is supported by stories of a seeker finding the original manuscript alongside actual alchemical powder. He praises the work's rational defense of alchemy and notes that it includes commentary by the respected translator Dr. John Everard.</summary>

<keywords>anonymous author, Philosopher's Stone, Powder of Projection, Art and Nature, Dr. John Everard, alchemical science, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page concludes Elias Ashmole's preface to the reader. He describes how he acquired additional notes for the text and reflects on the varied reception that works on alchemy (the "Divine Science") typically receive from the public.</meta>

<header>To the Reader.</header>

Having obtained these *notes* (they were added to a *transcript* of this *work*, and both were clearly written in the *Doctor's own hand*) from a very close *friend* (an exceptionally *learned* man and a great *honor* to our *nation*), I was willing to make them *public* as well.

And now (I confess), despite all this, I do not expect that what I publish here will please every *taste* <note>original: "Palate"</note>. The *fate* of *men’s writings* is much like that of *money*, which sometimes passes as current currency and at other times is devalued or withdrawn from circulation. To this, I consider how we are not born with *fancies* and *appetites* that relish everything equally; it is as impossible to tailor a *coat for the moon* <note>a proverbial expression for an impossible task, as the moon is constantly changing its phase and size</note> as to print a book that can please every *temperament* <note>original: "Genius"</note>. Such and so varied are the general nature of our *inclinations*! Besides, I have often observed that *men*, both *wise* and *learned*, dislike or show no interest in some parts of *learning*, and yet by a secret willingness or *natural* force, they are carried away in *admiration* and *love* of other *branches* of it. I suppose this partly grows from the neglect of a strict and unbiased examination of their choice, which (if it were made) would appear to proceed more from *affection* than *judgment*.

But if anyone whose *ignorance* of, or *dislike* for, this *divine* and praiseworthy *science* <note>referring to alchemy</note> should think no better of the *work* than of a *spider's web* [fit only to be swept away], I shall nevertheless confidently hope it will fall into other hands. Such readers may consider the intricate craftsmanship of the *weave* <note>original: "Woofe"; the horizontal threads in a woven fabric, used here as a metaphor for the text's structure</note> and deem it worthy of their *contemplation*. They may observe how our *author* (like that clever *creature* working with her own *diligence*) has composed a *discourse* whose *excellencies* will not reveal themselves to the satisfaction of a superficial *glance* <note>original: "Eye"</note>, but only to the intentional and serious *investigator* <note>original: "Inquisitor"</note>. That such people may reap every possible *advantage* from their labor is the hearty desire of

->**E. ASHMOLE.**<-

April 16.
1 6 5 8.

<summary>Elias Ashmole concludes his preface by explaining that he obtained handwritten notes from a learned friend to include in the publication. He acknowledges that while some may dismiss the work as useless, serious students of alchemy will appreciate its intricate and well-constructed arguments.</summary>

# The Way to Bliss.

## THE FIRST BOOK.

---

### CHAPTER 1.

### *What* BLISS *and* HAPPINESS *are.*

<margin>(a) Cicero, *On Duties* <note>original: "Cic. Offic. lib. 1." Referring to *De Officiis*.</note>, Book 1.</margin>
If in all organized speeches and academic subjects, (a) it is first of all necessary to agree upon the subject at hand—what it is, and what its logic and limits [or definition] are—then it seems very necessary in this discourse on *The Way to Bliss* to show first what Bliss is, because it is a matter of much doubt and debate among scholars <note>original: "the Learned"</note>.

Anyone who is accustomed to observing and examining the logic and nature of things can easily perceive by the outward form and inward gifts of *Man*—which are unlike and surpass all...

<summary>This opening page introduces the philosophical necessity of defining "Bliss" before discussing how to achieve it, citing Cicero to support the importance of clear definitions in scholarly discourse.</summary>
<keywords>Bliss, Happiness, Cicero, De Officiis, definitions, human nature, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into the nature of Bliss, arguing that because humans are the highest creatures in the physical world, their ultimate purpose must be found in the divine rather than in material wealth, health, or even the angelic spirits.</meta>

<header>The Way to Bliss. BOOK 1 <note>original: "LIB. I."</note></header>

...other *creatures* <note>original: "Wights"; a term for living beings or people</note> that he was made for some notable end and purpose above the rest; and so not for *pleasure*, *honor*, *health*, or an abundance of *necessary outward things*, which they call *riches*, nor yet for any other matters which other *creatures* void of *intellect* <note>original: "Wit"</note> and *reason* seek and follow. Therefore, a *man* ought not to make any such thing his end and happiness, unless he thinks it reasonable for the master and superior craftsman to learn from the servant and the inferior. For what other pattern or goal do we have in the world to follow? None at all, because we are the best creatures in the world.

Then you might say it is outside the world, and among the blessed minds [ *or spirits* ] above and beyond all things. But we have not found it there either; for they are our *fellow-servants* and *subjects* under one *Almighty King*. Therefore, nothing remains but GOD and His happiness to be sought and set before us—not with the hope of overtaking and reaching it (that would be madness), but with the desire to attain as much of it as the relationship between Him and us will permit <note>original: "suffer"</note>. Or, if the immeasurable and boundless [ *or infinite* ] blessedness of GOD admits no comparison, it were best (following the example of the best men) to make the boundaries of our BLISS so much of the bliss of GOD as our whole power and nature can hold and carry.

Now then, if we knew that divine pattern and bliss of GOD, all would be well. And this, as almost all other truth (especially in matters of life and conduct, for which it was chiefly written), could be known and proven by the witness and record of *Holy Scripture* <note>original: "Holy Writ"</note>, if that were not too unusual and far removed from this purpose,

<summary>The author argues that human happiness cannot be found in earthly riches or even in the spirits of heaven, but only in striving toward the divine bliss of God. He suggests that while we cannot reach God's infinite state, we should aim to reflect as much of His nature as humanly possible.</summary>
<keywords>human purpose, divine bliss, reason, intellect, Holy Writ, nature of God, spiritual hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how to define the ultimate purpose of human life, moving from the divine pattern mentioned on the previous page to an evaluation of human authorities—poets and philosophers—to see if they can provide the true definition of Bliss.</meta>

# Chapter 1. The Way to Bliss.

...purpose, which is intended (as you see) to run through the midst of *Nature, Reason, and Philosophy*.

Therefore, since in this and all other matters I do not intend to lean too much on my own invention—because a *man* (especially a *young man*) is liable to stray—I will instead call others to counsel with me. These counselors can be nothing more than men, at most endowed with mature and sound reason and judgment in the course of nature <gloss>original: "Kinde"</gloss> and *philosophy*. Yet we will ensure, as much as possible, that they remain guided by the rule of *truth and reason*.

<margin>
(b) *Terence, Prologue to Andria; and Plato, Laws, Book 2* <note>original: "Terent. Prolog. in Andr. & Plat. Nom. β."</note>
(c) *Pindar, Olympian Ode 5 and Isthmian Ode 5* <note>original: "Pind. Olym. Od. 5. & Isthm. Od. 5."</note>
</margin>

Then, to find this happiness of *heaven* among men, to whom would it be best to turn? To the *poets*, do you think? No; because they always aim at a vain goal (b)—the approval of the people. We can see this in *Pindar*, one of the best of them (for I will not settle for the worst <note>original: "draw of the dregs"</note>), when he says, (c) "*If a man is rich and has his health with a contented mind and honor, let him not care to be a* GOD." This is a vain and worldly BLISS, God knows, and far from a divine nature.

Nor do we need to visit the lower and lesser schools of *philosophy*. Just as they are flawed and unsound in other areas of learning, so too in the matter of ethics <gloss>original: "Manners"</gloss> they are wrong to place our BLISS in *honor, pleasure, health*, or similar outward things. Nor should they define it by a *good life* alone, or even *virtue*.

*Plato* and *Aristotle*, because of their matchless understanding of natural things and their divine insight into the proper order of life and ethics <gloss>original: "Manners"</gloss>, have for many ages been most accepted by the wisest men and followed in all things. Therefore, regarding this high point of ethics which we have

<summary>The author argues that true Bliss cannot be found in the works of poets like Pindar, who value worldly health and wealth, or in lesser philosophies that focus on outward things; instead, one must look toward the higher reasoning of Plato and Aristotle.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Reason, Philosophy, Bliss, Pindar, Plato, Aristotle, Ethics, Virtue, worldly happiness</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the evaluation of ancient philosophers' definitions of "Bliss" or the ultimate human good, picking up from the previous page's critique of lesser schools to focus on Plato and Aristotle.</meta>

# Chapter 1. The Way to Bliss.

...have in hand, let us see what these men believe, and how near they come to the right line of *Truth* that we spoke of before.

To begin with *Plato*, the source <note>original: "Spring"</note> of this *Philosophy*: his *Bliss*, as he argues in the *Philebus*, as near as I could gather from such a long and rambling dialogue, is nothing but *Pleasure*.

<margin>(d) *Cicero, On the Nature of the Gods, Book 1* <note>original: "Cicer. de Nat. Deor. lib. 1."</note></margin>
<margin>(e) *Plato, in the Philebus* <note>original: "Plat. in Philæb."</note></margin>
<margin>(f) *Plato, in the Theaetetus* <note>original: "Plat. in Theæt."</note></margin>
And yet this divine man does not mean (lest you should be surprised) to join that "herd of swine" <note>A common derogatory reference to the followers of Epicurus, who were often accused of pursuing only physical lusts</note>—though they were not the originators of that foul opinion, but merely watered their gardens from other men's springs, as (*d*) *Cicero* <note>original: "Tully"</note> says—to throw open all the gates of the senses and let in everything that comes. Instead, he opens only a few narrow windows to receive pure delight, unmixed with any grief; specifically, (*e*) *delight in colors, harmony <note>original: "Concent"</note>, and certain smells, and in health, wisdom, and virtue*. And again he says in the *Theaetetus*, (*f*) *that justice and holiness, together with wisdom, make us like* GOD.

<margin>(g) *Aristotle, Ethics, Book 10, Chapters 7 and 8* <note>original: "Aristot. Ethic. lib. 10. cap. 7. & 8."</note></margin>
Letting those two passages suffice for him, let us come to *Aristotle*. Just as there are two sorts of men—one disposed to deal with others, who are called "worldly men," and another quite the opposite, inclined to live alone and seek knowledge, who are called "philosophers"—so he, in his *Ethics* <note>original: "Book of Manners," referring to the Nicomachean Ethics</note>, (*g*) establishes two different goals and *Blisses*. For the first, he designates *Virtue* (meaning an active, not an idle virtue), adorned and protected by outward assistance and gifts of the body and fortune. For the second, he designates the *knowledge of the highest things*. He places this second goal above the first for many reasons argued toward the end of that book, but especially because GOD, whom we ought to follow, lives that same life of contemplation.

These are the best foundations of BLISS that ever any

# Chapter 1. The Way to Bliss.

...any *philosopher* has laid at any time (for not a single one has completely built it up); let us see how they are squared <note>In the context of masonry or building, "squared" means how well their ideas are measured and aligned with the truth</note>.

<margin>(h) *Cicero, On the Nature of the Gods, Book 2* <note>original: "cicer. de Nat. Deor. lib. 2."</note></margin>
If the stall-fed *Epicurean* <note>original: "Epicure"; a follower of Epicurus, often stereotyped as living purely for physical pleasure</note> may again be justly rebuked and counted as an ungodly person—whom no heavenly thoughts ever touched—for introducing (h) an idle God who neither rules the world nor cares for it; how then can *Aristotle* be seen as wrongfully accused of impiety, and for that reason banished from the *Academy*, if there were no other proof against him than what he says in that place: that GOD leads no other life than this beholding and gazing *life* of His? Is it not an idle and, as it were, a greedy and envious life—turned back upon itself and estranged from all outward action toward others? Yes, (and that) according to his own and all other men’s understanding? Then to confront him with his worthy master, *Plato*: if that were the best life, or the life of GOD, why did GOD make the world? He lived so before, if that had been the best life; <margin>(i) *Plato, in the Timaeus* <note>original: "Plato in Timæo."</note></margin> (i) but because He was good, He wanted others to enjoy His goodness; and therefore He was busy in making, and is yet busy in ruling, the world. And yet indeed, it is no "business" as we define it—that is, no worry or trouble—but an outward deed and action, entirely opposite to the inward deed of a *musing mind* that only aims at its own good state, which is *wisdom* and *knowledge*.

<margin>(k) *Aristotle, Politics, Book 7, Chapter 3* <note>original: "Arist. Polit. lib. 7. cap. 3."</note></margin>
But if he denies all this—as he likely will, because to increase the heap of sin he grants no beginning <note>Aristotle argued that the universe was eternal and had no moment of creation</note>—then what can be greater evidence than his own writings, which completely contradict one another? But you may say, "We have raised our **BLISS** up high and made it a fair and goodly work, but it is more fit for the dwelling of those single and pure Minds [*or Spirits*] above—which they call Messengers [*or **Angels***]—than for us men, who are so buried here below in these earthly bodies that we are scarcely able to look up toward it." (p) Aristotle, *Ethics*, Book 10, chapter 8 <note>original: "Arist. Eth. lib. 10 c 8."</note>.

The study of (p) **Morals** <note>original: "Manners"</note> and of (q) **Politics** <note>original: "State"</note>, with good counsel, often provides enough bodily and external goods to assist in this matter. However, these are not the cause of **BLISS** itself; they are merely like a *musical instrument* <note>The author uses the analogy of a musical instrument to show that while you need the tool to make the music, the tool is not the music itself.</note>. Thus, we see that *Plato* calls these things his *servants* and *helpers*.

Indeed, I admit that this full and high degree of *Happiness* (I mean that measure described above) comes freely and easily to free and lively spirits. But for the rest of us, it is impossible without other external means and aids. Nevertheless, these shall not be counted as part of the actual structure of **BLISS** required to make it whole; rather, they are like separate steps and stairs leading up to it.

Since these aids are so necessary, it is equally necessary to define them accurately and list them in a proper account—something those *philosophers* fail to do. If there are too few, our *Happiness* might be hindered <note>original: "halt"</note>; if there are too many, the unnecessary <note>original: "idle"</note> parts might in time infect and spoil the rest. We might fear this regarding *Plato's* "first three delights," even though they are not harmful in themselves. To put it simply, the total summary is this:

To obtain as much **Happiness** as our nature is capable of receiving and holding, the body must first be willing and obedient, and then a store of necessary external things must be ready and at hand. Everyone knows this. As for the body, it is "obedient" when it is *long-lived, healthy, young, clear-headed*, and *temperate*. When all these advantages come together, we may be happy if we choose to be; but if any are missing, we never shall be—no matter what we do—as we will hear later.

Finally, let us organize these things in order. Comparing **BLISS** to a *family*, let us make that loving

### Chapter II.

#### *A refutation <note>original: "Reproof"</note> of the common and weaker <note>original: "lighter"</note> arguments directed against the Way to Bliss.*

Now that we know what **BLISS** and **HAPPINESS** are, we may begin the journey whenever we wish and show how all people can be blessed. In this task, I am completely deprived of any help from the *Greeks* <note>original: "Grecians"</note>, who were always more talented at speaking and thinking well than at actually doing or performing anything. (If their sayings had been consistent and unified, they would have placed **BLISS**, along with other good things, within the power and reach of all people.) Therefore, I must seek aid from *Egypt*, a people so far...

...surpassing all other nations, as it is better and closer to **GOD** to work and perform great, wondrous things than to merely behold and look upon them.

For it is delivered in ancient and true records that one **HERMES** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure often identified as the founder of alchemy and a contemporary of Moses.</note>, a king and *law-giver* of that country [Egypt], a man of rare and divine gifts in knowledge above all who ever lived, found a *medicine* able to bring all people to that **BLISS** mentioned before, and left it behind in writing to his people. After him, it was for a long time secretly produced <note>original: "wrought"</note> and used by the *wiser sort* until at last it crept abroad and stole into *Arabia* when she flourished in arms and learning. There, it received the name which it now commonly keeps: the **PHILOSOPHER'S STONE**. From there, in the same secret and disguised manner—for that is its nature, as fits such a deep *secret*—it has traveled and spread itself over all nations, now and then opening and discovering itself to a *few* of the better and wiser company.

Therefore, this is **THE WAY TO BLISS** which I intend to take. And at the same time, I will prove it is no pleasant dream or happy tale—even if it were true, as the common proverb says of it—but rather, as it is in *nature*, a heroic and almost divine deed, scarcely to be reached or matched with any words. I vow it is a true and certain story, a thing often done, and to be done again just as often.

I am unfit, I grant, and unable to bear so great a burden <note>original: "Burthen"</note>, except that the great desire I have both to defend the truth from slander and to do good to those who love it makes it light and easy. And again, this hope upholds me: that if I chance to stumble or faint

First, they say, since there are seen in all places and times so many hundreds who study this **Art** with great pain, care, and skill, and put the recipes <note>original: "Receipts"</note> into practice—surely, if they were as true and flawless as other arts are, some people would appear to hit the mark. But notice how light and weak they are, and how easily they are wiped away: for how could the acts and deeds of these **Philosophers** come into the writings and records of men (to begin with them), when they have ever shunned the fame, and indeed the very company, of such men? Now, when people decide that credibility should be denied to these men's own reports and testimony, it is a sign of one of two things. Either their own honesty and testimony are of such little weight that they judge others by themselves, or else their thoughts are vain and fanciful—puffed up, I mean, with that new kind of self-love and arrogant <note>original: "over-weening"</note> wisdom that seeks to elevate themselves by tearing down established authorities. In practice, it often turns out that while they strive to avoid the lake of *Superstition*, they accidentally run headlong off the cliff of *Impiety* <note>original: "Impiety," here meaning godlessness or a lack of reverence for truth</note>. For if such a wild assault and entry is allowed against the credibility and authority of writers—who are the very life of *Antiquity* and the light of *Memory*—great darkness and confusion will soon overwhelm the world. Indeed, this skepticism could go so far that nothing would be believed or judged true unless it is seen with one's own eyes. (w) It would be as if those who live deep in the interior of a country refused to believe the sea exists. Such a view is not only foolish and childish for any person, but also (heaven help me if I do not speak my mind) wicked and godless for us *Christians*, whose entire *Religion*, as *St. Augustine* says, stands upon that ground of faith.

into gold, folly into wisdom, vice into virtue, weakness into long life, and all diseases into sound health, and age into vitality <note>original: "Lustiness"</note> and youth again—how can you disprove them? When did you see the contrary? You scarcely know the nature of these deeds and effects, for they require great knowledge; but the *doing, cause, and workman*—that is, *this medicine*—you never saw, nor can you imagine what it is, much less conceive the *reason, strength,* and *nature* of it. Indeed, you see nothing, but grope and blunder in the dark, like blindfolded men at all things. Well then, let us return to our purpose. There is a nation of ***Wise Men*** dwelling in a land as much more blessed [than yours] as yours is than theirs—that is, just as they live underground and you live upon the surface...

...thereof, so these men inhabit the edge and border of *Heaven*. <margin>x</margin>
But they will not be dismissed so easily, and they follow as closely from another direction: they argue that so many have been, and are daily seen to spend <note>original: "wear"</note> their lives in *Alchemy*, only to find nothing that is good. Instead, for the most part, they find the opposite: namely, untimely and unusual death, sickness, and old age, instead of *long life*, *health*, and *youth*; and always smoke instead of *gold*, and folly instead of *wisdom*. Very nearly as often, they develop bad and wicked characters instead of good and honest natures. (For, by boiling themselves for so long in such deceitful materials, as though they were burnt at the bottom of the pot, they most commonly carry an unpleasant reputation <note>original: "unsavoury smack"</note> for ever after.) They argue it is a plain sign the trade is vain, false, and deceitful. This is the third charge they bring against us; let us see how to endure and resist it.

The most wise and great *philosophers*, although they knew God had made all mankind for that *happy life* mentioned above, and that it was at first enjoyed (or else it would have been made in vain), and that by the corruption of bad custom...

<header>Chapter II. The Way to Bliss.</header>

<margin>X (a)</margin> Through custom (and by His secret appointment), our humankind has strayed from its true nature; therefore, it may be restored, because this is a deviation and not the original intent of Nature. (These insights gave the sages the opportunity to seek the remedy.) Yet they thought it would be unlawful, and directly against the Will of GOD, for everyone to be restored. It seems He purposefully sowed both Good and Bad, and a great abundance of both together, just as we see them. He did this so that if everyone were the same and in a single state of happiness, the great variety of activity and industry—and thus society and the Commonwealth among men—would be completely destroyed. This is similar to how, if the four primary, competing <term>Seeds</term> <note>original: "Seeds"; referring to the four elements—earth, water, air, and fire—which were believed to be in a constant state of "striving" or conflict to create the physical world.</note> (from which all things are made and spring) were all identical and at peace with one another, everything would be still and quiet. There would be no succession, change, or variety in the world, and thus there would be no world at all. <margin>(o)</margin> For when GOD set His mind to the building of the world, He intended to create a grand and beautiful work, fitting for the power, wisdom, and pleasure of such a Builder. Therefore, He made it an active and ever-changing work, because no power or skill is demonstrated, and no delight is found, in something that is always the same or motionless. <margin>X</margin> But a light step for the sake of speed is always best on such ground; let us move on.

Therefore, following the example and, as it were, the secret inspiration and prompting of GOD, after our predecessors had found this <term>Restorative</term> <note>original: "Reſtorative"; the alchemical Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, capable of restoring health and perfection.</note> and used it for a time, they intended to leave it to posterity as becomes good men. They decided to hide it safely, as if in a strong castle, in which all the wide gates and common, easy entrances were firmly shut and barred. They left only one small and secret back door open, <margin>X</margin> protected by a winding <term>Maze</term> <note>original: "Maze"; alchemical authors famously used "Decknamen" (cover-names), allegories, and complex metaphors to ensure only the "worthy" or "initiated" could understand their instructions.</note>, so that the

...the best sort, by intelligence, hard work, and foresight <note>original: "Wit, Pains and Providence"</note>, might come into the appointed BLISS, while the rest stand back forsaken. <margin>(n) Peder Sørensen, *The Idea of Philosophical Medicine*, Chapter 1. <note>original: "Severin. Sørensen (1542–1602) was a Danish physician and follower of Paracelsus who argued that nature should be studied through observation rather than just ancient texts.</note></margin> Their plan and method is this: first, they hide themselves in obscure and untrodden places, so that they might be free from the power of princes and the eyes of the wicked *world*. Therefore, when godless and unlearned men—obsessed with profit and status—presume against the will of *Minerva* <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and strategic warfare.</note> to manipulate these words when they should instead be focusing on the actual substances <note>original: "Things"; likely referring to the physical chemical elements or the practical laboratory work of alchemy.</note>, they distort and twist them a hundred ways. <margin>(o) Speech is more easily molded than wax and similar things. — Plato, *Republic*, Book 9. <note>original: "εὐπλαστότερον κηροῦ & τῶν τοιούτων λόγος. Then, if they put these methods and fantasies into practice as quickly as their fingers itch to do so—and fail just as quickly, as they inevitably must—will they then claim they followed our rules and precepts, and that they tested our work only to find it false? That would be as if a skillful <note>original: "cunning"</note> archer and *huntsman* had provided obscure rules for *shooting* and *hunting* to his countrymen, and these rules by chance had fallen into the hands of another wild and uneducated nation. This nation, simply misled by misunderstanding his intent and meaning, might have made plows to shoot with, or used their oxen to gore the prey; and then, failing in their goal, they cried out and blamed...

<margin>X <note>This "X" in the transcription likely marks a specific citation or cross-reference in the original margin.</note></margin>

Neither would I have you pursue the next argument so aggressively or be so insistent upon it: the idea that even if our men had cause to hide their work and practice, they still would have shown the fruit and effect of it by advancing themselves, as others do, to honor and pleasure, rather than living like the outcasts <note>original: "refuse"</note> of the world in such mean conditions and wretchedness. <margin>X</margin>

This is because wealth is, for the most part, unruly and ambitious; for that reason, riches are granted no liberty...

Therefore, is it any wonder that our men acted this way? They did no more than *Wisdom* requires, and no more than all wise men have ever taught and practiced. This is not done (as (q) *Pliny* writes of *Hermolinus* <note>Also known as Hermotimus of Clazomenae, a philosopher whose soul was said to leave his body to wander and bring back news from distant places.</note>) by literally sending the mind out of the body to gather and bring home news. ...of his friends, that he carried all his own things with him—which was nothing but a naked body.

<margin>(u) Aristotle, *Nicomachean Ethics*, book 10, chapter 7. <note>original: "Arist. Ethic. lib. 10. cap. 7."</note></margin>
*Aristotle* is of the very same mind as *Plato*, as appears notably in his (u) last book on ethics <note>original: "Book of Manners," referring to the *Nicomachean Ethics*</note>, where he has laid down many sound reasons why this life is best, and is, and ought to be, chosen by wise men. He says it is because it is the most quiet life, fullest of true delight, and best supplied with all necessary things—for indeed, it lacks nothing. Just as a mind is divine in relation to a body, so is the life of the mind (which is what we are speaking of) in relation to a social and worldly life. And again, if our minds are *ourselves*, it is appropriate to lead our own life rather than the life of a stranger. But last of all—and this is worth everything—it is because God, our only pattern, leads no other life but this.

I could be very long-winded if I chose to search for and examine this matter further, but here is enough to show the purpose and reasoning our men of <term>Egypt</term> <note>A reference to the legendary Egyptian origins of alchemy and Hermetic philosophy.</note> had, if it was their choice, to choose this kind of life that the world so despises. But what if I could show that they were deprived of all choice and free will, and driven by the force of necessity to do the same? Wouldn't that stop the loudest mouths, do you think <note>original: "trow you"</note>, in all this talkative company? Let us know first that the mind of man, having come from that high city of <term>Heaven</term>, desires of its own accord to continue living that heavenly life—that is, the blessed life described above. And if there is any obstacle <note>original: "lett"</note>, as there easily is, it lies in the weight and coarseness of our bodies, weighing our minds down to the ground and to all our own muddy affairs. Furthermore, after our men have obtained this <term>Golden Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note>, which is so famous in the world, they do not act as others think they would, and would themselves do...

...straightway run to their money chests, but first and chiefly "gild" <note>To "gild" the body refers to the ingestion or application of the medicinal elixir, often called the "tincture of gold," to achieve perfect health.</note> their bodies with it. Therefore, after they have used that powerful, refined, and balanced medicine to scour out all impurities and imbalances <note>original: "distemperature," referring to an imbalance of the bodily humors</note> of the body—which are the only obstacles to understanding and good manners, as we shall hear later—and have thereby left the *mind* at liberty and almost in her original state of freedom, she (and they together), setting aside and casting down all earthly matters, must naturally return to their own former divine life again, as far as the condition and state of *man* will allow. And so, even suppose you find your own dark and dusky eyesight so easily captured by every foul and vain worldly beauty, yet you must not judge these heavenly *men* by your own standard, but rather think that the extremely sharp and clear vision of their understanding is easily able to see the blemish and avoid the bait of common love.

Therefore, to conclude this point at last: since this *blessed craft* and *WAY TO BLISS* of *HERMES* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy and Hermetic philosophy.</note>, for all they know, may be true and honorable, let the common and unlearned people hold their judgment, and leave the trial and examination of any further matter to the wise and learned.

<margin>(w) Suidas in *Chemistry* <note>original: ".Χημεία."</note> and Caelius Rhodiginus, book 7, chapter 2; Pancirolli, section on Alchemy; Michael Maier, *Symbols of the Golden Table*, book 1; Paulus Diaconus, *Life of Diocletian Caesar*; and Orosius, book 17, chapter 16.</margin>

<margin>(x) Seneca, *On the Brevity of Life*, chapter 13</margin>
...and were never fathered by Hermes or any of his followers <note>original: "sons," referring to his intellectual heirs</note>. Therefore, there is no reason they should slander the name and lineage of Hermes; instead, they should bear the burden of their own faults. They can be distinguished and known from the holy, stinging, and productive *bee*—first by their loud words and boasting, and then (as naturally follows) by their stingless and defenseless weakness in protecting true learning. And thirdly, they are known by their sloth and idleness. For although they never stop <note>original: "lyn"</note> moving, yet, (x) as *Seneca* says, *they are busy doing nothing* <note>original Latin: "operosè nihil agunt"</note>. They laboriously accomplish nothing because everything they do lacks purpose; it is all fruitless and unprofitable.

But the Emperor *Diocletian* lacked this discerning *wisdom*. He rashly attacked everyone and burned their *books*, much like the story of *Lycurgus*, who cut down the *vines* to stop the people from getting drunk. Would it not have been better to have brought the water springs closer and, as *Plato* says, *to have bridled that mad god with the sober one?* <note>This refers to the practice of diluting wine (associated with Bacchus, the "mad god") with water to encourage moderation rather than total prohibition.</note> In the same way, the Emperor, with better advice, could have tempered the heat of *alchemy* with the cooling influence <note>original: "cooling card," a period idiom for a restraining or sobering factor</note> of *discretion*. He could have made it a lawful art for only a small number of people to practice under specific regulations. This would have been counsel worthy of a wise prince: neither letting the hope of such a great treasure be lost over a small annoyance, nor risking a certain great thing—namely, the lives and property of his *subjects*, which were well and orderly established—for an uncertain hope, no matter how great it might be.

# THE SECOND BOOK.

## CHAPTER I.

### Concerning LONG LIFE.

After addressing the common arguments that the unlearned use to discredit <note>original: "deface"</note> this excellent *science* <note>Here, "science" refers to the Hermetic or alchemical arts.</note>, we must move forward and face the arguments of the learned. These scholars consider the *work* to be impossible and the *worker* <note>original: "Workman"</note> to be conceited, false, and deceitful because the great deeds and effects that are promised—namely, to make all people *long-lived, healthy, young, rich, wise*, and *virtuous*—are beyond any skill of theirs or of their ancestors, the *Greeks* <note>original: "Gracians"</note>. I must, as I have been tasked, prove that these great acts and deeds can be done and per-

# THE WAY TO BLISS. BOOK II.

...formed by other weaker means than Hermes's Medicine <note>original: "HERMES MEDICINE"; a reference to the alchemical elixir attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</note>. And I must do this with more effort and diligence, because once this path and entry are established in their hearts, the great and marvelous truth of this famous Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note> may more easily enter and take possession.

But in such a variety of difficult and slippery subjects, where would it be best to start? Which path should I take first? Is it not fitting that the means and aids toward Bliss should be settled and cleared before we arrive at Bliss itself? And among those aids, we should give *Long Life* the first place—if not for its own merit, then for its benefit and necessity. It is necessary for all states <note>original: "Common-wealths"</note> and private individuals to first seek to live before they seek to live well, even though living well is the ultimate goal. Therefore, let us see what *Long Life* is and how all people may achieve it.

But why do we hurry so much? We should be slow and cautious in such a significant matter. We must ensure that before we venture upon such a long path and a journey of so many days, we are well provided and furnished with all things. In this, I hope that if I do not have enough of my own—or if, in a frugal manner, even when I am well-stocked myself, I borrow to avoid lending—it will not damage my reputation <note>original: "Credit"</note>. Instead, it should be seen as a safe and cautious way to prevent robbery both at home and abroad, especially if I borrow from those men who are most famous and well-regarded.

<margin>x</margin> These should be my friends from *Egypt* and *Arabia* (though we have their secret help now and then); they are indeed the most capable and the closest to me, if they were...

**GOD**, because he was good, (*a*) did not begrudge <note>original: "grieve"</note> having others enjoy his goodness (that is, to exist and to live well). From this springs the (*d*) *Love* which we see develop between them, and the great desire to be joined and coupled together again.

Warm *(p)* ashes alone will never achieve it <note>This refers to the "gentle heat" required for alchemical transformation, which cannot be achieved by simple fire or hot ashes.</note>. What more do we need? Imagine a heavenly flame produced by a high-quality *(q)* "burning water" <term>burning water</term> <note>original: "burning Water"; likely referring to *aqua ardens* or alcohol, used by alchemists as a solvent and a source of clean, "philosophical" fire.</note>. When this water flames upon your hand or a dry cloth, it heats them both gently without causing any harm or destruction. Now, for the raw material (the "Female" principle) to be ready for processing, it must first be "open"—that is, soft and moist. Furthermore, it should not consist of just one substance, nor of many identical substances. If it were, the material would remain stagnant and unchanged. Instead, when stirred by the workman, the different parts must rise up, struggle, and break each other down through constant transformation. This continues until they finally reach a state of harmony, rest, and stability. To ensure that this harmony might be disturbed again to allow for further change—the ultimate goal of the work—**God** initially threw in the *(t)* four famous "fighting enemies" (the elements). Yet, in the soft and open raw material, only two are visible at the beginning: *(u)* Earth and Water in a single mixture. This exists *(w)* before the diligent Soul draws out and develops the rest: *Fire* out of *Earth*, and out of *Water* that breath-like, windy thing called *Air*.

<margin>
(x) Cardano, *On Subtlety*, Book 2.
</margin>
Consequently, *(x)* if there were a large amount of *Earth*, a small amount of *Water*, and great *Heat* to mingle them, *Fire* would reveal itself and dominate. If there were only a small amount of *Heat* applied to the same measure of Earth and Water, Earth would rule the rest.

If, on the other hand, a small amount of *Earth* and much *Water* are subjected to only a little *Heat*, the result will be raw and watery. If the same quantity is subjected to a stronger *Heat*, an *Airy* substance arises, which is called a fat and oily *Body*.

<margin>(y) Cornelius Agrippa, *On Occult Philosophy*, Book 1, Chapter 14; Marsilio Ficino, *On Obtaining Life from the Heavens*, Book 3, Chapter 3; and Jean Fernel, *On the Hidden Causes of Things*, Book 1, Chapter 10.</margin>

Therefore, when the *Soul* descends upon the *Matter* <note>original: "Stuff"</note>, clothed in a fine, windy coat of the purest *Air* nearest to *Heaven*—called *Ether*—its moist, mild heat moves and alters it in many different ways. Without the mediation of this *(y) Medium* <note>original: "Mean"; referring to the Ether as the intermediary between spirit and matter</note>, the two extremes—complete strangers to each other—would never negotiate or agree. This process produces many varieties and kinds of things, differing according to the *Strength* of the active force and the *Responsiveness* <note>original: "Obedience"</note> of the matter.

<margin>(z) Ovid, *Metamorphoses*, Book 1; Marsilio Ficino, *Arguments on Plato’s Critias* and *Arguments on Meno*; and Francis Bacon (Lord St. Albans), *Natural History*, Century 9, Experiment 100. (a) Tommaso Campanella, *On the Sense of Things*, Book 4, Chapter 19; and Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, *Conclusions according to Avicenna*, 5. (b) Aristotle, *On the Generation of Animals*, Book 2, Chapter 5; and Girolamo Cardano, *On Variety*, Book 7, Chapter 37. (c) Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo, *On Northern Regions*; Girolamo Cardano, *On Variety*, Book 7, Chapter 37; and Dithmar Blefken, *Description of Iceland*.</margin>

Thus, because during that initial separation of the fine and *Male* part, *(z)* the matter was thoroughly tossed and mingled, and the *Heat* of *Heaven* was consequently very fierce and eager (like a hot *Summer* following a wet *Spring*), these two causes worked together perfectly. All *Living Beings* <term>Wights</term> <note>original: "Wights"; an archaic term for creatures or people</note>, *(a) Man* included, were created in the same way, without any seed being sown other than by the great *Seedsman* of *Heaven* upon the common matter of *Earth* and *Water*. 

This is still seen in the natural *Cultivation* <note>original: "Tillage"</note> found in those imperfect *Creatures* <note>original: "Wights"</note> that some call *Spontaneous Generations* <term>Start-ups</term> <note>original: "Start-ups"; refers to the belief that some animals like insects or mice could spring fully formed from decaying matter or mud</note>, which spring forth of their own accord. We are easily led to this conclusion if we consider how all kinds of *Plants* grow by themselves in certain places without any planting or sowing; how *(b)* some kinds of *Fish* in the *Sea* are only *Female*; and also what a great abundance of *Fish* *(c)* exists in the frozen *Countries* because of the intense heat and richness...

...of the waters; and especially that upon the slimy and hot land of *Egypt*, there are still some flesh-and-blood <note>original: "bloody"</note> and <margin>(d) Girolamo Cardano, *On Subtlety*, Book 2.</margin> perfect *land-creatures* <note>original: "Land-wights," meaning living beings</note> (such as *Hares* (d) and *Goats*, etc.) fashioned and made in this way.

But because later the well-mixed, fertile, and refined matter <note>original: "Stuff"</note> and the strong, active heat failed (as it inevitably must over time), and yet the great *Lord* intended for the continual movement, change, and succession of life to persist, these same two appropriate causes were duly maintained by continual succession (e) within the bodies of perfect *creatures* <margin>(e) Petrus Bonus, *The Precious New Pearl*, Chapter 25.</margin>. This involves the matter <note>original: "Stuff"</note> in the female and the heat in both sexes—and indeed, as far as was necessary, in seeded *plants* as well.

Now we must also understand that this heavenly *Soul* (which is called *Spirit* when it is clothed in that airy body) not only moves and works <margin>(f) Fortunio Liceti, *On Life*, Book 2, Chapter 11, and Marcello Palingenio Stellato, *The Zodiac of Life* (the section on Libra).</margin> with its heat, but also (f) consumes <note>original: "wasteth"</note> the matter for food. For nothing that is created is able to maintain its state and existence without its proper and compatible food and sustenance. For age and natural <note>original: "kindly"</note> death come from decay <note>original: "Rottenness"</note>, which results from stagnant <note>original: "stillness of"</note> *Heat* and a failure to preserve <note>original: "salt"</note> and refresh the body's parts.

The world is full of such examples. 

And look, on the other hand, at the *elephant*. # The Way to Bliss. Book II.

Now that we know what *Long Life* is and its cause, let us see whether all *people* <note>original: "Men"</note> achieve it or not, and then how they might achieve it.

<margin>
(d) Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, <note>original: "Tusc. quaest."</note> Book 3; and Cardan, *On Variety*, <note>original: "de var."</note> Book 7, chapter 34.
</margin>
In the beginning, all *mankind*, by the will and appointment of *Nature* <note>original: "Kinde"</note>, was *healthy* <note>original: "Sound"</note> and *vigorous* <note>original: "Lusty"</note>, and lived long. All the decay and corruption we see today—which falsely appears to be a weak *condition* of our *nature*—crept in through our own *disorder* little by little. By continually producing similar *children*, this corruption eventually spread and became deeply rooted, until it became, as it were, a fixed *state*, *nature*, and *type* of *man*. Therefore, by maintaining good order within *ourselves*, this state can be reformed and brought back to its ancient *condition*. 

But how can we prove this? If God and *Nature* have ordained *man* for a *Divine End* and *Bliss* above all other creatures, and yet some *beasts* live longer than our average lifespan—and even longer than the limits set above (as *(d) Theophrastus* complained, marveling at the fact)—then surely we ought to live just as long, or longer, by the laws of *Nature*, *right*, and *reason*. Indeed, we did so at first, before we fell through our own faults, which can be corrected.

But I may happen to deal with some who will neither acknowledge the *justice* of God nor accept the ultimate *purpose* <note>original: "End"</note> of *man*. For those who have so far abandoned their own *humanity*, I will present *natural causes*. I will explain and lay before them the different types and categories of *living creatures* <note>original: "Wights"</note>—both *men* and *beasts*. Perhaps then, those who are a monstrous and questionable *sort* between the two—meaning they are *beasts* on the inside, clothed only in the outward shape of *men*—may better judge both. (As the poets describe such misshapen *monsters*—they know my meaning, and it is not worth further elaboration).

# The Way to Bliss. Book II.

<margin>(i) Aristotle, *On Respiration*, chapter 6. See Fortunio Liceto, *On Secret Lamps*, Book 4, chapter 9; and Aristotle, *On the Generation of Animals*, Book 2, chapter 4.</margin>
...the more he surpasses a beast in *intellect* <note>original: "Wit"</note>, (i) so much more does he burn in *vital heat* above it. For *intellect* springs out of the clarity of the body, and this clarity arises from *heat*, as I will prove in its proper place hereafter.

Now, if this first point is established and granted, the next is quickly made—just as one match is lit by another. It is consistent with the justice of *Nature*, which creates nothing in vain, to match this greedy *heat* with a store of good *fuel* <note>original: "Meat," here meaning sustenance or fuel for the body's fire</note>—that is, of firm and fine primary *moisture* in a suitable proportion. Otherwise, surely, says *Heraclitus*, the officers of justice—the *Furies*—would soon apprehend her.

To be brief: both this point, the former, and the third likewise—namely, a dense, fine body—are all clarified, if it is true that *man* in his creation is the most thoroughly and finely mixed and refined <note>original: "broken"</note> of all the lower *creatures*. As we heard just now, this was decreed in the council of the best *philosophers*. For if nothing creates except *heat*, then nothing creates well except much *heat*—provided there are no other differences in *souls*, as was said above. And if the beginnings are well and firmly mingled, and the *concoction* <note>original: "Concoction," the physiological process of refining or "cooking" nutrients into bodily substance</note> holds, they must necessarily gather themselves together into a dense form as well, to create another cause—indeed, the final one. For what is firm, fine *oil* and *fatness* but *water* (which flows within us, as our *brain* demonstrates) thoroughly mingled and raised into an *airy*, or rather into an *ethereal*, dense substance? But if you will not accept this decree, then consider and weigh this one example: that although *man* is more given 
<margin>(k) Girolamo Cardano, *On Subtlety*, Book 12, page 468.</margin>
(k) to *lust* than any other *living creature* <note>original: "Wight"</note>, and thereby dries up his body—plainly stripping away more than any other and weakening all the aids to *long life* together—

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

...held and led forth toward those boundaries and that end of *long life* previously established.

Although I have done as much as can reasonably be required of me in this section—which was designated to show the possibility of the *matter*—I believe it is better to do good through plainness of speech (which is the goal of my writing) than to achieve my purpose through subtle arguments. Therefore, I will approach you and venture upon that point as well, however difficult and desperate it may seem, hoping not that *Fortune* will favor bold *men*, but that **GOD** will favor good *men*.

Since there are three causes of *life* and *existence*: the *life* and *soul* itself; its food, which is the *primary moisture* <note>original: "first Moisture"; a reference to "radical moisture," a concept in medieval medicine believed to be the vital fluid or "lamp oil" that sustains the flame of life</note>; and the *structure* and *temperament* of the body that contains them both—let us take them all in order and see how they may be preserved and kept together. We shall begin with the last one first, because it is the least significant and the easiest to address.

<margin>(n) Plato, *Timaeus*, near the end. Paracelsus, *On the Method of Preparing Medicines*, treatise 1, volume 5, page 151.</margin>
It is decreed by the Law of *Nature* (n) that no body, whether composite or simple, can live or maintain its state and existence without two helps or supports: namely, *nourishment* <note>original: "Meat"</note> and *exercise*, each according to its kind and nature. For example, in solitary and simple or subtle bodies—for this is obvious in the highest order of things, especially if they are "living" (as people call them), though all things truly have *life* and *soul*, as we noted earlier—the "hot" bodies require fiery nourishment and active exercise. "Moist" bodies, such as wind and water, require flowing food and exercise. Cold and dry things prefer earthly sustenance and rest as their form of exercise, which is suited to them and preserves their state and existence.

But if all solitary and simple things are within the scope of this *law*, then *Heaven* itself may not be free or exempt from it;

For although the *Water* is so distant and unlike them, their power and strength are such that they are able, through their labor, to easily refine it—turning it first into *Air* and then into *Ether* <term>Ether: believed to be the pure, fiery substance of the higher heavens</term>, a similar but lighter substance which is their proper food.

That this is true is made plain to us here below by hungry *Souls* (which are but offshoots <term>original: "Imps"; here meaning a graft or cutting</term> detached from the *Heavenly Body*). Indeed, if you observe closely, if this moisture is taken away, everything falls to the *Ground*. Then there is a great turmoil <term>original: "coyl"; meaning a disturbance or struggle</term> and fighting here below for *food* and *activity*—that is, for *life* and *existence*—which is the cause of all *action*, *labor*, *rest*, *change*, and of all things. Every being is naturally drawn to things like itself; and if its strength is even slightly greater, it subdues, digests, and turns that thing into its own *Nature*, gaining strength from it. But if it lacks its similar food nearby and is much stronger, it dares to encounter and is able to overcome <note>original: "quell"</note> unlike things as well—as I said of the *Stars*, the mightiest things in the *World* (which give might to all other things). # The Way to Bliss. Book II.

[for one] consumes the other, but both are marred, dulled, and weakened, which they call *Harmony* <note>original: "Consent"</note>, *Temperance*, and *Mixture*. For example, *Fire*—which is extremely hot and somewhat dry—and *Water*—which is very cold and somewhat wet—when meeting together in equal degrees of power and strength, are both impaired, but neither is completely lost or destroyed. But if this *Water* happens to be acted upon by the heat of the *Heavens* and turned into an airy and oily <note>original: "fat"</note> substance, then—even though there are now two types of moisture set against the dryness of the *Fire*—because of the abundant heat of the weather and the heavens, it has now become partly like the *Fire*. It becomes friendly, or at least a weaker foe and enemy, yielding itself as food to the fire and increasing its strength and nature. But if, on the other hand, *Air*—with its exceeding moisture matching the dryness of the *Fire*—gains some strength and watery coldness (as is seen in thick and foggy weather), it is easily able to overcome the *Fire* and consume <note>original: "eat him up"</note> it.

(p) Averroes, as cited in Joachim Cureus, *On Senses and Sensible Objects*, book 2, chapter 19, page 168 <note>original: "Aver. apud Joach. Curæum l.2. de sens. & sensib. c. 19. in p. 168."</note>.

Now, regarding a mixed body (which is a (p) harmonization <note>original: "consent"</note> and a dulling of the four primary famous *Enemies* <term>Enemies: the four elements—earth, air, fire, water—which were thought to be in constant opposition within the body</term>, made and kept in tune and submission by the force and skill of a heavenly and natural *Heat* acting upon them), the same logic applies. For when this *Heat* departs—either for lack of nourishment <note>original: "Meat"</note> or driven out by force—these "friends" begin to stir and fight for food and freedom until one element stands out above the rest and recovers some part of its former power. This causes pain to those who can feel and breeds diseases; at last, that element gains total lordship and rule over all, and turns the others into its own *Nature*. Then the old harmony <note>original: "consent"</note>, the "knot" and the body itself, is broken, lost, and ruined. A new state is created and acquired, which continues to decline until everything returns to *Earth*, from where they all originated.

# Chapter 1. The Way to Bliss.

<margin>
(q) Joachim Cureus, *On Senses and Sensible Objects*, book 2, chapter 24, page 175 <note>original: "Joach. cur. de sens. & sen-sib. l.2. c.24. in p. 175."</note>.

(r) Ocellus Lucanus, chapter 1, page 19 <note>original: "Ocell. Luc. c.1. in p. 19."</note>.

For example, and to look close to home: regarding the fiery frame of **Man's** body, (q) when the soul, for want of nourishment, fails and departs, the elements immediately retire and return in their proper order. First, **Fire** becomes moist and lukewarm, swallowed up by **Air**; and this, soon after, becomes thick and cold—that is, **Watery**. Then the **Water** becomes muddy, growing more and more thick and dry, until at last it becomes moist, dry, and heavy, and everything is devoured and returned to (r) **Earth**, from which they all originated.

This is the natural dissolution and death of our bodies. Forcible death and destruction happen through diseases (setting aside other forms of violence, which no man can guarantee against). This occurs when either **breath** or **food** <note>original: "meat"</note>, imbalanced in some quality, feeds and nourishes something like itself that begins to rise above the rest. This makes that element strong and able to vanquish the others, introducing **discord** <note>original: "Jarre"</note> into that **musical harmony** <note>original: "Musical Consent"</note> mentioned before. For instance, when by watery **food** and **air**, all the elements <note>original: "beginnings"</note> are changed into **Water**; or the **Hot** and **Dry** are changed into a fiery temperament, and so forth. Alternatively, this happens when the body lacks the **exercise** that is owed to it, which is **rapid movement** <note>original: "quick Motion"</note> intended to preserve the **Air** and **Fire** in the fine frame and balance of **Man** from the sloth and idleness of the slow and rusty **elements** <note>original: "Beginnings"</note>.

With these foundations laid, we see that the way to maintain the balance of our body is plain and easy. It is nothing more than to continually feed and cherish it with clean and temperate **air** and **food**, so that all the elements <note>original: "Beginnings"</note> are served and fed alike. In this way, one element may not be more dominant <note>original: "proud"</note>, strong, or able than another to subdue the rest and overthrow the body's state. It is for this reason that **poison** kills; it is because it is extremely cold and dry (for we may exclude all rotten, as well as fiery and watery temperaments, from the...)

...natural heat of the begetting breath and womb, forty days before it is fully framed and fashioned into the form and shape of a *man*, ready to draw food and nourishment (be it milk or menstrual blood <note>original: "menstrue"</note>, received by mouth or navel—I cannot pause to argue the point) from the *mother*, to the increase of the tough or more solid <note>original: "founder"</note> parts. But the first *moisture* <note>The "radical moisture" was believed to be a finite, oily substance present from birth that acted as the fuel for the body's "natural heat."</note> is now at its full growth and perfection; from that point on, it feeds *life* while being unfed itself, and it wastes away daily—contrary to the basic principles and rules of *medicine* <note>original: "physick"</note>. For the *child* has now received all that the *workman* <note>A metaphorical reference to the creative force or nature that shapes the fetus.</note> can provide, and for the rest—which is his nourishment—he is handed over to his *mother's* provision. But what does she have to give to the food of *life*? Nothing, as I showed; otherwise, we might live forever.

<margin>]</margin>
Then we see what the first *moisture* is, and how it excels the food of the body, and why it cannot be maintained by it. This is because it is the most fine and airy part (for the other parts each go their own way to form the organs from which they originated) of all the *seed* mingled, wrought, purged, raised, and refined, and then closely thickened and driven together—forty times more concentrated than our *food* <note>original: "meat"</note>, which is processed and ready to be discharged in a single day. Therefore, food is entirely unfit to increase or bond with our first *moisture*, the food of *life*, being just as unsuitable as *water* is to *oil* and *fatness*.

And by this, to come to the point, we have a plain pattern (if we are wise and careful) and a way to perform the great mystery of the *extension of life* <note>original: "adjournment of life"</note>. For if it is as I proved above—that all the moisture of the matter lies in the maintenance of our *natural heat*—and if that heat (as our *experts* <note>original: "men"</note> and all *reason* teach) follows the steps of...

Common *fire* waxes and wanes, becoming intense or faint according to the supply of its *food* (fuel) and first *moisture*. Therefore, if we can create an *oil* as refined and dense as this—indeed, identical to it in every way—it will easily combine and join with our own first *moisture*. It will then feed, nourish, and increase it, and increase *life* as well. This is based on the same sound and simple *reasoning* that adding the same *oil* to a *fire* increases both the fuel and the *flame* together. 

Even if our own natural *fire* should not only lose its strength for lack of *food* <note>original: "meat"</note> and diminish in force, but also shrink in size (as some *physicians* believe), no great harm would be done. For this second spark or offshoot <note>original: "slip"</note> of the great and common *fire* of *nature*—being a portion of the finer part of the whole (which is the same in all things) and similar to its *like* within us—when released and made accessible in this refined and *ethereal medicine*, would restore the *total volume* <note>original: "Heap"</note> and fix the *matter*.

But how shall we obtain such a refined *oil* and first *moisture*? The *matter* has been pushed so far that this is where the difficulty lies. I showed you the *pattern*: just as *nature* obtained it before you, using the same *stuff* and *seed*, and the same *heat* and moving *workman* <note>A metaphorical reference to the creative or transformative force within nature.</note>. According to the certain proof of all our *experts* <note>original: "Men," likely referring to alchemists or natural philosophers.</note>, this heat is easy to find—any gentle, continuous, even, and moist heat; that is, any rotting *heat* <note>original: "rotting Heat," referring to *putrefaction*, a key stage in alchemy where organic matter is broken down to be transformed.</note>. 

But the *seed* seems difficult and impossible to match because it was fashioned by a strange and hidden proportion and temperament of our *body*. No *man*, through imagination or knowledge—much less by hand or craftsmanship—can reach or counterfeit it, even if he boiled every possible *mixture* in every *heat* that all the *wits* in the world could devise.

## CHAP. II.
# Of HEALTH.

After a *man* has fulfilled his desire to *live*, he begins to wish for *health*, without which no life is sweet or pleasant. Therefore, let us turn our attention that way next and endeavor to show the means (besides the Way of Hermes) by which every *man* may gain and keep his *health*. That is, as I partly told you before, the harmony and equal (I mean, agreeable to *nature*) <note>original: "Kinde"</note> balance and moderating of the four first *elements* <note>original: "Beginnings"; the author refers to the four classical elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note>, which are the material of our bodies. For if this knot is broken and they are released toward their former liberty, they grow proud and strong and fight each other, as is their nature; they put us in pain and hinder <note>original: "Lett"</note> the rule of *Nature*, which they call *disease*.

To address these things one at a time, as we did before and will continue to do: keeping our *Health* and *Body* in balance <note>original: "temper"</note> does not seem to me to be the impossible task that the world makes it out to be. I say this because I know that all the *ways* and *entries* by which *diseases* enter and disrupt the body's balance <note>original: "distemper the same"</note> can be blocked and guarded with just a little care.

We must necessarily draw *breath* and eat *food* <note>original: "Meat"</note> for the reasons mentioned before. Since these are not entirely pure or suitable, *Nature* has her *waste products* <note>original: "Leavings"</note>. Furthermore, *labor* and *rest* are necessary, and perhaps we cannot help but be moved in our *minds* by *joy*, *grief*, *fear*, *hope*, and similar *passions*, even though the *Stoics* <note>A school of ancient Greek philosophy that taught the development of self-control and fortitude as a means of overcoming destructive emotions.</note> deny that such reactions are inevitable.

<margin>(o) Pliny's *Natural History*, Book 7, Chapter 50.</margin>
Through so many *ways* and *gates*, *diseases* may enter if they are not well watched and guarded. This monitoring can be done reasonably, and it has been done often, as we are assured by those who have lived long lives without any *diseases* or *sickness*. For instance, (o) *Pliny* <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, a Roman author and naturalist.</note> writes of a *musician* named *Xenophilus* who lived in health for one hundred and five years. Many similar *stories* can be found if we took the time to look for them. Some people are satisfied regarding everything except *air* and *food* <note>original: "Meat"</note>, for they say these often contain the hidden seeds of *diseases* that cannot be foreseen or prevented. Just as we find that those *foods* which make the finest appearance (such as *wine*, *sugar*, and similar enticing *baits*) have the most harmful dross and dregs hidden at the bottom, so too the *air*—even when it seems best and clearest <note>original: "lightest"</note>—is sometimes infected and poisoned with a venomous *breath*. This vapor is thrust into the air either from the earth below or from the *stars* of *heaven*. Because the cause is hidden and unknown to us, the resulting harm is impossible to ward off or prevent.
