If there is not a sufficient supply of other foul *food* <note>original: "Meat"</note> at hand, they act like a poisonous and purging medicine; they tend to draw out good *fluids* <note>original: "Juyces"</note> and make food of them.

<margin>(t) *Aristotle, On the Parts of Animals, Book 3, Chapter 8*</margin>
<margin>(u) *Aristotle, On the Parts of Animals, Book 4, Chapter 2*</margin>
Therefore *Aristotle*, that clever observer of *Nature*, as if he were an expert in this matter, showed the necessity and use of the major internal organs and bowels of *living creatures* <note>original: "Wights"</note>. He says very truly and wisely, (t) *The heart and liver, being the source of life and nourishment, are necessary for all living creatures*. He adds that for hotter animals, the *brain* serves to cool them and the *lungs* <note>original: "Lights"</note> to cleanse the heat. He stops there, as if he thought the other three organs were useless. Indeed, regarding one of them, (u) in the same book, I believe, while telling the stories of the *deer* <note>original: "Hart"</note> and the *camel*, and giving the reason why they are both so swift, healthy, long-lived, and endowed <note>original: "enfeoffed"</note> with other such good qualities above the rest, he declares in plain terms that the lack of the fiery and scummy *gallbladder* <note>original: "Gall"</note> is a great benefit to them.

<margin>(w) *The Discourse of Mattioli on the 3rd Book of Dioscorides, chapter 145; and Alessandro Alessandri’s "Genial Days," book 5, chapter 25*</margin>
As for the *spleen* <note>original: "Milt"</note>, that muddy organ, the fact that it may be left out as unnecessary in the bodies of the better *living creatures* is shown by (w) the *meadows of Crete* <note>original: "Candy"</note>, near to *Gortyna* <note>original: "Cartina"</note>. There, by a strange and hidden power, those meadows deprive the beasts who graze upon them of their spleens. Indeed, experience shows that the *spleen* is not only useless, but harmful as well...

<header>Chapter II. The Way to Bliss.</header>

Nature has taught this even in ourselves. For example, consider the Turkish light footmen (I do not know where the example came from, unless it was the camel, which can travel a hundred miles a day and go fifteen days without drink). These men, having had their spleen <note>original: "Milt"</note> removed <note>original: "gelt"</note> in their childhood, prove to be the swiftest, healthiest, and most capable of fasting of any footmen in the world.

<margin>(x) *Book 2, On the powers of the limbs, chapters 7, 8, 9, etc., Volume 3.* <note>original: "Lib. 2. de vir. membror. c. 7, 8, 9, & c. Tom. 3."</note></margin>
As for the kidneys <note>original: "Reins"</note>, those drawers of urine: just as creatures <note>original: "Wights"</note> that do not drink have none at all, so some men have only one, as if Nature did not care to make any. If we could refrain from drinking (as those beasts do by nature, and some men do by habit), we could better do without them and avoid many harmful things <note>original: "Mischiefs"</note> in our bodies. Therefore, that unique man (x) *Paracelsus* <note>A famous Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541) who challenged traditional medical authorities.</note>—guided, I believe, by some divine light from above—not only sees these faults but also finds ways to correct them. He aims to remove the harm of these three troublesome <note>original: "noisom"</note> parts <note>The spleen, gallbladder, and kidneys.</note>, not through surgery <note>original: "gelding Craft"</note>, but through his *divine* method of *healing*.

So, to avoid all diseases that arise from waste products <note>original: "Leavings"</note>, my advice is to eliminate the influence and power of those idle organs <note>original: "Bowels"</note>—either with a knife in childhood or, preferably, with this man's transformative <note>original: "gelding"</note> medicines (you know where to find them; I do not need to show you). Or perhaps even that would be unnecessary. In a lineage <note>original: "stock"</note> that follows our clean *diet*, Nature herself—just as she does in those meadows—would completely erase and eliminate them within a few generations.

But I will go further. Hear a new and unheard-of opinion, yet do not let your judgment race ahead before you see a solid foundation of *reason*. What if we could fast forever and live without any food? Might not all the harm and danger of food <note>original: "Meat"</note> then be prevented <note>original: "fore-stalled"</note>? If other creatures <note>original: "Wights"</note>,

...our **Health** than we do, because the cleaner part alone shall be received; and moreover, as he says, for the clean removal of that ordinary trouble and annoyance of ours, which your reverence will not permit me to name (although I might among **physicians**), but they know my meaning.

<margin>(g) Paracelsus, *Archidoxis*, Book 1, Volume 6, p. 5; and Fortunius Licetus, *On Those Who Lived a Long Time Without Food*, Book 1 <note>original Latin: "de his qui diu sine alimento vixerunt"</note>; and *The Admirable and Memorable Histories of Our Time* by Simon Goulart, translated into English by Edward Grimeston, pp. 352, 353, and 419, and following; and Carolus Bovillus's letter to Nicolaus Gambusia.</margin>

What need I say more? If you are both so hard of belief and dull of sight that neither the reports of reliable **authors** will sink in, nor can you see the light of **reason** shining before you, then take these few examples of ordinary matters in the life and habits of **men**, and compare them one with another.

Is it not as common a practice, and indeed as necessary, to spit, to eliminate another nameless **waste** <note>original: "leaving"; referring to excrement</note>, and to drink—but especially to sleep? If some of these—nay, all of them—may be spared, why not our **food** as well? Let us look a little at examples, because **reasoning** is both too lengthy and too open to petty objections.

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>The Way to Bliss. Book II.</header>

By the means mentioned before (of which there are more than one, so if people do not like this one, they may choose another), it is possible for all **people**, through nature and habit, to maintain their health forever. Let us now move to the next point: that health can just as easily be recovered if it is lost, and that all **diseases** may be cured. This is a point much harder to prove than the first, for it is so beset and blocked with all kinds of obstacles <note>original: "Letts"</note> and difficulties that a **person** can hardly tell which way to take a step forward.

First, there appear **Aesculapius**, **Hippocrates**, and **Plato**—the greatest of the Greeks—presenting various **diseases** of both types (those inherited and those acquired) <note>original: "both came by descent, and gotten by purchase"</note> as hopeless and beyond recovery. They abandon the **people** who suffer from them as being a burden both to themselves and to the state. Then you may see **Galen** and his refined company, followed by a long train of suppliers <note>original: "Caters"</note> and cooks loaded with all kinds of elegant drugs, standing forth and crying out:

<margin>(o) Francis Bacon, Lord St. Alban, *On the Advancement of Learning*, Book 2, page 200. <note>original: "Fr. S. Alb. de augm nt. Scient."</note></margin>

(o) *"For many ages, they have devoured heaps of books and taken endless pains searching out the properties of individual medicines and creating mixtures of them; yet they could hardly cure some fevers <note>original: "Agues"</note> and other minor diseases. But as for the four major conditions—namely, gout, leprosy, dropsy, and epilepsy <note>original: "Falling-sickness"</note>—they could never heal them, and have declared them incurable as if it were a divine decree <note>original: "for Oracles set them down incurable"</note>."*

What is the best thing to do in this matter? What shall we set against the weight of the authority of so many great men? Indeed <note>original: "Marry"</note>, let us put them in the balance, as we have done so far, and weigh them against truth and reason.

<margin>(p) Cicero, *Academic Questions*, Book 1, near the end; and Fernel, *On the Hidden Causes of Things*, Book 1, Chapter 7. <note>original: "Cic. Acad. quest. l. 1. sub fine. & Fernel. de abd. rerum caus. l. 1. c. 7."</note></margin>

"But where shall we find the truth?" they ask. Just as it is everywhere (as (p) **Democritus** said) "drowned in the deep," so in this matter, it is scattered all around and widely spread as well.

# Chapter II. The Way to Bliss.

...as well; for there are three things, and every one full of sub-branches, belonging to this ***Art*** and way of ***Healing***: The first is knowledge of the ***Diseases***; the second is the ***Remedies*** against them; and the third is the application of ***Remedies***. All of these should be examined in this ***Discourse***. But I hope it shall not be necessary—indeed, we must be careful how we enter into so large and long a ***journey*** <note>original: "Race"</note> in such a short and narrow amount of time. This is especially true since this path was never traveled before by any of our worthy ***Ancestors***, the wise ***Egyptians***, whose steps we strive to follow. For when they have once hit the mark they aim for, and obtained the ***Great*** and ***General*** MEDICINE <note>The "Universal Medicine" or "Elixir of Life," a legendary alchemical substance capable of curing all diseases.</note> which cures all ***Diseases*** with ease, they think it is enough. They consider it a pointless and unnecessary task (as it truly is) to trouble themselves and their children with extensive ***rules*** about the innumerable signs and causes of ***infinite diseases***, and about other such small details in their application.

Neither would I have you bring up ***Paracelsus*** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> and his followers against me, saying they have taken great and impressive efforts in this field; if you do, you will force me to speak my ***mind*** <note>original: "Fantasie"</note>. Although this ***man***—leaving aside his ***students***, who are still too young—possessed a great light of ***intelligence*** <note>original: "Wit"</note> and spent a long time searching <note>original: "proling"</note> with his eyes, ears, and hands into the ***Mysteries of Egypt***, where he saw and performed many of their ***deep secrets*** (and even found some of his own worthy of praise, though I think many are made up), yet his ***new art*** and ***rules of healing*** are not good in my opinion. He created them first against the example of his ***Ancestors***, from whom he had received everything, and then in spite and defiance of ***Galen*** <note>Aelius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note>, because Galen had insulted his ***countrymen***, as you have heard; but—

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

...primarily driven by a mad and raging desire for **Fame and Honor**; he took it upon himself (a **man** unfit for the task) to tear down and erase the old **system of medicine** <note>original: "Work of Physick"</note> and to establish that strange and famous **new one**. Then, see how it is performed: He sets down some false **rules**, some that are useless and pointless, and some that are lacking entirely—and all of them are inconsistent, disordered, and ignorant! In those instances where he does well (as he does sometimes), he does no more than what was done before him; he simply brings in the same things disguised with new, odd, contradictory, and unheard-of **names**. These might inspire **wonder** at first, but when they are examined closely, 
<margin>(q) *On the Ends of Good and Evil*, Book 4, near the end <note>original: "De finibus bonorum et malorum"</note>.</margin>
they provoke laughter, (q) as **Cicero** <note>original: "Tully"</note> says of the **Stoics'** similar device in **philosophy**.

And I could easily prove that I do not slander them—for this is not my **purpose** <note>original: "no Cause"</note>—if this space would allow for such a **massive book**. Therefore, let us follow the true and right **Egyptians** and leave **Paracelsus** to this bad **subject** (or a trivial one, even if it were good). Instead, let us spend all our care and thought on what truly matters: good **medicines** and **remedies** against **diseases**. With these, **midwives** <note>original: "old Wives"</note> in the **country** and simple **men** on our side (I mean simple in respect to the **Greek intellectual hair-splitting** <note>original: "Gracian Subtilties"</note> about **nothing**) have healed most—nay, even all—**diseases**. 

And with these remedies, indeed, the **German** (let us give him his due praise) has utterly destroyed **Greek medicine**. In doing so, he has done much for **mankind** by detecting and getting rid of our hidden and secret **enemy**. This enemy, under the guise of friendship and claiming to fight against our **enemies**, has for a long time betrayed us and done us much mischief. One of their best **captains** and **pillars** of their **state**, a man named **Fernel**

### Chapter II. The Way to Bliss.

...efforts (which we know were great) devoted to that kind of **healing**, saying it consists of more than just **words**. He says the entire weight and force of **this art** rests upon the **knowledge** of the virtues of **medicines** that are secretly hidden and tucked away within the **essential oils of substances** <note>original: "Oyl of Bodies"; in alchemy, this refers to the concentrated essence or "soul" of a material</note>. These are to be extracted and obtained through the **skillful methods** of **alchemy**—that very art, I say, which is so frequently condemned by his colleagues and peers. The best of his **colleagues**, both before and since his time, have fled to this harbor as well. They flee daily from the toil and trouble of their fruitless and barren "Dead Sea." Therefore, let us shift our sails and fly just as far—and even further, I hope—provided the tide and wind (which we have at our command) do not fail us.

But first, let us describe that haven of **medicine**. Let us see what landmarks it has and how it differs from other nearby inlets, lest we lose our way at our journey's end with great shame and grief, and suffer a shipwreck.

<margin>(s) Jean Fernel, *On the Hidden Causes of Things*, Book 2, Chapter 19, page 180 <note>original: "Fernel. de abdit. rer. caus. l.2. c.19. in p. 180."</note></margin>
<margin>and Fortunio Liceti, *On those who lived long without food*, Book 3, Chapter 42 <note>original: "& Fort. Licet. de his qui diu sine aliment. vixe-runt, l.3.c.42."</note></margin>
A **medicine** is that which destroys the power of whatever harms us. It does this in many ways, yet all lead to a single goal: its own food and sustenance (which, as I said before, is the ultimate goal of all action and work). Now, let us organize our discussion once more. A **medicine** heals us and kills our enemy either by dulling it or consuming it. When it meets an opposing force of equal strength (as when **(s) oil** and **poison**, etc., are joined), they do not consume or destroy each other in the fight. Instead, both are dulled and weakened, creating a lifeless mass which **nature** casts out as an alien and unnatural dead thing, which they call a **residue** <note>original: "Leaving (or Excrement)"</note>. But if the medicine has more strength and power than our enemy, then it completely destroys, devours, and transforms it into its own nature. And this **consumer** is either like...

...the thing that hurts us. In this manner—just as every **herb** of various qualities draws and feeds upon its own juice in a **garden**—so one (**t**) **poison** can cure another, and all purging and extracting substances heal us. All of (**u**) **Fernel’s** hidden and divine properties actually operate by plain reason. 

Alternatively, healing can occur through things that are dissimilar and contrary. In this way—just as dry **sticks**, **hemp fiber** <note>original: "towe"; the coarse fibers of flax or hemp used for lighting fires</note>, and **vinegar** can quench **wildfires** or other greasy **fires** better than **water** (whose own fatness feeds the flame)—the stronger, opposing quality suppresses and consumes the weaker. This is how any cold and dry substance, such as (**w**) **Armenian bole** <note>original: "Bole Armin"; a medicinal red clay from Armenia used as an astringent</note>, **Lemnian earth** <note>original: "Terra Lemnia"; a medicinal clay from the island of Lemnos</note>, and others, cures a corrupting **poison**. 

A great number of **cures** are performed this way. It is the only path that **Greek medicine** <note>original: "Gracian Physick"; referring to the Galenic system of medicine based on balancing the four humors—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile</note> claims to follow in theory, though it does not truly do so in practice. We have just heard of two other ways of **healing** which those doctors and other people used without realizing it. **Paracelsus** <note>original: "Paracelse"; the famous 16th-century physician and alchemist who famously challenged Galenic medicine</note> probably came up with the name for it recently, but he wanders as far off the path as the Greeks do, just in the opposite direction, because he believes all **cures** are performed by that method alone.

Now, once these consuming **medicines** have finished their work, **Nature** expels them as if they were **poison** or dissimilar foreign substances. This is according to the **Greek rules**, because all their **medicines** were—by their own admission—of that nature. But if they had either considered the calming **sustainer** <note>original: "dulling Nourisher"; likely referring to a substance that stabilizes the body's constitution</note>—which, as I told you, takes on the quality of a **leaven** <note>original: "Leaving"; in this context, a ferment or substance that transforms what it touches</note>—or if they had known the wholesome **medicines** used by our own people, they would have reached a very different conclusion. But let us leave them be and set out in time toward the **Harbor of Health**.

If the **Art of Healing** is nothing more than matching harmful things with their more powerful **enemies** (though sometimes a balance will suffice) or matching **likes** together, and the world is full of both kinds of **creatures**,

This leaves us to ignore our precarious <note>original: "tickle"</note> standing, being daily and hourly so beset with *destinies* that a man can guarantee nothing.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;By these examples, finally, the wise and diligent <term>painful</term> men of *Greece*—as they themselves report, whether they were *Apollo*, or his son <note>Asclepius, the god of medicine</note>, or whoever—and by combining *reason* with further proof, first established the *art* and *rules of healing*. They sought to understand where *diseases* come from and how to *remove* them. Then, searching everywhere for *remedies* for every occasion, they gradually matched most of the minor ailments with single *medicines*. For the more serious ones, they doubled and combined many of them together. This continued until finally (which was in the time of *Hippocrates*), they were able to heal everything except for four of the greatest and deepest *diseases*: the <margin>(s) Peder Sørensen the Dane, *The Idea of Philosophical Medicine*, chapter 2, page 15. <note>original: "P. They have followed this path ever since, both in *Greece* and throughout the *world*. This was the extent of their achievement—attained with great effort—and they could do no more, by common consent leaving the rest as "incurable."

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But to come to the point: those who know better easily see and laugh at what an injustice this was to both *skill* and *nature*. Wherefore, to complete the *Art of Healing* and to make it capable of helping and curing all *diseases*, there arrived (or rather, preceded them) the *Egyptians* (t). <margin>(t) Homer, *Odyssey*, book 4, verse 229, etc.</margin> They were *men* in great favor with *Nature*, both because of their land and their upbringing, and were notably praised above all other *nations*. Soon after—through what was a divine *light* and insight—they perceived that the immense labor of seeking such an infinite variety of *simples* <note>original: "Singles"—medicines made from a single herb or plant.</note> and *mixtures* was vain, empty, and <margin>(u) See Purchas, *Pilgrims*, volume 2, book 10, chapter 1, section 1623, regarding those trees which are poisonous on one side (the West) and an antidote on the other.</margin> pitiable in the eyes of *wise men*. ## Chapter II: The Way to Bliss

<margin>
(z) Girolamo Cardano, *On Subtlety*, book 5, page 186; Seneca, *Natural Questions*, book 3, chapter 10; and Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, book 4, chapter 5.
</margin>
...without any further doubt, toil, or labor. They believed this especially (z) because there is no single thing in the world, whatever you choose, that does not possess all the virtues of *heaven* and its qualities within itself. There is nothing that is not as good as everything else, and which may not serve in place of all other things; indeed, every thing is able to cure all *diseases*. When this fact was weighed and fully understood through the use of *wit* and *reason*, they began to practice. Through a similar sharpness of *wit*, they quickly found the natural and ready way to prepare and make fit those three kinds of *medicines* <note>In alchemy, these often refer to the three kingdoms: vegetable, animal, and mineral, or the three degrees of medicinal potency.</note> mentioned before, which contain the entire *art of healing*. All other methods are but wasted words and grievous toil, tiring out a world of thinkers over a useless matter.

In particular, they relied on that last type of medicine, which is sufficient on its own. They were careful to choose one of the best—or rather, the very best of all—for ease of preparation. However, *Paracelsus* <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> was not content with this in recent times. Instead, he hurried through the rest of the methods, partly to spite his enemies (as I said) and partly to make himself famous. In doing so, he went against the rules of *wisdom* and *virtue* and the example of all his *ancestors*.

But how does every thing possess all the virtues of *heaven* and *earth*—that is, all the curing and *healing* power of everything in the world? Very well; you must remember that I proved above that all the virtue and power of *heaven* poured down upon these lower creatures is nothing else but *one and the same life, soul, and heavenly heat in all things*. Furthermore, I proved that *all diseases flow from distemper <term>distemper: an imbalance of the bodily humors or a disruption of the body's natural harmony</term>, and from a discord in the body's natural harmony*. Therefore, it follows that any thing which is endowed with an abundance of *life* and with an exact and perfect balance, seated within a body that is both subtle and strong,

It can do as much as all the powers and forces of all the *Plants*, *Living Creatures* <note>original: "Wights"</note>, and *Minerals* in the *World*; that is, to put to flight all the trouble of *Diseases* and restore the *Body* to perfect health and quietness.

(a) *Aristotle, History of Animals, book 9, chapter 48; Pliny, book 9, chapter 8; Cicero, On the Nature of the Gods, book 2; and Oppian, Halieutica, book 2, verse 533.*

But how is all this done? We are bundling too many great matters together; it would be better to mark them out more distinctly. When this hot (due to the *Heat* we spoke of) and strong-tempered *Medicine* enters the *Stomach*, it requires no long digestion, being already thoroughly digested. Nor does it wait for any ordinary passages to be opened to it. Instead, as soon as it is awakened from sleep by its *Fellow*, the *Natural Heat*, it immediately flies out and rushes about as fast as the (a) *Dolphin* chasing its prey, or as *Nature* herself, whom *Hippocrates* says pierces all boundaries for that purpose—that is, to seek its own food and sustenance to preserve its *State* and *Being*, which is the goal of all things in the *World*, as was said above.

Now, there is nothing in the *World* so similar to a perfect temperature as the *Ethereal First Moisture* <note>Also known as "radical moisture," this was believed to be the essential fluid or "lamp oil" of life that sustained the body's heat.</note> of *Man*. This is found best and most abundantly in the *Heart*, the *Root of Life*. Therefore, the medicine hastens there and feeds upon that part first. That is the *Reason* why it immediately restores a *Man* who is half dead, and, as it were, pulls him out of the *Throat of Death*. Then it runs to the rest of the body, increasing by that means the *Natural Heat* and *First Moisture* of every part of the *Body*. When this is done, it turns back upon the parts themselves, and by encountering with

If we <margin>(b) Michael Sendivogius, *Treatise on Sulphur*, part 2, page 68.</margin> use it without moderation (b), it elevates us too high and too near its own Temperature, making us unfit for the deeds and duties of an earthly life.

But in the meantime, and in the midst of this work, we must know that by its exceeding *Heat* and *subtlety* <note>original: "subtileness"; referring to the refined, penetrating quality of the substance</note>—which is achieved by its lofty craftsmanship and which constitutes its *Strength* above all things—it divides and scatters, like smoke before the wind, all imbalanced and hurtful things. If they cannot be reconciled and turned to goodness, *Nature* throws them out as dead and unfruitful *Leavings* <note>original: "Leavings"; waste products or dross</note>.

But why do we talk so much of an exact and perfect balance <note>original: "Temper"</note> when, by the verdict of all who investigate these cases, no such thing is found existing in *Nature* except in *Heaven*? Yet you did not hear me say that it floated aloft; rather, it was sunk to the bottom of all *Nature*, though by skill it may be found and weighed up. For just as *Heaven* was once a gross and imbalanced *Lump*, refined by the Divine *Art* of GOD and separated into that place and nature where it now stands; even so, one of our gross bodies here below—being a piece of that same *Lump* and identical to what *Heaven* once was—may by similar *Art* and *Skill* <note>original: "Cunning"</note> be refined and parted from all its imbalanced, gross, and foul dross, and brought to a *heavenly*

And yet you must not understand me as though I would have the *Mind* and *Intellect* <note>original: "Wit"</note> of *Man*, which is but a spark of the Divine and great *Mind*, to be able to reach the excellency of His *Work*, and to match so great perfection. Then this *Heaven (c)*—nay, this *Sun* of ours—is nothing else but an *Oil* full of heavenly *Spirits*. But who is he, they ask, that can see the *Divine Art* and *Way* whereby GOD made His great and mighty *Work*? Or if he saw it, who could learn and match it by *Imitation*? None but he whom GOD has enlightened and whose *Eyes* He has unsealed. Then shall he easily spy the *Way* lying open in all places and in all natural <note>original: "kindly"</note> changes. He shall see them pass and travel, I say, always by that course which HERMES <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> calls soft and ingenious <note>original: "witty"</note>—that is, a natural *separation*. And if he is not swift and rash, but will have sober *Patience*, his own skill and labor shall be but little, and *Nature* herself very kindly will, in her due time, perform all—even all that heavenly *Workmanship*. And yet I do not mean that *Art* is unnecessary; *Art* must accompany and attend upon *Nature* (though without great pains and skill) both forward and backward in this journey—some know my meaning—until she comes to her wished...

If you do not perceive this, call to mind and consider the way by which we made our *Ether* <note>original: *Æther*; in alchemy, the purest element of the heavens</note> in the former chapter, and matched our first *Moisture*—a thing ethereal, I say, and almost temperate. Then you will see the way to cure all diseases by the third way of *Egyptian Healing*, which they do (and we may well) call the *Egyptian Heaven* (d). And yet, it is a way far beneath the medicine of Hermes <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note>, as we shall hear hereafter.

But if they will not yield yet to reason, but mutter and babble out—still in a rustic fashion—that this heavenly medicine of ours is too high for the reach of men's simple wits here scattered below upon the ground for other lesser and baser uses; and that no man since the first man <note>Adam</note> has ever yet been known to have found and crafted the same: I will not stand to beat reason into such hard heads. Instead, I will go to the other two lower and weaker ways of healing which the Egyptians also found, but did not use. They called the first *Mineral Medicines*, and the next *Mysteries* and *Secrets*, as may appear by the works of Paracelsus <note>original: *Paracelse*; a famous Swiss physician and alchemist</note>. 

We may fitly call this *Second Kind*—because that other name is too broad—a "Cure-itself" (if it be lawful for us, as well as for all learned men, to create a new word where a fit one is lacking). I say we do well to call it a *Cure-itself* because it is that way of healing whereby every self-same thing, when further broken down, cures itself. In this process, the inward and hidden thing cures the outward and apparent thing, following that course of nature whereby the stronger version of a substance consumes the weaker version during a trial.

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

If this permission is once granted, we will borrow a little more for the other two as well, because their *names* do not yet quite fit our purpose. We shall call that *Heaven* a *Cure-all* <note>original: "Panacea"</note>, because it performs that function; and the next we shall call a *Cure-the-great*, because the practice of the *Paracelsians* <note>Followers of Paracelsus, a Swiss physician who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> is always to match the greater and more stubborn types of *diseases* with stout and mighty *minerals*, and the rest with those hidden *Cure-themselves* <note>original: "Autogeneia"</note>. Or at least, in the lower rank of lighter *diseases*, they match them only with their *likes* in a raw state—as the *Greeks* <note>Refers to Galenic medicine, which relied on herbal "simples" and the balancing of humors.</note> use them—without any sophisticated preparation.

Let us move forward again toward the *subject matter*. Because the *Greeks* themselves are able—if things go well—to cure the lighter *sort* of ailments and to heal all but the four *persistent diseases* <note>original: "Stagers"; likely referring to the four traditionally "incurable" diseases: dropsy, gout, leprosy, and falling sickness (epilepsy).</note> mentioned before, we will leave the rest to them. We will let this second kind of healing go, called our hidden *Cure-themselves*, and direct all our efforts against these four which the Greeks could never shake. We shall see how, by the force of *mineral medicines*, these diseases may be overcome, beaten down, and quite erased from *existence*.

<margin>(e) The Sea-Hare <note>original: "Lepus Marinus"</note>, Pliny, Book 9, Chapter 48; and Mattioli's discourse on Book 2 of Dioscorides, Chapter 18.</margin> We see the poisoned spirits and breaths of *venomous* things, and with what force and might they attack our *bodies*—things in nature and quality set against them—and how they spoil, waste, and consume them. To consider substances that enter the body: that costly poison in (g) Nubia <note>A region in northeast Africa</note> kills a man instantly in a dose of only one grain <note>term: grain; a traditional unit of weight, approximately 65 milligrams</note> in weight. Otherwise, surely such heaps of poison could not remain within us for long without extinguishing life in a moment.

<margin>(h) Pliny, *Natural History*, Book 27, Chapter 2.</margin>

By this means, they pierce swiftly everywhere and overcome through those contrary qualities. Therefore, let us take the strongest minerals—those called "Middle Minerals" by our experts, or "hard juices" <note>original: "hard Juyces" or *succi rigidi*, a term used by Georgius Agricola to describe solidified mineral substances</note> by Georgius Agricola (leaving the metals aside for a better purpose). Whether they are poisons, as some say, or whatever else they may be, I do not care; once we have cleansed them through pure workmanship, stripped away their clogs and hindrances, and refined them into a fine substance, let us match them with their "likes"—the harmful things within our bodies. Shall they not leave everything else alone and immediately cling to their fellow substances (just as a purging medicine does) and so devour and draw them out little by little? If there are no "likes" <note>referring to the principle of "like cures like" or "like attracts like"</note> present, I admit they will—(h) just as a purge does—attack their enemies, our healthy fluids <note>original: "Juyces"; the vital humors of the body</note>, and feed upon them.

Then what do you doubt? Is not a *mineral body* far better? This is both because a *like* substance yields more easily than an *opposite* one, and because the more refined substance here is the stronger.

And by the way, consider: if such mild things as *wine* and *honey*, so simply prepared, are able to subdue in that manner the most stiff and tough things in the world, what they would—nay, what *minerals* in their highest degree of dignity would—do to the stoutest *disease* that can grow in our *bodies*.

But I waste time in vain to speak so much about so small a matter; and yet since all are not of equal capacity, I will add yet one familiar example. When a <term>surgeon</term> <note>original: "Chirurgeon"</note> goes about to search a wound or abscess somewhat deeply, if he thrusts at it with a *butcher's skewer* <note>original: "Prick"</note>, he shall do nothing but move laughter. Let him take a *thorn*, and it will pierce somewhat effectively; but to do it thoroughly and at his pleasure, he will use (though to the great grief of his *patient*) a fine and long *instrument* of *metal*. But a right <term>surgeon</term> (the common ones are but *butchers*)—such a one as is a <term>physician</term>, nay, a philosopher also—

Even so, a good *physician*—the kind who is not often seen—if he had to encounter our greatest enemies (those four humors or diseases we spoke of), would not, I hope, be so mad as to thrust at them with raw and blunt *herbal medicines*. Such a medicine would work mightily to vex and spoil his *enemies* <note>the diseases</note>, but feed and comfort—or at least not offend and hurt—his friend the *patient*. This is the *medicine* which a good and wise *physician* ought only to seek and use; if he cannot find it, let him leave the patient to *cure themselves*. In contrast, by stripping off the foul and gross *stuff* that dulls the medicinal action, and retaining the *virtue* <note>original: "virtue"; the active power or essence of a substance</note> in a narrow, strong body, they might do as much at one time—and without harm—as they currently do in twenty. <margin>(q)</margin>

<margin>(s) For indeed, we who have now grown old in the study of Galen, etc. <note>original Latin: Etenim qui in Galeno nunc consenuimus. Yet, for the sake of their own credit, virtue, and even profit—if they value what is best—they should leave behind those gilded *pills*, sugared *baits*, and all other crafty snares with which the *world* has been so long caught and tormented. ### Chapter III.
# Of Youth.

Although we may live *long* and in *health*, if our *bodies* are weak and unwieldy—as is the case in *old age*—it must necessarily hinder and clog us in this happy race toward perfection. Therefore, the third aid and step toward BLISS, which is *Youth*, was not included here without reason or out of proper order. What, then, is *Youth*? Those who have lost it know it best. It is the most active, fruitful, and beautiful state of the *body*. These are the marks and characteristics by which we may distinguish it from all other states. I mean *activity*, not only in the sense of *physical movement*, but also in the functions of *life* and *sensation*. This activity is what constitutes the very *nature* of *youth*. The other two marks <note>fruitfulness and beauty</note> are included not because they are essential requirements for either *youth* or BLISS—for they could be spared, especially *beauty*—but because they are very notable signs by which to recognize *youth*. Just as we heard regarding true *honor* and *pleasure* previously, these qualities will inevitably persist and follow, even if they are unexpected or ignored.

This, then, is the subject at hand: we must prove that it is possible for youth to be kept and preserved until the end of our lives; and furthermore, that even if it were lost before, it may be recovered and restored. Yet, first, we must consider how our *natural heat* <note>In early medicine, "natural heat" or "innate heat" was believed to be the vital force sustained by the heart that powered life and growth.</note> is the cause of our *being*.

<summary>The author defines youth as a state of physical activity, vitality, and beauty that is essential for achieving "Bliss," arguing that this state can be preserved or even restored throughout one's life.</summary>
<keywords>Youth, Bliss, Natural Heat, Vitality, Physical Health, Restoration of Youth, Early Modern Medicine</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the preservation of "in-bred heat" as the primary secret to maintaining youth and vitality.</meta>

# Chapter III. The Way to Bliss.

So the cause of our best estate and *youth* is the flower and best condition of it—that is, its chief strength and quickness. Therefore, keep or recover this, and all is done.

But we must be certain of this: that the flower of *heat* makes us *young* and flourishing. We must be sure by proof and experience, the best assurance in the world. Let us look everywhere, and we shall find it so.

<margin>(x) *In Timaeus* and *On the Republic*, as *Plato* is testified by *Clement of Alexandria* in *Stromata*, book 8, page 555; and *Tommaso Campanella* in *On the Nature of Things*, book 3, chapter 14. (y) *Girolamo Cardano* in *On Subtlety*, book 8, page 312; and *Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo* in *History of the West Indies*; and *Pietro Andrea Mattioli* in *Discourses on the First Book of Dioscorides*, in the Preface. (z) *Cardano*, as cited above in (y).</margin>

To begin with *plants*, although their life is dark and they are but lame and imperfect *beings* <note>original: "Wights"; here meaning living creatures or entities</note> (for (*x*) *Plato* gives them sense), yet their flourishing and decay, their *youth* and *age* (as I may term them), clearly follow the quickness or dullness of their in-bred heat, caused by the two *seasons* of *summer* and *winter*. This appears in (*y*) *India* <note>The author likely refers to the West Indies or tropical regions generally</note>, where because of the continual heat, moisture, and *summer* of the *country*, no *plant* feels *age* or the fall of the *leaf* (that word is useless in those parts), except for *fenugreek*. This is because, by a strange property unlike the rest, it has a strange cooling effect above all others, growing in *water* first and then standing somewhat deep away from the *sun*. Nay, (*z*) among us we see those *plants* which are *hot* and *dry*, sound and hardy, and able to withstand the force of *cold* to keep their leaves in *winter*.

<margin>(a) *Thomas Erastus*, *Disputations against Paracelsus*, part 2, page 181.</margin>

Moreover, keep off that starving *cold* and cherish the *life* within, and you may help and amend *nature*, making any *plant* flourish and bear fruit in *winter*. How is that done? It is an easy matter: (*a*) plant it in a *stove* <note>A heated room or greenhouse</note>, cover the *root* with *horse-dung*, and the rest with *chaff*, and you shall see the proof, even if the profit is not worth your cost and labor <note>original: "travel"</note>. The same is seen in *beasts*. But let us leave the middle, so that we do not come to the end too late.

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

Then why are children and old **folk** less active, fruitful, and beautiful than the younger **sort**, if not for want of **heat**? For let the **Sun** <note>The author uses "Sun" here as a metaphor for the vital internal heat or "innate radical heat" of the body.</note> on the first day—as **Galen** <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher (c. 129–216 AD) whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> says, or even before birth, as I have shown—be greatest in its store, bulk, and quantity (because it waxes and wanes according to its food, our first **moistener** <note>original: "Moistner"; referring to radical moisture, the fuel for the body's internal fire.</note>), and though this moisture decays daily from that point on; yet this **quality**, **strength**, and **activity**, which makes it worthy of the name **Heat**, is then very small. It is drowned by an excess of foreign and strange wetness—just as we see in a green **faggot** <note>A bundle of fresh, unseasoned wood that smokes rather than burns.</note>—and is unable to work its will or show itself, either to knit the sinews for **strength**, or to refine <note>original: "concoct"; the process of "cooking" or maturing bodily fluids through internal heat.</note> the **blood** for **seed** <note>Semen or reproductive essence.</note> and **color**, until that foreign moisture is spent and gone, which does not happen for a long time.

<margin>(b) **Aristotle**, *On the Generation of Animals*, Book 1, Chapter 19.</margin>
<margin>(c) As cited above, Chapter 20.</margin>

Now as for old **folks**, what is as clear as this: that by reason of the daily decay of the food of **life**, the fainting heat allows the strong knot of **strength** and **lustiness** <note>Vigor or vitality.</note> to slacken and loosen again? Does it not also cause the good refinement and color of **blood**, which previously produced **seed** and **beauty**, to decline and turn into wateriness? Sick **men** and **women** are in the same condition for the same reason. And although **women** have their **seed**, it is not a hot and quickening **seed**, but a dead **stuff**, only fit to receive **life** and **shape**. And even if we admit they are more (b) fair and smooth than **men** (who are hotter), this happens by chance; it is because the foul **leavings** <note>Waste products or impurities in the blood.</note>—the blemishes of **beauty**—are driven outward by the force of manly **heat**, whereas the slackness of the heat in **women** allows them to remain within and turn into **menses** <note>original: "Menstrues."</note>. This is a thing more grievous and noisome, in truth, than **beauty** is delightful. And because of this, (c) **Aristotle** very well calls her a **"weak man,"** which our **tongue** more fitly calls a **"wombed-man"**; and

Then, if it be cleared of all doubt that the chief strength of heat is the cause of the flower of *Age* and *Youth*, and nothing else in the world; let us take and stick to that matter, and see how that may be maintained first, and then restored.

shadow, the *men* are five cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 to 22 inches; five cubits and two handfuls would be approximately eight to nine feet tall.</note> and two handfuls high, and live a hundred and thirty years, never growing *old*. When they die, they are as they were in their middle *age*, and at their chief *strength* and *vigor* <note>original: "Lustiness"</note>. What more words are needed? If this report be true—as we should not easily doubt such an *author*—then surely this matter is not impossible, as some would have it. Instead, all *men*, if they lived in such an *air* and followed such a good course of *life* as I described (I must still rely on that point for support), might preserve their *youth* and never grow *old* until the appointed term and limit of *life*. Then, what means may we find? What preserves this natural and heavenly *heat* of ours? Common practice uses hot *foods* <note>original: "Meats"</note> and *drinks*, and thinks that these preserve *heat* and *nature*. This is as foolish as if a *man* should put *lime* to the root of a *tree* which he loved; for just as lime hastens the fruit with *heat* but kills the *trunk* <note>original: "Stock"</note> with *drought* by soaking up the living juice and moisture, so do their unnaturally hot *foods*. This was a fact they did not know because their eyes were set too high to see the lower cause and course of **Nature**, which is most plain and certain.

For God, when he intended to make our changeable **World** here below, by a wonderful, far-sighted wisdom, restricted the **Sun** within known bounds—the North and South turns (which they call **Tropics**). This was done lest, if he had run round about the whole globe, he should have worn and wasted it everywhere alike and made it smooth and even in all places. Had he done so, the world would be either all dry **Ground** or a standing **Pool**, both of which are unfit for the variety of change which He intended to see play before Him. 

But now, because the Sun is so curbed and restrained within those aforementioned bounds, he can wear the ground no further than his force can reach, nor in any other way than his force allows. (n) <margin>(n) Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo, *History of the West Indies*, folio 183.</margin> So it follows that the **Earth** must necessarily be most worn and lowest where it lies within the compass of his **Walk** <note>The Sun's path between the Tropics.</note>, and so it rises little by little on both sides outside of those **Turns**, until it comes to the top and highest pitch where it is furthest away: that is, under the **Pins** (which they call **Poles**) of the **World**.

Then here, because of the **Coldness**, the **Earth** is suited to thicken the **Air** and breed **Water**; and because of the slope and falling of the land, it sends that water down to the widest and lowest part. But for all this, and in all seriousness (for we have only argued the point so far), it is not good to seek an exemption against the Law of Nature. It would be better to discredit Pliny the reporter (though he is a very good author) than Nature herself, who is the author of all things. For this story <note>Referring to Pliny's claims of people who do not age until the moment of death.</note> is set against the whole course and drift of Nature, whose works are not woven and completed all at once, and likewise decay and wear away little by little. 

Therefore, even if we admit that these men of India, by special license from above, do keep their appearance fresh and young for a long time compared to other nations, we must by no means think this lasts forever and until the very moment of death, as Pliny says. For if that were true, they would not die and depart naturally as other men do—which happens when age, creeping on and changing the body little by little, is at last made ripe for falling. # Chapter III. The Way to Bliss.

If sickness and disease were removed—as he says they almost are—they might live forever; yet another breach of the never-broken laws of nature <note>original: "Kinde"</note>.

Therefore, let this story go, and let us hold this rule as certain: because there is no other food for our natural heat <note>In medieval and early modern medicine, "natural heat" was the vital energy that sustained life</note> provided by nature except for our radical moisture <note>original: "fitst Moisture"; the essential fluid believed to fuel the body's heat</note>, which daily wastes away for want of supply, youth must necessarily fail by nature and cannot last forever. And yet we must also (to come to the purpose) remember how it was frequently proved above that such a supply of the proper food of life could be created by skill and extracted from the depths of natural things by the divine art of Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note>. Therefore, to avoid the jarring and ill sound of repeating ourselves, our "Cure-all" <note>original: "Panacea"</note> and "Heaven" described previously is what feeds our hearts and preserves our youth. This is it, I say, that does the deed for many causes set down before; I will send those who cannot follow the argument here back to the beginning to review everything.

But there is another thing—I mean motion—that helps to support the state of life and heat. I barely touched upon it there, and yet it should be handled; because although it is not as essential as the former, it cannot in any case be lacking. For just as a martial people, resembling Mars (as we call it) and valor itself, lose their glory and brightness in peace and quietness, so this heat that rules our body—though it be ever so strong and lusty—cannot rest without decaying and, as it were, rusting with idleness. Nay, the body itself, being (as I showed above) a mixture of air and fire <note>original: "ayery and fiery temperature"</note>, must necessarily have quick motion as one of the two pillars of its state. # The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

<margin>(o) Plato in *Timaeus*. Plutarch, in the book *Precepts of Health*, near the end.</margin>
*Plato*, (o) following the example of the "Great World" <note>original: *Macrocosm*, or the universe</note>, very wisely advises us to constantly move both *Body* and *Mind*, and to do so together, if we intend for them to last a long time. We find his counsel proven true by daily experience: we see that those who exercise the *Memory* most—such as wise and learned *Men*—retain it the longest. However, because they mostly fail to exercise their *Body*, they lose their physical health quickly. Conversely, common people, because they move the body much and the mind little, remain robust in *Body* for a long time, even after their *Memory* has quickly faded.

<margin>(p) Cardano, *On Subtlety*, Book 2, page 81.</margin>
How *Motion* increases *Heat* is apparent everywhere. First, consider the source of all *Heat*, the *Sun* above; its warmth could never reach far enough to heat half the *World* at once if those huge quantities of heavenly *Beams* and *Spirits* <note>The author refers to the "vital spirits" or active particles thought to inhabit sunlight</note> were locked up tight—as they are in *stones*, *metals*, and similar dense, hard materials—rather than being (as we see them) completely free, quick, lively, and swiftly moving. (p) Likewise, no *Fire* here below burns as fiercely as one that is driven together by a cold blast, which we see causes it to move and stir most vigorously.

<margin>(q) Cardano, *Varieties*, Book 7, Chapter 39. Caelius Rhodiginus, *Ancient Readings*, Book 8, Chapter 12. Johann Heidfeld, *Theological Sphinx*, Chapter 6.</margin>
To move past *Lightning* (which is the swiftest and strangest fire in the *World*) and many other such proofs—for why should I dwell so long on such an obvious matter?—*Motion* does not only increase *Heat* where it already exists, but creates and acquires it from nothing. This happens not only in the way every *Man* sees by rubbing two hard objects together, but also by striking a hard object against the soft and yielding *Air*. Though this is somewhat rare, it was known to the (q) *Babylonians* in the past, who used to roast *Eggs* by whirling them around in a sling in that very manner. And so those

*Archers* (r) who have seen the leaden heads of their *arrows* melt while flying—so great a father of *heat* is *motion*—know that we may easily judge that motion is able to maintain heat once it is acquired.

Now, if this has been sufficiently shown and proved, we need to bestow less labor in teaching *men* how to move their *heat* and *spirits*, because every child who can walk can do it; it is enough simply to exhort those who love themselves to actually do it.

Therefore, by these two means of consistent *heat* and *motion*, we retain our youth—that is, our primary *complexion*, *fertility*, and *activity*. Is there anything else? These make up the entire *being* and nature of *youth*, unless you fear the loss of its "hang-bys" <note>original: "Hang-byes"; meaning secondary characteristics or appendages</note> and appurtenances, which are: *teeth*, the *sweetness* of *breath*, the *smoothness* of the *skin*, and the *natural color* of the *hair*. But there is no danger here, if you will let me review them; for if our *heat* and *moisture* remain without decaying, first the *jawbones*, in which the *teeth* are mortised <note>fixed or socketed</note>, will remain full and moist, able to grip and "glue," and so hold the teeth from falling out.

Gray *hairs* come from the same cause; for when our *natural heat* faints and fails, it withdraws itself from the outermost and coldest parts first. This leaves the *moisture* raw, and for lack of inward *heat* and *salt* to preserve it, that moisture lies open to the force of outward *cold*. (u) Having fallen into the hands of *pirates*, he was wrapped in a sail and prepared to be thrown overboard. He could never get his hair to return to its original color for the rest of his life, though he lived a long time afterward.

And so we have briefly and easily settled this point, because it was a simple matter. But the next task—to recover *young years* that have been spent and blown away—seems to be no such thing, nor can it be handled in the same way. Just as a new and strong *building* is kept sound for a long time through proper and daily repairs, yet cannot be renewed without great cost and time if it falls into decay through lack of care; so it is with our *body*. While it is easy to preserve the body if we take care of it in time, if *negligence* has allowed the *weather* to beat in and rot the structure, it seems a marvelous work to repair it.

Although this task is indeed much harder, not only than the previous point but than all the others that came before, we will not give up now. We will not, like a lazy *poet*, *faint in the last act of life*. Therefore, let us go forward and strive with all our effort to show that *youth*, though lost long ago, may—though not easily—be

# Chapter III. The Way to Bliss.

...be recovered, as it was before preserved.

<margin>(w) Cardano, *On Subtlety*, book 9, page 353.</margin>
There are so many kinds of "waxing young" <note>original: "waxing Young"; becoming youthful</note> again mentioned in philosophy, and attributed to the nature of living creatures <note>original: "Wights"</note>, that it would be best first to categorize them. We must specify which kind we mean in this passage, lest our labor fall into the hands of those who are quick to misunderstand. One of these ways is in name only, and not in reality: for example, when soft-skinned and bark-skinned beasts, by the course of nature, twice a year—at the spring and the fall of the leaf—cast off their upper coat and skin, people say they "put off old age" and "grow young" again. In truth, this is actually the "putting on" of age and the decay of nature. This is apparent to those who know the cause: that due to extreme cold and drought (w)—the two clear hallmarks of age—their skins loosen and wither away.

<margin>(x) "The Egyptians concerning wisdom." Synesius, *On Providence*, at the beginning.</margin>
There is another kind of renewal at the opposite extreme, which is entirely real. *Alcmaeon* <note>A Greek philosopher and medical theorist</note> calls this "joining one's end to his beginning." He says that man cannot do this, and therefore he dies. This is, and ever was, the opinion not only of poets but of philosophers; and not only of Greece, but of all nations—except for our ancient Egyptians (x), men who are always excepted for their rare wisdom. These men, as I mentioned above, do not only observe the steps of nature and her most strange and unusual changes, but they also attempt to replicate them through skill. Indeed, they venture even further, if any reason will guide them. So, at length, they arrived—I know not whether by guess or by certain knowledge—at this rule and certain ground: that it was possible for any man, unless cut off by a forcible and violent death, to rise and quicken again by natural means, and so be renewed and restored as if by a new birth.

<summary>The author distinguishes between superficial biological shedding (like a snake's skin) and the true philosophical "rejuvenation" sought by the ancient Egyptians, who believed natural means could restore life and youth.</summary>

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

<margin>

But what are their new and marvelous methods? In what way is this incredible process performed? They observed not only that some parts of other <term>Wights</term> <note>original: "Wights"; an archaic term for living creatures or beings</note>—such as (y) the tails of lizards, the (z) eyes of snakes, and (a) swallows—but also the entire bodies of cold-blooded creatures, though completely erased and destroyed, would naturally spring forth anew and be restored. For example, (b) a snake cut into pieces and rotted in dung will come to life, and every piece will become a whole snake again. Seeing such things, they began to reach through invention and practice toward even greater matters. They attempted to flay certain warm-blooded creatures—those that do not emerge from nothing but are bred by the power of seed and the union of male and female—and by a similar process of natural corruption, to raise them up and renew them. (For instance, (c) a bird might be burnt alive in a sealed glass vessel, allowed to rot, and then enclosed in a shell to be hatched under a hen to restore it.) They did not cease making such strange experiments until at last they were bold enough to think that any living being, even a man, might by the same process grow young, be born again repeatedly, and live forever.

This is the second method of becoming young again. It is as great an extreme as the first method I mentioned, and just as far from my own meaning. Nevertheless, there are various reports and stories circulating about men who attempted this same process on themselves and others. They met with both good and bad success, depending (as a supporter of the theory might think) on how well the work was managed by those placed in trust. <term>Medea</term> <note>In Greek mythology, a sorceress known for her powers of rejuvenation.</note> succeeded well, they say, in her experiment upon Jason’s father, and made him young again, as <term>Tully</term> <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator.</note> says, *by boiling him again* <note>original: "recoquendo"; Latin for "by cooking again" or "by re-boiling," referring to the myth where Medea restored Aeson's youth in a cauldron.</note>.

<summary>The author discusses alchemical and pseudo-scientific theories of rejuvenation inspired by the regenerative abilities of animals like lizards and snakes, leading to the radical idea that humans could be "re-born" through controlled decay and restoration.</summary>
<keywords>rejuvenation, alchemy, Paracelsus, Medea, Jason, Cicero, resurrection, natural history, spontaneous generation, regeneration</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
But Hermes <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note>, and the poet Virgil, and that Spanish Earl <note>likely a reference to a popular alchemical legend of a nobleman who attempted rejuvenation</note> failed in their attempts, as some believe; however, others hold that they had good luck and achieved their purpose.

What should a man say to this matter? Although I do not choose this particular method of renewing the body, yet I will not condemn it without cause or judge it to be impossible. For I see no reason why the story of the snake <note>referring to the ancient belief that snakes could shed their skin and regain youth</note> might not easily be true, because the snake is bred by itself and from even more unfit material in the same manner. As for the rest, it is all the same to Nature, provided the material and the location are suitable and ready. Nature possesses that general "seed" of creation (which, as I said before, is the same in all things) ever ready in her bosom. By this means, she still creates seeded plants without actual seeds in certain places—as we heard before—and even perfect living creatures <term>wights</term>, both of water and land. Indeed, at the beginning of the world, when the material, the womb, and her own heat all served perfectly, she created man and all other things in this way.

But now, why is seed given to living things? It is because Nature—lacking those original conditions, as they could not last forever—is not able in all places to work the raw material of beginnings into such perfection, unless she finds the material well-prepared and half-made for her hand, along with a hot womb like an artificial furnace to help and propel her work forward. Well then, regarding this matter of ours and the method of restoring man, let us hold it to the account of reason and consider: what is that seed that makes man, and what is the place where he is made? What is the entire process? Is it anything other than a part of man (excepting his mind) rotted <note>original: "rotted"; in alchemy, "putrefaction" or breaking down was considered the first step of transformation</note> in a continual, even, gentle, moist, and living heat? Is it not likely that the whole body, if rotted in the same manner and in a suitable womb,

It performs more than the one on the outside, and less within than the other. ...marvellous turning. Is not this a much more difficult and greater natural <note>original: "kindly"</note> change and alteration than that which we spoke of? Then we must grant that Nature is able to do this, if she is willing; but it seems she is not, because she never does it. She runs her appointed race continually, if she is not hindered <note>original: "letted"</note>. But if there are many accidental chances able to stop and hinder this course of Nature, how much more can the wit of man—which is a spark of that Divine Mind which gave Nature <note>original: "Kinde"</note> her commission—do it? As he often does (if I had the time to show it) both stop, lengthen, and turn her course another way, and yet she is always willing and consenting.

Let us see then, regarding this matter in hand, how *skill* is able to overrule *nature* <note>original: "Kinde"</note> by her own consent, and make her willing to return and grow *young* again. First, let us know that all *philosophers* <margin>(l) Macrobius, *Commentary on the Dream of Scipio*, Book 1, Chapter 14.</margin> hold the *life*, *soul*, and *natural heat* to be always of itself young and lusty, and never old, but only appearing to be so by reason of the failing parts, which are its instruments. And I have often shown it to be a kind of *fire*, waxing and waning according to its fuel <note>original: "Meat"</note> and *motion*. 

Then here is one good help to the great work of renewing: in like sort, the parts of the *body* are not ruined and lost, but are as they say of a *rich man*: that he is decayed when his *money*—the *life* of the *world*—has left and forsaken him. <margin>(m) Aristotle, *On the Soul*, Book 1, Chapter 3.</margin> Even so, when our *natural heat*—the *life* of this little *world* <note>The "microcosm," or the human body.</note>—is faint and gone, the *body* shrinks up and is defaced. But bring *heat* back into the parts, just as you would bring *money* back into the bankrupt's coffers, and they shall both be lusty and flourish again as much as they ever did.

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

In a few words, she <note>The "Life" or vital spirit of the human body.</note> is maintained and held just as she is kept: by proper *Nourishment* <note>original: "Meat"</note> and *Motion*. For if she faints and falls only for want of these, then give them to her, and she shall recover herself again. *Nourishment* is the bait that draws her down; *Motion* follows after, like a *Horse-fly* <note>original: "Gad-Bee"</note> to prick her forward. 

But the work is performed in this order: first, this *Nourishment*—which is that fine and *Heavenly Oil* <note>original: "Æthereal Oyl"</note> described often above—divides, scatters, and scours away the gross and foul dregs and *residue* <note>original: "Leavings"</note> by its exceeding, piercing swiftness. These dregs had overgrown in our *bodies* for want of the cultivation of *Heat*, and were cast like a sluggish *Remora* <note>original: "Stay-fish"; a fish believed in antiquity to slow or stop ships by attaching to them.</note> in the path to stop the free course of the Ship of *Life*. These dregs, flying out of all sides abundantly, pluck up all the old *residue* of hair, nails, and *teeth* by the roots and drive them out before them.

In the meantime, our *Medicine* does not only make a clear way and passage for *Life*, should she choose to stir and run her accustomed race (which some think is sufficient for this matter), but it also scatters all around her the proper and desired *Nourishment* and primary *Moisture* to draw her forward.

By these means, our *Life*—having regained both her full strength and liveliness, and having returned like the *Sun* in summer into all our parts—begins to work afresh as she did at first. Since she is the same force acting upon the same material, she must necessarily produce the same results: knitting and binding the weak and loose *joints* and *sinews*, watering and ripening <note>original: "concocting"</note> everything through good digestion. Then the dormant parts, like leaves in this hot summer, shall spring and grow forth anew out of this new and young temperament of the body; and the entire face and appearance shall be *young* again and *flourishing*.

This is quickly spoken, you say; if it were as soon done,

# Chapter III. The Way to Bliss.

If this were done, it would be a happy *Medicine*; indeed, that would be a miracle, but I work no miracles. I only help, as I said, the willing course of *Nature* <note>original: "Kinde"; referring to the natural order or species</note>. Therefore, just as a man is a long time decaying and wearing away—or rather, a long time in the making and growing toward his perfection—so in healing, no doubt, he cannot return to health all at once. He must creep back little by little and so be restored. 

Otherwise, I would have told you at the very first mention of that spring in the *Island of Bimini* <note>original: "Iſle Bovicca"; likely referring to the mythical "Boinca" or Bimini associated with the Fountain of Youth</note>, which (as *Peter* (n) *Martyr* witnesses) will in a few days completely restore a *man* (even one having gray hairs and wrinkles) and make him *young* again.

Truly, if I had taken a course intended to delight *women* and *children*, and to win credit among the common people, I could have searched the *legends* and rifled through all the goodly *wonders* in the *world*, fitting many of them to my purpose. But as I serve *Nature* and wait upon a wiser *Mistress*—indeed, in the most inward and secret place among them—I would, by my own will, speak nothing that should not be pleasing in her sight and well-sounding in the ears of *Wisdom*. Therefore, let these few words suffice for this matter.

### CHAPTER IV.
## Of RICHES.

<margin>
(a) <note>original: Aristot. lib. 10. eth. c. ult.</note> Aristotle, Ethics, Book 10, final chapter.

We have finally arrived at the point which the *Golden World* <note>The "Golden World" refers to a materialistic society or the "Gilded Age" of the author's perspective.</note> looked for first: the way to *Riches*. This is discussed last because it is indeed the lowest part of our study—since riches are merely *servants* and should be used as such—and yet they are very necessary. They cannot be spared in this blessed *household*; for although we may have every help that *Long Life*, *Health*, and *Youth* can provide, if we lack the service of *Riches*, *Poverty* will besiege us. It will keep us suppressed and hinder many excellent deeds and works of *Wisdom* and *Virtue*. But what exactly are *Riches*? The *World* and *Philosophy* do not agree on this definition, nor does Philosophy even agree with itself.

<margin>
(o) <note>original: εὖ γὰρ τὸν πλοῦτον πολλάκις τιθέασι νομίσματ@ πλῆθ@.</note> For they often rightly define wealth as a multitude of money.

The *World* (o) considers a store of *Gold* and *Silver* to be *Riches*; *Aristotle* defines it as having enough of necessary things; the *Stoics* <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy that taught self-control and detachment from material distractions.</note> claim it is having enough of *Earth* and *Air*. To begin here: these definitions might be stretched and made broad enough, were it not that we already know their limitations. Would the Stoics have us live by breath alone and never eat, according to the method I described in the *Art of Healing*? Even if that were possible, as it seems to be, it is a somewhat feeble existence, as I demonstrated there. Such a life is somewhat halting and imperfect—lacking *Youth* and *Vigor* <note>original: "Lustiness"</note>—compared to the primary and perfect *Life* we are meant for, not to mention the injuries and hardships of *Poverty* which I just mentioned.

# Chapter III. The Way to Bliss.

<margin>(p) Aristotle, *Politics*, book 1, chapter 9.</margin>
<margin>(q) Pliny, *Natural History*, book 9, chapter 57.</margin>
If he had said that wealth consists of those things necessary for a good *life*—the purpose for which we were created—he would have spoken much better, yet still not entirely accurately. If we defined it that way, all the physical means and aids mentioned previously would be categorized as *riches*, which is far too confusing. Now, we can even less accurately consider *golden wealth* to be right and true *riches*, (p) because a man may die of hunger despite possessing all of it. An example of this is the man who sold a *mouse* for two hundred pence; he died himself for lack of food while the *buyer* lived. (q) This occurred (to set aside the fictional story of *Midas* <note>The mythological king whose touch turned everything to gold, leading to his starvation.</note>) when *Hannibal* besieged *Casline* <note>original: "Cas-line"; likely referring to Casilinum, a city besieged by Hannibal where extreme famine occurred.</note>.

Therefore, true *riches* consist of a sufficient amount of outward things necessary for a good *life*—that is, for the "Bliss" we defined above. However, because golden and worldly *wealth* is a ready and certain means to achieve this (barring violence, which no man can guarantee protection against), we will use the cause for the effect in this discussion. We will strive to show how all men may obtain enough *gold* and *silver*. We shall do this by means less intense than "Hermes' Medicine" <note>A reference to the Philosopher's Stone or the ultimate alchemical elixir attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, as the situation requires, although we will follow the same method regarding the *material* we work upon: that is, by turning base *metals* into *silver* and *gold*. 

This is the difficult subject that dulls the edge of worldly minds. I say that the brightness of this glorious thing dazzles the eyes of common and "blear-eyed" <note>meaning dim-sighted or intellectually short-sighted</note> people because they consider it the best, highest, and most happy thing in the world. In reality and truth, it is the least, lowest, and worst of all the aids belonging to Bliss. Furthermore, in actual practice and trial, it is the least difficult and troublesome for both Art and Nature to achieve, being the most ready and easy to be obtained and performed.

And to demonstrate this (we will not delay long), it would be best first of all to enter into the method and order

...rals: But as the *Water* runs (to take the *stuff* <note>The raw material or "materia prima" of minerals.</note> as the next thing in order), it washes and shaves off small (z) pieces of the *Rock*. When it stands and gathers together in one place, (a) by continual draining, it cleanses and refines the same, until the middle heat of the *Earth*—which is the heat of *Heaven* <note>Likely referring to celestial or solar influence penetrating the earth.</note>—arrives. By long boiling, this heat makes it thicker, causing it to grow together into one body of many kinds, according to the difference of the *stuff* and the heat. These they call *Hard-juices*, as I said, or *Middle-minerals*.

<margin>(b) *On the Origin and Causes of Subterraneans*, Book 5, Chapter 12.</margin>
This *Workman* <note>Nature personified as a craftsman.</note> continuing and holding on his labor, out of the heart and best part of them, wrings out at last a clean, close, heavy, raw, watery, and running body called *Quicksilver* <note>Mercury.</note>. This occurs even though Agricola (b) says the cold and drought of the *Rock* now take hold of the *stuff* and, by little and little, at last bind it into that hard form of a *Metal*; indeed, even though Aristotle from the beginning attributes the work to the same cause. Here it stands in the perfection of this *Mineral* work, unless there happens (which happens often) by means of that boiling, any contrary hot and dry breath of the same kind to be made at the same time in the same place. Then, this meeting with that raw, watery, and unshapen lump—like *Rennet* with *Milk*, or *Seed* with *Menstruum* <note>original: *Menstrue*; referring to the alchemical concept of the female reproductive fluid or base matter being "curdled" by the male seed.</note>—curdles, thickens, and fashions it into the standing body of a *Metal*.

<margin>(c) Severinus Danus, *The Idea of Philosophical Medicine*, Chapter 7, pages 67–68.</margin>
<margin>(d) See Bernard Trevisan, *Letter to Thomas of Bononia*. *The Golden Art*, Volume 2, pages 83–84.</margin>
This *Mineral* breath our (c) *Men* <note>The Alchemists or "Philosophers."</note>, because of its likeness in quality (though their *Substance* differs greatly), are accustomed to call *Brimstone* <note>Sulfur.</note>. Now, when this (d) second and earthly heat has entered the work, the mild (d) heat of *Heaven* sets the *stuff*, which was stationary before, to work again and drives it forward. These (d) two together...

<margin>

(f) Gaston Claveus, *Apology for Chrysopoeia* <note>original: "Chrysop." refers to the art of making gold</note> and *Arguments*, Theatrum Chemicum, volume 2, page 80.
</margin>
Through continuous boiling and mingling, they alter and change, cleanse and refine it from degree to degree. At last, after many years of labor, it reaches the height of perfection in *cleanliness*, *fineness*, and *density* <note>original: "closeness"</note>, which they call *Gold*. These degrees—if the *heat* is gentle and patient, as they say—are first (e) *Lead*, then *Tin*, thirdly *Silver*, and finally *Gold*. But if the heat is strong and sudden, it quickly drives the weak work off course and burns it up, creating nothing but *Iron*; or at least, if the *heat* is somewhat better, it produces *Copper*. Indeed, sometimes the foulness of that earthly *brimstone* <note>term: sulfur</note> alters the course of *nature* in this work. There are also differences (f) in the quality of *quicksilver* <note>term: mercury</note>. But truly, the cause of all the variation lies in the working *heat*, which shapes and directs the beginning, middle, and end of everything in this way or that, according to its strength and duration. The main point for our purpose is that *quicksilver* is the mother of all metals.

<margin>
(g) Thomas Erastus, *Disputation on Metals*, page 19; and Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo, *History*, book 6, chapter 8, cited in Simon Maiolus, *Colloquy 19: On Metals*; and Font. *On the Origin of Living Fountains*, book 3, chapter 15.
(h) Ovid, *Metamorphoses*, book 15; and Pliny, *Natural History*, book 32, chapter 2; and Gaudentius Merula, book 4, chapter 21; and Dioscorides, book 5, chapter 29; and Johannes Langius in his *Epistle to William Rascolon*, found at the end; and Andrea Bacci, *Book on Gems*, page 2274.
</margin>
Now, when the work is finished, it still remains (g) as it did throughout the process: in a thick, flowing form, like the state of a molten *metal*. When the owner comes to collect it, introducing the cold breath of the *air* upon it, it behaves like (h) *coral* and other soft, growing *sea-plants*: it freezes and hardens suddenly, becoming fit for the service and use of *man*, for which purpose it was made and ordained. These are the principles held by the majority and the best of *men*—that is, men most knowledgeable and most widely traveled in such matters. *Cardano* <note>Girolamo Cardano, a famous 16th-century Italian polymath</note>, an impartial man who is not one of our circle and yet is very learned, agrees exactly with this.

But lest these dim and little lights seem to be darkened by the brightness and fame of *Aristotle*...
