# Chapter IV. The Way to Bliss.

And his scholar *Theophrastus* <note>Theophrastus (c. 371 – c. 287 BC), a Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle, known for his works on botany and mineralogy.</note>, and the late renowned *Agricola* <note>Georgius Agricola (1494–1555), often called the "father of mineralogy."</note>, holding firmly to the contrary, and sometimes stiffly maintaining the same, I will—as much as lies within me and as my narrow limits will allow—endeavor to lay down all the *reasons* in order which moved them to think thus and stayed them in that same opinion. I do this so that *wise men* at least may weigh one *reason* against another and judge which is the weightiest and worthy of the highest value, without the vain regard for outward appearances and authorities.

<margin>
(i) It is called by the Greeks *hydrargyros* <note>original: ὑδράργυρος</note>. By Aristotle and Theophrastus it is called "poured silver" <note>original: ἀργυρόχυτον</note> and "movable silver" <note>original: ἄργυρον κινητὸν</note>. By Alexander of Aphrodisias it is called "poured" <note>original: χύτον</note>. In Arabic, *Zaibar* or *Zibach*. In Spanish, *Argento vivo* or *Azoque*. In French, *Argent vif*. In German, *Queck silver*, etc. Matthias Untzer, *Anatomy of Spagyric Mercury*, Book 1, Chapter 1. They call it *Argento vivo* [living silver].
</margin>

First, all cunning *miners* can tell you that the *mineral stuff* <note>The raw ore or matter.</note> springs out from those rock-shavings mentioned before. These miners, by observing the nature and grit of the stone—even if there are twenty different types in the *rock*—are able to say with certainty which *vein* follows. But to pass lightly over these minor matters (which they grant as well as we): the real question in great dispute is whether *quicksilver* is the nearest *stuff* and *menstruum* <note>term: menstruum; a solvent or foundational substance used in alchemy to dissolve or "mother" other materials.</note>, or the *mother of metals*.

In my opinion, this need not be a question if we mark the consensus of all those *men* in all *nations* who gave names to things (and they were not of the most foolish sort). They flatly allow this saying when they call it in *(i)* *Greek, Latin*, and other *tongues*, *quick* or *liquid silver*. In their secret meaning, they plainly say that if it were stabilized and better purged by the force of those two hot *workmen* <note>Referring to the internal heat of the earth and sulfurous heat.</note> mentioned before, *(k)* it would be nothing other than *silver*.

<margin>
(k) Greek: *hydrargyron* <note>original: ὑδράργυρον</note>, that is, formerly "fine living silver." Arabic: *Zaibar* and *Zaiback*. The Germans: *Quicksilver*.
</margin>

Indeed, *Avicenna* <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose alchemical theories on the sulfur-mercury composition of metals were highly influential.</note> and some others of the learned side, leaving out the middle degrees, hold this very same opinion. I also think this is true, provided the *stuff* and the *heats* (as they are found in hot countries) are good and without fault.

<margin>
Synonyms: *Azoque*. Discourse of Mattioli in the 5th book of Dioscorides, chapter 69. (k) Cardano, *On Subtlety*, book 5, page 220.
</margin>

</margin>
But the disputers will consider this kind of argument unskillful and soon dismiss it. Therefore, (l) remove the cold that eventually came upon the *metal* and hardened it, and it appears to the eye as nothing else but such an altered *quicksilver* <note>original: "Quick-silver"; mercury</note>. Or, if the witness of the senses is sometimes false and deceitful, enter into our *school* and behold them, by a more natural and gentle way, lead the metals back to a true *quicksilver* that remains stable in both cold and heat. For it is a true (m) rule in *philosophy* that "everything is made of that into which it is loosened and dissolved" <note>original: "Omne compositum in illa ex quibus componitur resolvitur"</note>.

But if this will not suffice, pass a little further into the border and edge of secrets. You shall see them—by following the steps of *nature* <note>original: "Kinde"</note> underneath (which I marked out before)—sowing the dissolved seeds and breaths of *metals* upon *quicksilver* to curdle and bring her into that form of *metal* which they desire and wish for.

<margin>
(n) Aristotle, *Physics*, book 2, chapter 2; and *Metaphysics*, book 12, chapter 4.
</margin>
<margin>
(o) Francisco Vallés, *Sacred Philosophy*, chapter 49, page 279.
</margin>
Now, concerning that earthly *brimstone* <note>original: "Brimstone"; sulfur, considered the active "male" principle in alchemy</note>: just as *nature*, to make a perfect *living being* <note>original: "Wight"</note>, is obliged to break her first order and take the help of a hot *womb* and of another *workman*; even so, to frame a perfect dead *creature* <note>In this context, a mineral or metal</note>, besides the help of a certain dead *womb*, she must necessarily use the hand of a vigorous fellow *workman* both to fashion and boil it to perfection. Then, as (n) *Aristotle* says, "*Sun and man produce man*" <note>original: "Sol et homo generant hominem"</note>, and the rest have two working and moving causes: the *heat of heaven* and the breath of the *male-seed*. So in this work of *metal*, there is not only the great and (o) general begetting breath of *heaven*, but also the private and particular seed of the earth, their father.

That there lacks a little earth to stay <note>To "stay" here means to fix or stabilize a volatile substance</note> *quicksilver*,

<margin>(p) Aristotle, *On the Parts of Animals*, Book 2, Chapter 4; *History of Animals*, Book 3, Chapter 19; and *Meteorology*, Book 4, Chapters 7 and 10.</margin>
<margin>(q) Hippocrates, Book 6, *On Ancient Medicine* <note>original: περὶ ἀρχ.</note>; Plato in *Timaeus*; and Galen in the book *That the Soul's Manners Follow the Body's Temperament*.</margin>

*Aristotle* himself shows this by a very similar example; he (p) says that the blood of a *hare* remains fluid even when it is cold, whereas the blood of other animals thickens <note>original: "stand"</note>, because it lacks those earthly fibers <note>original: "Streams"</note> which others have to make it clot together. As we may see by experiment, (q) we find no *blood*—once those fibers have been removed with a strainer—that will thicken and cluster; instead, it runs continually. Even so, if you take away the *Earth* and *Brimstone* <note>term: sulfur, considered a primary active principle in alchemy</note> of a *Metal* (which our Art is able to do), the *Water* will not thicken again, but will flow forever. This is a general rule, if we observe closely: nothing thickens and stops running until the dominant *Earth* binds and stays it.

<margin>(r) Aristotle, *Meteorology*, Book 4, Chapter 8.</margin>
<margin>(s) Georgius Agricola, *On the Origin and Causes of Subterraneans*, Book 5.</margin>
<margin>(t) Pietro Andrea Mattioli in the preface to Book 5 of *Dioscorides*; Denis Zachaire's *Opuscule* in the *Theatrum Chemicum*, Volume 1, page 802; and *The Consort of the Marriage of the Sun and Moon* in the *Theatrum Chemicum*, Volume 5, page 484.</margin>

Whoever does not accept this method of making *Metals* will, besides other failures and errors, never unfold the nature of *Quicksilver* <note>term: Mercury</note>. We may see this in (r) *Aristotle* and (s) *Agricola*, who struggle and strive against the facts regarding it. They attribute the cause of its flowing and its tendency to evaporate <note>original: "flying from the Fire"</note> to an abundance of *Air* within it. If that were true, then its lightness and its ability to fuel a *Fire*—two things far from its actual nature—would be as apparent to us as they are in all other airy bodies. 

But he who relies upon our grounds and rules laid down before may easily perceive its *raw*, (t) *cold*, and *watery* condition, which makes it flee the *Fire*, its enemy. Furthermore, one can see that the equal proportion in power and the balanced rule of *Earth* and *Water* within it is the cause of its fluidity. The first point is plain; but there is as much *Earth* in power as there is *Water* in *Quicksilver* (even though it seems to be all *Water*, for a little *Earth* is as strong as much *Water*). That there is no more of one than the other, surely mingled and put together, is evident because it is the only *dry Water* in the world. Its *Earth* pulling one way makes it dry, and its *Water* pulling another way causes it to flow; but

This is a certain sign of it: we find through reason that all other things—if either earth or water rules over them—either stand firm with cold and harden, or else melt with fire and water. Yet we see plainly that this one "dry water" called quicksilver <note>mercury</note> yields to neither. But to return to our purpose.

<margin>
(u) For the lightning of heaven passes through the enclosures of houses, etc. <note>original Latin: "Transit enim fulmen coeli per septa domorum"</note> Lucretius, Book 1; and Paracelsus, *Archidoxa Magica*, Book 5; and the anonymous *Enchiridion Physicae Restitutae*, canon 199; and Asclepius to King Ammon, Book 1; and St. Alban <note>Francis Bacon</note>, *On the Advancement of Learning*, Book 3, chapter 4; and Dr. Hakewill, *Apology*, Book 2, chapter 4, section 1; and Pico della Mirandola, *Disputations against Astrology*, Book 3, chapter 4, page 311.
</margin>

The reasons why the heat of heaven is the "workman" in the mine are many; but hear a few, delivered briefly. If this heat works and mingles all perfectly composed bodies (as I proved above), then what shall hinder or bar it from this labor also? Is it the depth and hardness of the rock? No; for if those subtle bodies which we call spirits are able, in the opinion of all men, to pierce through stone walls without a breach or sign of passage, how much more subtle, strong, and able to do so is this heavenly soul? 

Furthermore, all men grant that the creation <note>original: "workmanship"</note> of living things flows from that single cause and fountain. Tell us, then, how it happens that fish (by the witness of reliable authors) are sometimes found deep within the solid earth where no water runs? Nay, by what way do actual toads get into certain rocks in Germany, and into millstone-rocks in France? They are so enclosed that they cannot be seen until the stones are set for grinding and break open, as George Agricola <note>A famous 16th-century German scholar known as the "father of mineralogy."</note> reports.

<margin>
(x) *Book on Subterranean Animals*, near the end. </margin>

But if minerals, as well as plants, take food and nourishment, and increase and grow in size, then all is clear, I hope, and free of doubt. I will prove this hereafter. In the meantime, let us confirm it again by proof and trial.

# Chapter IV. The Way to Bliss.

The strongest battery <note>original: "Battery" — here meaning a forceful argument or assault</note> that may be. *Cold* binds and gathers in the *matter* of both like and unlike, gross and fine together, without any cleansing or sundering. But *Metals*, especially *Gold*, are very finely and cleanly purged *Bodies*. Again, if *Cold* had frozen and packed up *Gold* together, 
<margin>(y) Aristotle, *Meteorology*, book 4, chapter 7.</margin> 
the force of *Heat* (as we see the proof in all things) should cut the bands and unmask the work again, which does not happen. To this, what *Color* springs from *Cold* but its own watery and earthy color? 
<margin>(z) Nuisement: *Treatise on Salt and the General Spirit*, chapter 3, page 24.</margin> 
So that if a thing be dyed with other colors, we know straightway where it obtained them. 
<margin>(a) Aristotle, *On Sense and Sensible Objects*, chapter 5; and Thomas Norton, *Chemical Treatise*, chapter 5, page 147.</margin> 
Besides, *Cold* leaves no smell behind it; but *Heat* is the cause of all smells. Then, to omit the fiery smell of some stones, and the sweet savor of others, and the variety of scent in *Juices*, how did it happen that silver found at *Marienberg* <note>A famous mining town in Saxony, Germany.</note> smelled like *Violets*, as 
<margin>(b) Georgius Agricola, *On the Nature of Fossils*, book 1, and *On the Origin and Causes of Subterraneans*, book 5; and Cardano, *On Subtlety*, book 5, page 186.</margin> 
*Agricola* reports? Or that all *Men* feel the unpleasant scent of *Copper* and other base metals? But mark the practice of the plain *Men* <note>Common miners or laborers.</note> when they devise to judge 
<margin>(c) Cardano, ibid., page 201.</margin> 
of a *Mine* below: they take their aim at no better mark than if, by grating two stones of the hill together, they perceive a smell of *Brimstone* <note>Sulfur.</note>, because they take this to be the *residue* of the *Metals* in their concoction <note>original: "concoction" — the process of "ripening" or purifying metals through heat within the earth.</note>.

<margin>(d) Cardano, *On Subtlety*, book 5, page 190; and G. Agricola, *On Ancient and New Metals*, book 1; and Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo, *Book on the Generation of Metals*; and Sendivogius, *The New Light of Alchemy*, Treatise 5; and Augurelli, *Chrysopoeia*, book 1.</margin> 
To be short, do but consider with yourselves why there are no *Metals* except in *Rocks* and *Mountains*, unless these unload them and shoot them down into the *Plain*; and then, why there are chiefly foul *Metals* in *Cold* regions, and fine *Silver* and *Gold*, besides *Precious Stones*, in *Hot Countries*. You shall find the cause of this to be 
<margin>(e) Cardano, *On Subtlety*, book 5, pages 190, 191.</margin> 
the difference of that purging and refining *Heat*, and the closeness of the *Place* to keep in that heavenly heat, and the barrenness and lack of *Plants* to draw it forth and expend it.

Some people cannot conceive how *heat* should create this matter when they do not feel *heat* in the mine. I will not say to such people that this *heat* is very <note>original: "mild and gentle"</note> temperate everywhere, and especially there; instead, I bid them bring up a piece of *mineral* earth and lay it in the open *air*. They shall feel, if they lay their hand upon it, <margin>(g)</margin> no small heat, but a burning *heat* stirred up and raised by the cold breeze—just as the hidden heat of *lime* <note>Quicklime reacts exothermically (releases heat) when touching water.</note> is stirred up with *water*.

<margin>(h) Richardus Anglicus, *The Corrector*, Chapter 5, *Theatrum Chemicum*, volume 2, page 421; and Philippus Musenius, *Institutions for this Perfect Contemplation*, part 4, section 3, chapter 5, page 333.</margin>
Therefore, we may safely conclude <margin>(h)</margin> and rely upon the fact that all *minerals* are made with *heat*, and receive their existence and perfection from it; even though the outward shape and the final "cover" (as it were) of the work is applied by *cold*.

Now, regarding the stages and degrees of *metals*: all of them—except *iron* and *copper*, though some writers do not even except those—arise from the stages and degrees of "baking" <note>original: "baking"; refers to the alchemical process of coction or slow heating within the earth.</note> the very same substance and material of *quicksilver* <note>mercury</note>. This is evident in *lead mines*, where, according to the reports of reliable *authors*, some *gold* and *silver* are almost always found.

<margin>(i) Petrus Bonus of Ferrara, *The Precious New Pearl*, Chapter 10, *Theatrum Chemicum*, volume 5, page 804.</margin>
And therefore, <margin>(i)</margin> *Albert* <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a philosopher and alchemist.</note> says that skilled *miners* in such cases are known to shut the *mine* up again for thirty or forty years to allow the *lead* to "bake" better and progress toward perfection. He notes that this was found to be true in his time in *Slavonia* <note>A historical region in modern-day Croatia.</note>.

<margin>(k) Pliny, *Natural History*, Book 33, Chapter 6; and Georgius Agricola, *On the Nature of Fossils*, Book 9, beginning.</margin>
<margin>(l) Michael Maier, *Book on the Squaring of the Circle*, Chapter 5.</margin>

Again, how does it happen that simple <margin>(l)</margin> *craftsmen* <note>original: "Artificers"</note> can extract some *gold* and *silver* out of every *metal*, and some base *metal* out of those, unless *gold* and *silver* were the heart and best part of the...

### Chapter IV. The Way to Bliss.

<margin>(m) *Paracelsus, Book on the Generation of Natural Things, Book 2, Volume 6, page 207.*</margin>
...the whole body, and of the very same substance as the *metals*? Indeed, (*m*) *Paracelsus* <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</note> claims that not only these, but even *mines* of *middle-minerals* <note>original: "Middle-Minerals"; substances like antimony or sulfur that were categorized between "true" metals and non-metallic earths.</note>—things even further removed from perfection, as you know—are never without some *silver* or *gold*. Therefore, he advises us to water them, as if they were *plants*, with their own mineral essence and natural water; he assures us that they will grow to ripeness and, in a few years, prove as rich as any *silver* or *gold mine*.

<margin>(n) *Artephius, The Major Key of Wisdom, chapter 1, Theatrum Chemicum volume 4, page 225; and Ramon Llull, Theory of the Testament, chapter 12, page 28.*</margin>
Thus we see at last that the truth of this *metallic foundation* remains unshaken. It stands firm against the attacks of the strongest *Greeks* <note>original: "Gracians"; likely referring to Aristotelian philosophers or classical skeptics of alchemy.</note>: that (*n*) *all metals possess only one kind of mercury, substance, type, and nature. They are all the exact same thing, differing only by degrees of cleanliness, fineness, density, and color.* That is to say, they differ only by those external traits called "accidents" <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are qualities (like color or smell) that do not change the essential nature of a substance.</note> which arise from the various stages of boiling and *concoction* <note>original: "Concoction"; the process of "ripening" or "cooking" within the earth through natural heat.</note>. It is now time to build upon this **matter** and to show how these lower and unclean **metals** may be improved and changed into *silver* and *gold* to provide a way to attain *riches*.

If all *metals* are so closely related and similar to one another—especially some of those I mentioned before—lacking nothing but a continued process of cleansing and purging through *concoction*, then surely this exchange should not seem like such a difficult or impossible task. Perhaps it does not even require the help of the *divine art of Hermes* <note>The "Great Work" or high alchemy associated with Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, but a *lesser* and *simpler skill* might suffice. Just as *nature* is not poor or needy but full of abundance and variety, so may *skill*—if it observes and follows the steps of *nature*—find more than one way to reach the same **matter**. What, then, is the lower way and the lesser *skill* that follows *nature*? We shall derive it from the same path we just saw *nature* take...

<summary>The author argues that all metals share a single essential nature and differ only in their purity and "ripeness," suggesting that transmutation is a natural process that can be replicated through alchemical skill.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Artephius, Ramon Llull, alchemy, transmutation, mercury, concoction, accidents, metallic foundation, Hermes</keywords>

As Nature in her work below used two hot *workmen* <note>referring to heat and corrosive agents</note>, so will I; and because we cannot wait for her leisure and the long time she takes for that purpose, we will match and offset her little *heats* with proportions suitable and appropriate for our time, so that we may do in forty days what she does in as many years. And this proportion is not hard to be found when we consider the difference and space that lies between the founder’s *fire* and the gentle *heat* of *heaven*. And again, consider the difference between such a scouring purger—that *eater* above, consuming *stones* and *iron* so quickly—and the mild *heat* and easy *breath* that thickened the *quicksilver* <note>mercury</note>. However, we must do so with greater force and skill, even as much more as it is harder to separate the inward and inbred uncleanness than the outward and foreign scurf and foulness.

This mover is like a *Carpenter* or other *Workman* in relation to the work he creates.

Then let this part go by; and since now the *Metal* is as clean, fine, and weighty again as *Mercury* <note>original: "Quick-silver"</note>, and as solid and steadfast as *Silver*, or rather more; let us take the next point in hand, and bring on the color of *Gold*. That first part is an easy matter in the experience of common skill; but here is all the cunning: to dye the *Metal* all over with an everlasting color. For this purpose, it must be able to pierce the *Metal* and to endure all fire. That first part is not hard either, but how shall this be done? Perhaps we need not strive to make the color steadfast and abiding before we apply it; but just as *Gold* will so fast embrace and hold its flying maker, *Mercury*, if she be a little cleansed and made fit to receive him, so that no fire shall separate them, so the closeness of this our steadfast *Metal* shall defend and save the color.

A man so well-educated (but indeed not skilled in this *Art*, the chief of all *Learning*) might speak well for a great while. He might allow that all *metals* are made of the same material and travel by the same path of *concoction* <note>term: "concoction" — the alchemical process of "ripening" or "cooking" base metals into gold through heat</note> toward one end: (c) *Gold*. He might even admit that they differ only by the accident and chance of their degrees of boiling. Thereby, he yields that all the fouler *metals* may be turned into one another, and *silver* likewise into *gold*, because silver is nothing else but imperfect *gold*—the lesser part of it—wanting nothing but *color* (which is easy to provide) and a little density, which may be given to it by purging out the greasy fuel of *fire*. Yet, for all this, he denies it is possible to change any of the lower *metals* into either *silver* or *gold*. He claims that because of over-sudden *heat* (as I said of *iron* and *copper*), once they are burnt, they cannot be brought back to their original *quicksilver-like* <note>original: "Quick-silvery"</note> cleanliness, nor can they be made to endure and remain steadfast in the *fire*.

He would never have said this if he had been brought up in our school of *learning*. He should have seen us easily lead the *metals* back to the source from which they all came, and then, by the means mentioned before, stabilize them. For he himself grants that the entire cause of a metal being loose, unsteady, and wasting away in the *fire* is that fatty *brimstone* <note>term: "brimstone" — sulfur, which alchemists believed was the "combustible" principle in metals</note> within it, and he admits that this can be cleansed out of *silver*. Why not out of the rest also? Will they not endure the violence of the heat? Not at first, perhaps; but by little and little they will, as *gentle* and *wise men* know how to manage them. There are others also, as well as he—*Erastus* <note>Thomas Erastus (1524–1583), a Swiss physician and theologian who famously opposed alchemy and Paracelsus.</note> and such like—who deny this *Art of Changing*. If I thought these *men* required any effort of reproof—men who, through ignorance of the points they handle, blunder and rush into...

Might not then a tougher and harder *metal* be more easily armed and fenced against all force and violence?

So does *oil* defend *paper (g)*, to such an extent that you may boil *fish* in it without either burning the *paper* or the *oil* soaking through. All this occurs because the extreme and deadly feuds <note>The author refers to the "antipathy" or opposing qualities of elements that, paradoxically, protect the substance in the middle.</note> do save the middle thing...

To speak more directly to you: it is very well known that base and unripe *gold*—<note>original: "unripe Gold"</note> which is, as it were, a middle point between *silver* and *gold*, lacking color and density and wasting away significantly during testing and trials—may be refined by some of the lesser and lower degrees of "binding" <note>The alchemical process of fixing a volatile substance so it can endure fire.</note> and made as good as the best *gold* in the world. Though he does not name the specific means by which they made those exchanges, we may easily judge them to be those methods of binding and coloring described before. That is to say, they are lesser methods than the Medicine of **HERMES** <note>Refers to the "Philosopher's Stone" or the highest alchemical elixir attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, yet they are sufficient to serve our purpose and to generate the intended wealth. We can see this by observing their common practice, which would otherwise be empty, vain, and foolish, and also by the low cost of *middle-minerals* <note>Substances like antimony, mercury, or sulfur that were thought to sit between earth and metal.</note> compared to the return and profit of *gold*.

I might overwhelm you with more proofs and trials from equally credible *men*; but I say there is enough here to cause you to pause your judgment for a while. Let us go forward.

## CHAPTER VI.

## Of WISDOM and VIRTUE.

Since *long life*, *health*, *youth*, and *riches* have now been addressed, and we have acquired such a magnificent choir of helps, instruments, and means toward *wisdom* and *virtue*—that is, toward perfect BLISS and HAPPINESS—what is lacking but the *will* and diligence to bring all *men* to it? This is certain, unless there are some—as indeed there are many—who are so wicked and foolish by *birth* and *nature* that their human distinction is erased. Such people are so far estranged from their own *humankind* and reduced to the nature of *beasts* that, although they do not lack those helps and provisions, nor even good will and endeavor

# Chapter V. The Way to Bliss.

...knowledge of Divine and Human things; the former containing all Minds and Bodies, and the latter the matters and affairs of private men, families, and commonwealths. It will be very hard, indeed, to bring a fool to be able to understand all these matters: but let us march on, for we have passed greater dangers.

And if in this discourse of the Mind (as well as in the former regarding the soul, and some others), I call in again the best *Philosophers* and make them bear the brunt of the argument, I hope you will not blame me. This course has always been blameless and allowed in matters of such weight, both so that the *Truth* might be better sifted out <note>original: "bolted out," a metaphor from milling flour</note>, and the *Man* protected against the shots of *Envy*.

Then, just as the *Sun*—and do not think it strange if I am constantly driven to use *analogies* <note>original: "Likes"</note>, because it is the clearest way of delivering divine things, in which you see me immersed; for just as the *eye* can $(t)$ behold all things except itself and the *sun*, and those it cannot see except in another thing fit to represent the image; $(u)$ even so the *Mind* cannot understand itself, nor yet other divine matters, as well as it can through a likeness and comparison. As the *Sun*, I say, of himself always shows and sees all things, if his beams are not stopped by a cloud or some other thick obstruction; even so, the *Mind* alone, and before she falls into the cloud of the *Body*, is always active and likewise knows all things, as belongs to such a divine thing. <margin>(w) *Regarding the five inconveniences that matter brings to the human mind, see Fortunio Liceti, On Life, Book 2, Chapter 17.*</margin> But now that $(w)$ she is entangled and so darkened in this manner, she is sometimes idle, and never sees all things—indeed, she sees nothing at all without the permission and help of the *Body*.

# Chapter V. The Way to Bliss. 145

The outward *spirits* <note>original: "Spirits"; in this context, these are "animal spirits," thought to be subtle fluids that carried sensory information through the nerves</note> that sit at the edge and border of the body serve as messengers to receive—by means of the instruments and parts where they reside—and bring in tidings; that is, the appearances and shapes of things. Then, the inward beams sitting in the *brain* take those same tidings and represent them as if in a mirror before the soul, so that she may cast her light (which they call the "passive" or "receiving" *mind*) upon them and see them.

To skip over the well-known *five senses*: those inward *wits* <note>original: "Wits"; here meaning mental faculties or senses</note> and *spirits* which we have (not unfitly) compared to a *mirror* are divided into various and several seats and offices. First, <margin>(x) Almost the same words, word for word, in *Hebrew Physics*, chapter 2, page 11.</margin> (x) one sort called **Thought** <note>original: "Thought"; referring here to the "Phantasy" or "Imagination," the faculty that forms mental images</note>, inhabiting the forepart of the *brain*, takes hold of and represents the shapes let in at the **windows** of the *five outward senses*. 

Then, another crew which we call **Remembrance**, occupying the hinder part of the *head*, continually receives these shapes in great plenty and lays them up as if in a storehouse. This continues until the third company of the soul’s spirits, called **Common Sense** <note>original: "Common sense"; the faculty that integrates data from all five senses into a single perception</note>, sitting in the middle of the *brain* (as becomes a **judge**), calls for them to examine them and determine what they are—though this lower **judge** hears present matters in *Thought* as well. And then at last, the great and chief **justice**, called **Understanding**, by laying things together and gathering one truth from another, judges all.

But where is the seat of the chief **judge**? That is a question among the learned; yet I take it to be no question at all if they all grant that the *soul*—following the pattern of her *father*, the <margin>(y) Macrobius, *Commentary on the Dream of Scipio*, book 1, chapter 20.</margin> (y) **Sun** in the great *world* <note>original: "great World"; the Macrocosm or the universe</note>—dwells in

<margin>Archangelus of Borgonovo, Minorite, *Commentary on Cabalistic Dogmas*, dogma 20; and Gerard Dorn, *The Physics of Hermes Trismegistus*, volume 1, page 406; and Fortunio Liceti, *On the Origin of the Human Soul*, book 1, chapter 15; and Robert Fludd, *History of the Microcosm*, section 1, book 8, chapter 8.</margin>

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

<margin>(z) Hippocrates, *On Principles*, and Caelius Rhodiginus, *Ancient Readings*, book 15, chapter 23.</margin> The Heart is the (z) middle of the Body, so that by casting her beams all around, and equally to all parts, she might give light and life equally to all, being at an equal distance from all. And in the midst of the Heart—as the only immovable part, and therefore the only fit part of the body to move others—the Mind must also reside. <margin>(a) Philo Judaeus, *On the Creation of the World*.</margin> For then surely the Mind, being the inward kernel (as *Plato* says) (a) of the other two—the *Soul* and the *Spirit*—must necessarily rest and be rooted there as well.

Seeing then that the *Mind* sees and knows nothing except by means of the *Soul* and its inward *senses* <note>original: "Wits"</note> and *spirits*; and these see nothing except by the help of the outward ones, called the *five senses* <note>original: "five Wits"</note> or *Messengers*; and neither can function without <margin>(b) See Pierre Palmier, *Dogmas of the Philosophical Stone*, or Libavius Restored, chapter 15, page 88.</margin> the physical parts where they lodge and rest; then, (b) just as the parts of the *Body* are affected and disposed, so does the *Mind* understand. Let us look more particularly at the matter and see what condition or disposition of the *Body* helps or hinders this work of *Understanding*.

After the *Five Senses* and *Messengers* have received and delivered their tidings to the threefold *Mirror* <note>original: "Glasse"; referring to the internal faculties of the brain</note> within the *Brain*, this mirror—by stirring and running up and down—presents and musters these images before the *Mind*. She, by casting her light and view upon them, judges and determines what they are. From this, we may easily gather two things necessary for *Wisdom* and good *Understanding*: first, such a *Mirror*, or such inward *spirits*, as are able to receive and hold many imprinted shapes. That is to say, they must be very clear, clean, and smooth *spirits*, following the example of an *Eye* (that natural *Mirror*) or an artificial one, which will easily receive and show every little spot, shape, and fashion set upon it; whereas, when they are dark, foul,

Secondly, these *Spirits* <note>original: "Spirits"; in this context, the "animal spirits" thought to be the fluid or gas that carried signals through the nervous system</note> must be quick and lively—that is, hot—to be able, by their swift running to and fro, to represent and show everything quickly and easily; for the *Mind* performs all its functions by matching and laying things together.

That *Heat* is the cause of the *Quickness* and stirring of the *Spirits* appears in *Sickness*, *Age*, and deep *Sleep*—especially in *Age* and *Sickness*, more clearly than requires any further instruction. But how is this so in *Sleep*? When the heat of the *Spirits* serving the *Wit* <note>original: "Wit"; here meaning the intellect or cognitive faculty</note> is either burdened with the clogging *Fumes* and *Breaths* of the *Stomach*, or spent through *Labor* or *Sweat*, and remains still (for *Rest* abates *Heat*, as I have always said); or else if that heat is lent for a time unto its fellow-servants, the *Spirits* of *Life* <note>original: "Spirits of Life"; the "vital spirits" responsible for involuntary functions like digestion and heartbeat</note> for the sake of digestion, then the *Spirits* of the *Brain* become still and quiet. Consequently, the outward and inward *senses*, *Wit*, and *Understanding* all cease at once. 

But if the *Food* (to set aside the loss of *Heat*) was neither too much, nor of a heavy and clogging kind—and so neither breathing out heavy matter nor needing outside help to digest it—then our perceiving *Spirits* begin to take their own *Natural Heat* back unto themselves. They move a little before the *Mind*, by which she beholds some old shapes and appearances of things as they pass by, which is called *Dreaming*. But in the event that they recover all of that *Heat*, they bestir themselves quickly, running to the outside of the *Body* and bringing back new news to the *Mind*; when she perceives this, it is called *Waking*.

Therefore, the cause of *Wisdom* is clear at last, as we see: namely, a clean and stirring *Glass* <note>The author uses the metaphor of a mirror or "glass" to describe how the mind reflects reality</note>; and the cause of *Folly* is when that same glass is foul and still. If the *Glass* be fouled all over...

Moreover, it causes natural or voluntary *folly*, as seen in *fools*, *children*, and *drunkards*. But if it be only here and there smeared and drawn, as if with dark strokes and lines of foul humors <note>original: "humors"; in medieval medicine, these were bodily fluids thought to dictate health and temperament</note>, the shapes appear in the *mind* just as forms in a broken *glass* <note>original: "glasse"; meaning a mirror</note> appear to the *eye*: in halves and confusedly, and this creates *madness*.

But how did the *spirits* <note>original: "spirits"; referring to "animal spirits," the refined substances then believed to transmit sensation and thought through the nerves</note> of this inward *mirror* become so foul and sluggish, when they are by nature (as befits the beams of a *heavenly soul*) very clean, clear, fast, and lively? (We need say no more than *clear* and *foul* alone, since these two qualities make or mar the entire process of perception; for if the *spirits* are *clear*, it is a sign they are in their own *nature*, and thus hot and fast as well. Neither is that ethereal thing—which is called by the name of a *spirit*, which carries the *soul* and all its beams down into the *body*, and acts as a broker (as I said (c) above) between them—foul or still by itself. For *spirits* are not, as some *physicians* <note>original: "leaches"; an archaic term for doctors</note> think, made of the breaths of our *food*, but are merely fed by them; they are instead very fine, clear, and lively, as all *men* agree regarding *ether* <note>original: "Æther"; the rarefied element believed to fill the upper regions of space</note>. How then? Must it not necessarily follow that the entire cause of failure and deficiency in this case springs from the *body*, and especially from that part where the *senses* <note>original: "wits"</note> inhabit? If the naked *reason* brought in above will not serve to satisfy this matter, let us lead it forth clothed with the proof of *eyesight* and *experience*: the plainest, greatest, most fulfilling, and satisfying *reason* in the *world*.

Now, just as air and fire are clear and quick, while earth and water are foul and slow, so are the living creatures <note>original: "Wights"</note> affected in both mind and body where those elements predominate. (e) As Aristotle well notes, it is because outward heat acts as a cleanser; it dries and clears the bodies. On the other hand, cold binds and thickens. By preventing the escape of coarse, foul, and watery humors <note>The "humors" were the four fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to determine health and temperament.</note> and waste, cold makes everything not only dark and cloudy, but also hot and moist, as if the body were drunken by "boiling together," as (e) Aristotle terms it.

By this you may see, if you consider well, that a *Beast*, existing in a lower kind of mixture, can in no case be improved and made a *Man* unless his temperament be destroyed first and made anew, and so his life and being extinguished and erased. 

However, a foolish *Man* has no such limitation. This is because of his *Divine Mind* (though it be eclipsed by the shadow of an earthly *Body*) and because his temperament is a degree above a *Beast*. In the state and condition of *Mankind*, fire abounds in him—as his upright shape declares—just as much as in other *Men*, though perhaps not in the same exact point and measure. 

And what is the cause of his folly? It is not because *Nature* intended it to be so, but because she was obstructed and hindered by some contrary thing laid in her way within the physical matter. Because of this, she was driven to stray, miss, and fall short of her purpose. It is just like the *Mole*, as (l) *Aristotle* <margin>(l) <note>original: Dʳ Hist. Because the mole has all the parts of an *Eye* whole and perfect, it is a sign that <gloss>Nature</gloss> intended to have gone forward, but was obstructed by the barrier of a gross and thick *Skin*.

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

Now then, we see the failures and errors of Nature <note>original: "Kinde"</note> daily corrected by *Skill*. Indeed, some hold the opinion that even the blemish of a *Mole* <note>likely referring to a birthmark or a physical deformity</note> may be washed out and mended as well. Therefore, we may consider it possible to do the same for the fault of *Folly*. In fact, we might think this easier than some other cures, because folly is not a distinct category or an entire species—as if *Nature* had run this race on purpose, which seems to be the case with the *Mole*. Instead, folly appears in some odd and rare examples, acting as *Monsters* <note>term: "Monsters" refers to biological anomalies or things that deviate from the natural order</note> within their kind; or, more accurately, as *Diseases* left by *Nature*, *Descent*, and *Inheritance*, springing from some ill temperament in the parents' seed.

But how may this *Disease* be cured? All things in nature, by the course of *Nature*, have both their highest and deepest points and ends—their *South* and *North* turns, as it were—from which they always return and go back again to avoid infinity. So these natural and inherited *Diseases* have their own race, which they run and exhaust little by little. When that race is finished and the entire stock of corruption is spent (which usually happens within nine or ten generations of offspring), then they improve and return to health again. Such is the race of *Wisdom* also, and of all health of the *Body* (for the health of the *Mind* is enclosed within the health of the body), as we see by the *Children* whom *Wise men* father, and so forth; the case is plain and easy.

Furthermore, we see in this matter how *Nature* inclines and is ready to help herself. If *Art* <note>term: "Art" refers to human craft, science, or alchemy</note> would lend a hand, we may believe the cure would be much more speedy, and many portions of the time would be cut off and reduced. Just as we find this accomplished in *sores* and other lighter inward hurts by the slight means of slender skill, so we may judge that by more mighty means, more great and mighty

deeds may be performed. But why do I beat around the bush regarding this matter, when it has been—as I believe—sufficiently proved above? All remaining *leprosies* <note>original: "Leprosies"; used here to mean any chronic, corrupting skin or systemic disease</note> and other *natural diseases* of the *body* may be quite cleansed and driven away by those *heavenly* and *mineral medicines* (which I call the *panaceas* <note>original: "Cure-alls"</note> and the *great-cures* <note>original: "Cure-the-greats"</note>). This condition <note>referring to "idiocy" or lack of wit mentioned on the previous page</note> is among the number of *remaining* and *natural diseases*, all springing from an ill temperament of the *genetic seeds* <note>original: "seeds of Parents"</note> of the parents. 

To omit the rest: if *leprosy*, flowing from the foulness of the *blood* of the entire *body*, may be cured, then much more can this condition be cured, which proceeds from the ill-frame of one part only—that is, from a muddy *brain*. Or, if that *disease* <note>leprosy</note> may be said to come from one part alone—the *liver*, because it is the maker of all *blood*—yet that one is a most dangerous part if it be ill-affected. This is because, by the necessity of *nature*, the liver sends blood to all places, and so reaches through the whole body and strikes everything by contagion. Whereas the *brain*, like other organs, keeps itself within its own bounds and stretches no further.

But let us go further: if a person of good and fine *temperament* <note>original: "Temper"; the balance of bodily humors</note>, through ill *diet* and passions of the *soul*, has often fallen from a good *intellect* <note>original: "Wit"</note> to a kind of *madness*—scarcely to be distinguished from the state of an *idiot*—then surely, through the contrary cause, a foul physical frame may be cleared and rise to *wisdom*. This stands to reason, especially if those contrary *passions* and *diet* are helped and set forward by suitable *medicines*. The *Greeks* <note>original: "Gracians"</note> know and teach of these, and with them they make great changes in *men's bodies*. But without all doubt and question, if our most fine, clear, and hot *Egyptian panacea* <note>original: "Egyptian Cure-all"; likely a reference to an alchemical or hermetic elixir</note> were used to help the mat-

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

For, if the mightier *enemy* shall overcome the weaker in a fight (as you all grant, and upon which principle your *medicine* <note>original: "Phyſick"</note> stands), then shall this surpassing fineness and clarity—when it arises in the *body* like the *sun* in the *morning*—scatter and put to flight all *mists* and *darkness*. It shall mightily clear and scour every part of the *body* by its matchless heat, strength, and swiftness.

Neither shall you say that *life* will not endure such violent and forceful dealing, since *life* itself shall be the actor. For what is it that first made and mingled the foolish <note>original: "fond"; meaning trivial or physically weak</note> *body* but a beam of *heavenly fire* carried on a *couch* of *ether*? And what is this our *heavenly medicine* but the same substance, as has been shown at length above? Therefore, let us join like to like and strength to strength. If the life-force was previously too weak to break down and finely mingle the weak body as it desired, now both together—one helping the other and constantly renewing the *battle* with fresh supplies—shall, I believe, be able to overcome the task. At last, they shall bring it to the desired conclusion, state, and perfection.

If you retreat to your last defense and excuse, saying that the time has now passed, and the opportunity of *place* and *material* <note>original: "Stuffe"</note> is now lost and slipped away—claiming it is too difficult for *nature* to work such transformations upon such stubborn *material* and in such a difficult *place*—consider her ordinary course in all things. You shall see that she is able to, and daily does, rule, regulate, and frame very gross and unsuitable *material* in what we would think are the most unfit *places*. Indeed, she does much more than what is required in this *work*. 

Nature does this not only with the thick and sturdy *material* of *minerals*, cleansing the *rocks* (though in hidden places) down to the bowels of the *ground*, and with that gross and crude matter at the bottom of the *sea* to create *shellfish*; but also...

<summary>The author argues that the "Heavenly Medicine" acts as a concentrated form of life-force that can refine the human body, just as nature refines minerals in the earth and creatures in the sea.</summary>

# Chapter V. The Way to Bliss.

There are also living, moving, and perceiving *land-creatures* <note>original: "Land-Wights"</note> in the closed *rocks* (as you heard before), and in the cold *snow*, and burning *fire*, such as those *worms* and *flies* mentioned in *Aristotle*.

To conclude everything and end this matter at once: remember how this our *Heavenly Cure-all* <note>The author refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone as a universal medicine.</note>, when it was sent into the *body* to produce *long life*, *health*, and *vigor* <note>original: "Lustiness"</note>, did not only strike, kill, and extinguish all foul and gross imbalances—which are its enemies and ours—but also cherished, nourished, and fed our *bodies*. It brought the body toward our own ideal *nature* (as far as we desired, depending on the dosage). You may easily conceive the plain and certain way of this great transformation once you understand its most clean, fine, clear, bright, and luminous *nature*.

<margin>(m) Virtue, then, is a state of character concerned with choice, lying in a mean relative to us, determined by a rational principle, and by that principle by which the person of practical wisdom would determine it. *Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, Book 2, Chapter 6.*</margin>

Now that we have finished the first part of BLISS, let us go to the second. Because we have not done so before, though we talked much about it, we will now begin to define the *subject matter* and define *virtue* (as (*m*) *Aristotle* and *Truth* teach us) as: *A middle path in our outward deeds and dealings with other men.* Or, as *Plato* terms it, *A rational principle in manners and character*; it is all the same. The cause of *virtue* was likewise identified at the beginning—namely, *a temperate body*. However, I left the *proof* for this section, which contains all the difficulty in this cure for *wickedness* <note>original: "Lewdness," here meaning general moral vice or ignorance rather than specifically sexual misconduct.</note>. For, if it is once known that *temperance* is the cause of *virtue*, we shall easily obtain and procure virtue by using that temperate *medicine* so notable in the preceding discourse. As for why that is so, the argument has been used so often before that we may set it aside; it is enough, in this place, to prove that a temperate state of *body* is the cause of and the way to *virtue*.

<summary>The author concludes that life exists in all elements and argues that a "temperate body," achieved through a heavenly medicine, is the essential foundation for moral virtue and "Bliss."</summary>

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK II.

But first, let us see whether all *manners* <note>original: "Manners"; here meaning character, behavior, or moral conduct</note> flow from the *body* or not, and then from what *state* and *condition* of the *body* they arise. Among those who have searched for the *reason* and *nature* of things, the cause of *manners* is attributed either to the *disposition* of the *stars* <note>astrological influence</note>, or of *men's bodies*, or of their *wills*, which are shaped either by the inclination of *nature* or by the force of *custom*. 

Let us examine the matter, yet briefly. These behaviors cannot flow solely from the *will* of the *mind* of *man*, otherwise all *men* would necessarily be good—which contradicts our daily proof and experience. This is because the *mind* itself, originating from goodness, is inherently good and equally good in all *men*, as I stated previously. Certainly, no *custom* could alter and turn such a divine and righteous *will* toward wickedness, except by a great force of *necessity*. Such force cannot be imposed upon the mind by the *stars*; for whether the *stars* are *living beings* <note>original: "Wights"</note> or not, they are all (as I showed above) of one good <margin>(n) Plotinus, *Enneads*, Book 2, Chapter 9, Section 13, page 212; and Cornelius Agrippa, *Of Occult Philosophy*, Book 3, Chapter 39, page 417.</margin> strain and quality.

Or, if they were not—or whatever they may be in either substance or quality—they cannot touch the *mind* directly. They must instead be let in through the "loops" <note>openings or pathways</note> of the *body*, and so change and dispose the *body* first; by means of this, they affect the *mind*. For if the *mind* itself, being a finer substance than the *stars*, cannot pierce out of the *body* (as we heard before), then much less shall the stars find a way to get in by themselves without the helps allotted to our *mind*. And as these are all bodily (I mean the primary helps), so the nearest cause of *manners* must necessarily flow from the *body*. 

Furthermore, if the inward *spirits* and *intellect* <note>original: "Wits"</note> likewise do nothing without the instruments of the *body*, and follow the affection and disposition of the same, then the appetite of the irrational *soul*—which we have in common with beasts (upon which *Aristotle*... <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword indicates the next page begins with "Aristotle"</note>

<summary>The author argues that human character and behavior (manners) are primarily determined by the physical state of the body rather than the stars or the inherent will of the mind, which is divine and good.</summary>

# Chapter V. The Way to Bliss.

...Aristotle and his heirs do lay the cause of *Manners* <note>original: "Manners"; here meaning character, morality, and behavioral habits</note>) is dispatched also, and all the whole strain must needs clearly run from the *Body*.

But, lest some *All-denier* <note>A skeptic or someone who rejects established truths</note> come and shake these old *Grounds* <note>Foundations</note>, which you saw the *Philosophers* lay so long ago, and so this *Building* might fall and tumble, I will shore it up with *Experience*, a thing most fit to fill and please the senses of those who have nothing else but sense.

As all *Diseases*, so all *Manners* spring either from the natural and inherited, or from the purchased <note>Acquired during one's lifetime</note> temperament of the *Body*. To keep the first until later: this we have either from the *Air* and *Soil* where we live, or from the *Food* <note>original: "Meat"; used here as a general term for all sustenance</note> which we take. The *Air* follows either the position of the *Sun* or the *Nature* of the *Ground*. But this is somewhat too hard and thorny a kind of teaching; let us enlarge our perspective and unfold and prove how (though I showed the method at length before) the *Air* and *Food* alter and change, and cause to differ, the *Bodies* first, and consequently the *Manners*.

All (o) *Astronomers* and *Philosophers* (no differently than we see by proof) hold the opinion that where the *Sun* is either too near the *People*, as directly over them, or too far off, as under the two *Pins of the World* <note>The North and South Poles</note>, there the *Bodies* are big and strong, (p) and the *Manners* rude and fierce. Whereas within the two middle and temperate *Zones* <note>original: "Girdles"</note> *of the Earth*, they keep a middle ground and hit the mark, as they say, both in *Body* and *Manners*, because of their balanced relationship with the sun and his fellow-wanderers <note>The planets</note>.

To come down to the basic facts (for I must be brief), we see that a fat and (q) foggy <term>original: "foggy" — here meaning damp, marshy, or thick-aired</term> *land* makes the *blood* and *spirits* thick and gross. This makes men dull and slow, and so they become foolish in *wit*, rude, and simple, yet also faithful, chaste, and honest, remaining steady in those *manners*. On the other hand, a barren and dry *ground*—if the *sun* is temperate there (as at *Rome* and *Athens*) (r)—makes the blood thin, clear, and lively. This produces subtle and deceitful *men* who are valiant, unchaste, and so forth, regarding all other related properties. For the appropriate *manners* in *men* are like the *virtues* and *properties* of *plants*: they follow the various temperaments of the *bodies*, even though the *soul* in plants and the *mind* in us is essentially the same in everyone. 

Then, just as the mixtures, qualities, and virtues of *plants* are altered according to their food and sustenance—to omit the outward nourishment of the *ground*, by which (f) *pepper* brought out of *Calicut* <note>A major port city in India</note> into *Italy* will, after a few plantings, turn into *ivy* and such—the case is plain. A cunning *gardener*, either by steeping the *seed* or *slip*, or better yet by enclosing the *root* or *stock*, can give to any *plant* any color, taste, smell, or power of *healing*. In the very same way, the temperament of *men's bodies* and the *condition* of their *manners* change back and forth based on the same influences.

Therefore, to dismiss the *astronomers'* opinions as a discordant theory, we must recognize that those *manners* spring out of the *(c) parents' seed*, which is a part of their *bodies*. I mean their "second bodies," acquired through *food* and *nourishment*. If these *bodies* use a good and temperate *diet*, they remain like the original; otherwise, they follow the *nature* of the *foods* and their imbalances. As *Cardano (d)* <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), an influential Italian polymath.</note> has most notably observed regarding a few of the worst *diets*: parents who are drunken, or over-studious, or who fast too much, or who eat too many onions, for the most part beget and bring forth mad and frantic *children*.

To conclude this *first part* with one final proof: if we find ourselves doing many things against our *wills*—as when something fearful is presented, our *hearts* will pant and fail with fear; or when something beautiful appears, *lust* and its impulses will arise whether we wish it or not (and all lack of self-control springs from that *root*)—then surely the *body* must exert this force upon us. 

But how is this? In what way does the *body* so violently overrule and carry away the *will* and *mind*? When any image appears in the thought of *man*, the active *mind* perceives it immediately (we must become accustomed to these terms to understand them better) and, weighing it as good or bad and comparing all things, it makes a decree and determination. Then his *will* and *reason*, which *Plato* places in the *head*, follow and desire it. But at the same time, another double *will* and appetite steps in, sent from that *unreasonable* and perceiving *soul* which we have in common with *beasts*. And whichever of these is stronger—that is, whichever has the stronger house, either by natural descent or by habit <note>original: "purchase"</note> (otherwise the baser part would always be the weaker and obey the better)—that part prevails and moves the *Spirits* <note>In early medicine, "spirits" were subtle fluids that carried commands from the soul to the body.</note> toward it. Therefore, to come to the point more plainly, we shall never be good and follow *Virtue*—which is a middle ground and the use of reason in our desires and actions—until these two parts, the *Heart* and the *Liver*, are first by Nature <note>original: "Kind"</note>, and then by Diet, kept in order, balance, and temperament, so that they are fit to obey the Laws and Rules of *Reason*. For to begin with the Root: if the *Heart* is very Hot and Moist, the *Man* is Courageous and Liberal, desiring Honor and great outward things. If it is Hot and Dry, he is Cruel, Angry, Deceitful, and so on. But if it keeps a middle ground and is Temperate in Quality, it keeps a middle ground and obeys *Reason* in those types of *Behaviors* <note>original: "Manners"</note>.

<margin>Plato in the *Timaeus*.</margin>
As for the *Liver*, if it is likewise Hot and Moist, it pursues Lust <note>original: "Venery"</note> and Gluttony. If it is Hot and Dry, it does the same, but in a twisted and erratic way. But if it is Cold and Dry, the *Man* on the other hand is very Chaste and Abstinent; and if Cold and Moist, he is somewhat Chaste and Abstinent, but in an unhelpful or awkward way. Whereas a Temperate *Liver* holds a middle ground in both; following the course of *Nature*, such a person desires to live Soberly in Society and Honestly in Marriage—a Life as far removed from *Monks* and *Hermits* <note>original: "Eremites"</note> as it is from *Gluttons* and *Lechers*.

Therefore, we see that all Behaviors proceed from the Temperament of those parts (and perhaps *Understanding* also,

The mind also varies according to the different *heats* and *moistures* of the *brain*. If these two parts are the springs of all *heat* and *moisture* in the *body*, then it follows that all good *manners* and all *virtue* bud forth from the good, middle, and equal mixture and temperament of these same parts. All our labor and travel (if we seek *virtue*) must be to bring those two into balance and temperament—that is, an equality, as near as may be, of the four qualities. This is achieved not only by the *philosophical* remedy of *use* and *custom*—though (g) *Plato* hits the mark in his *Timaeus* when he argues that no man is wicked by his own *will*, and therefore should not be blamed, but rather becomes so through his body being ill-disposed by *habit* or *nature*—but rather by good *diet*, and especially by right *medicine* <note>original: "Phyisck"</note>.

And thus we have, at last, finished these parts, wherein we meant to prepare the *minds* of both the *common* and *learned* people, and to make the way to the **Truth of HERMES' MEDICINE.** <note>Hermes' Medicine refers to the "Hermetic" or alchemical tradition of healing, named after Hermes Trismegistus.</note>

purpose) than to be traveled and gone through by the lovers of *Wisdom* and *Virtue*. But how shall we prove this, unless we unlock the door of *Secrets* and let light into these matters, which have always been most closely kept and hidden in darkness? We must, I say, first reveal what HERMES' MEDICINE is, unless we would put on a *mask* <note>original: "Vizard"</note> and make a long buzz and empty sound of words about that which no man understands.

We are now likely to be driven into a marvelous strait: either to flee the field or to venture upon the curse and displeasure of many wise and godly *Men*—yes, and of GOD HIMSELF, as we heard in the beginning.

It is strange, but I am persuaded that a thousand ounces of *Plant* or *Living Creature* <note>original: "Wight"; referring to organic or animal matter</note> (as for (e) *Minerals*, they are much better) cannot—even with great labor, cost, skill, and time—be brought to that goodness and nearness to perfection which one ounce of *Gold* has already been given by *Nature*. To leave behind living *creatures* <note>original: "Wights"</note> and turn to *plants*: are not *trees* more useful the longer they live, because of that long period of growth and ripening? And among *trees*, does not the *oak* (t), after its long growth to perfection, remain standing for our great benefit almost forever? What I say may seem strange, yet *Josephus* (u) writes of one oak tree that stood from the time of *Abraham* until the destruction of *Jerusalem*—at least two thousand years—and God knows how long it lasted after that time. To be brief, the best *tree* of all that the *earth* brings forth, the *Coconut of India* <note>original: "Cocus of India"</note> (w), scarcely begins to bear any fruit within the span of one *man's* age, yet after that, it lasts through almost all *ages*. 

Therefore, by the course of *reason* and *custom*, *minerals*—being granted by all *men* to be the longest in forming and perfecting—must necessarily be the best in *power* <note>original: "Virtue"</note> and last the longest of all things. Among them, *gold* is above all because it is the ultimate goal of all minerals. In that regard, it so far surpasses most of them that some *men* believe a thousand years are spent before it reaches perfection. As for its durability, we plainly see that it is everlasting. And if we doubt its heavenly *power*, let us consider the place and "womb" where it is bred and fashioned; we shall see it is a (x) common gulf or receiver of all the beams of *heaven*, just as the *sea* is the receiver of all the rivers that flow.

How is this? All the beams of *Heaven* set forth from a round and wide compass, and like *lines in a circle*, after they have traveled a great wide way away from one another, they finally meet together exactly (y) in the *navel of the earth* <note>The center or core of the world.</note>. Indeed, they meet with great force and strength above all other *spirits* in other places—not only because of the length of their journey (for in all natural things, the (z) further they travel, the more they increase their pace), but chiefly because, meeting in such a narrow space and in such abundance, they violently thrust and throw one another into heaps together. We see this same force when *winds* or *water* meet in that manner; or rather, as the *Sun's* beams falling upon the stone (a) *Hephaestites* <note>A legendary precious stone, named after Hephaestus, said to reflect light like a mirror or act as a burning lens.</note>, or a steeple-shaped *burning-glass* <note>A magnifying lens used to concentrate sunlight to start fires.</note>. In contrast, *plants* and *living creatures* <note>original: "Wights"</note> dwell in one place on the outside of the ground above, where these *beams* and *breaths of heaven* are more scarce, sluggish, and weak. Consequently, they must not only have a smaller store faintly bestowed upon them, but also quickly lose and give up what they do have because of their loose and soft substance.

A few exceptions are made of *copperas water* <note>original: "Copperess Water"; water containing iron or copper sulfates</note>, as is evident by their dyeing property, by which they give any white *metal* their own yellow and *copper* color.

Now, concerning *metals*: if it is true that precious *stones*, despite their hard and unyielding nature, show such virtue and power of healing, why should we label the *German* <note>referring to Paracelsus</note> a *liar*? He calls this mixture *Electrum*, saying it will cure *cramps*, *numbness*, *palsy*, and the *falling sickness* if it is worn on the ring finger <note>original: "hearty finger"</note>. He also claims it provides signs if the *body* suffers from any ailment by producing *spots* and *sweat*. Furthermore, it reveals *poison* if it is fashioned into *plate* <note>tableware</note> by those same signs. This is despite the fact that (*q*) *Pliny* insists *poison* is only detected by natural *Electrum* <note>a natural alloy of gold and silver</note>, which he says will wash off *gold* and *silver* and reveal poison through the appearance of *rainbows* and the hissing sound of *fire*. Pliny rejects the artificial mixture made of *silver*, *gold*, and *copper*, calling it "adulterating the adulterations of nature" <note>original: "adulterando adulteria naturæ"</note>. This was a remark more clever than consistent, considering he had previously attributed such pure *virtue* to the substance.

(x) But **Galen** did but aim blindly at the matter by guesswork, since (y) in another place, forgetting himself (as he often does), he says he had never actually tried its force, neither inside nor outside the body.

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK III.

<margin>
(a) Andrea Bacci, *On the Nature of Gems*, chapter 17.

Therefore, to return to my purpose: if *Nature* has bestowed such large *gifts* and *virtues* upon these three sorts and suits of *minerals*, what has she given to *gold* (a), the end and perfection of them all? Gold has passed in that journey through the midst of them all (b), thereby receiving and holding the *virtues* of them all with even greater advantage. What do the *physicians* <note>original: "Leaches"</note> say to this matter? They are loath to say anything, although their deeds speak enough when they lay raw *gold* to the outside (c) of the *head* to heal its *ache*; right (e) against the *heart* to comfort its sadness and trembling; and when they apply it in such a manner to such purposes. Again, why do they boil it in their (d) *strong broths* <note>original: "Cullises," a strained meat broth used for the sick</note>? Why do they mix it with their (d) *pills* and *medicinal pastes* <note>original: "Electuaries"</note>? Why do they bid the *lepers* swallow it, and so on? Do they not seem to sense its great and matchless power against *diseases*, and its marvelous comfort and wholesomeness to our *nature*? It is only that, like rude and unskillful *cooks*, they do not know how to prepare it. 

But if they possessed the *skill*, they would see it rise in *power* and *virtue* according to its degrees of *freedom*. And when it reached the very top—which I call the *Son of Gold*—it would prove to be *almighty* (I mean within our human limits). For consider: *gold* is already good and friendly to us above all things because of its exceeding store of comfortable *heavenly heat* <note>The "Heat of Heaven" refers to the solar or celestial essence believed to be trapped within gold</note> shining through the mist of a most fine and temperate *body*. # Chapter 1. The Way to Bliss.

If the body were refined and raised in its kind at least a hundred degrees by the extensive mingling and breaking down of the material (which our *Art* <note>original: "Art"; referring to Alchemy</note> professes to do); and if those lively and piercing heaps of comfortable *spirits* <note>original: "spirits"; the vital, volatile essences within a substance</note> were freed and set at liberty, and all of these were seated upon a most mighty *Body* that subdues all things: is there anything in the *World* to be compared to the marvelous *Work* which it would perform in our *Body*? Could any of those very violent and mortal *Poisons*, which I mentioned above, destroy us as easily and completely as this would help and save us? 

But to come to the point: if our old, fine, close, and *Ethereal oil*, which they call a *Fifth Nature* <note>original: "Fifth Nature" or "Quintessence"; the purest essence of a substance beyond the four elements</note>, was able alone—for the reasons set down in their proper places—to breed and beget all those blessed bodily gifts and properties (that is, *Lastingness*, *Health*, *Youth*, and the two springs of *Wisdom* and *Virtue*, *Clarity* and *Temperance*); how much more shall this *Son of Gold*, the *Medicine* and *Stone* of HERMES <note>The Philosopher's Stone, attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</note>, and his offspring, be sufficient and equipped for it?

For first, since his *Soul* and *Heat of Heaven* is much greater and mightier, and his *Body* is a finer and more stable oil—that is, a more similar and lasting *Food of Life*—it both upholds and strengthens *Life* and *Natural Heat* better, and so proves to be the better cause of *Long Life* and *Youth*. Then, being more temperate, and having 그 quality carried upon a finer and tougher (that is, a stronger) *Body*, it is able with more ease and speed to subdue its enemies and ours: the imbalanced *Diseases*. It is able to cleanse, clear, fashion, and bring into good order and temper the whole frame of our *Body*, and so procure *Health*, *Wisdom*, and *Virtue* in a better way, and in a more full and overflowing measure. For you must not think that a

Therefore, one part of the Stone will last longer and spread further with all its *virtues* <note>original: "Virtues"; inherent powers or medicinal properties</note>, doing more good for our bodies than twenty times the amount of the former preparation. It is enough, in this place, to prove by force of reason (which has been done as much as necessary) that this Medicine is much better and more powerful than any *Ether*, *Heaven*, or *Fifth Essence*.

Then these *men* may see (I mean (f) *Paracelsus* <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist</note> and those who actually know what they are talking about—let the rest go) how rash and ill-advised they prove themselves to be when they are willing to associate the name of "Poison" with this blessed Medicine. They claim that it works all those wonders in our bodies through that method of curing which I previously described—by using stronger, similar poisons to drive out illness. If that were true, it would be, at most, only a general medicine or a "cure-all" for diseases, suitable for health alone. It would not be a blessed path to *Long Life*, *Youth*, *Wisdom*, and *Virtue*, all of which he and everyone else admit this Medicine provides. If it were a poison, it could not be taken and used in a healthy body, any more...

For such a substance would then battle with our nature, spoiling and overthrowing the primary moisture and the whole framework of the body. It would be far from nourishing our natural heat and moisture, or clearing and balancing the body to produce long life, youth, wisdom, and virtue. 

The reason for this rebuke is that while every poison is barren and devoid of the "heat of heaven" <note>original: "Heat of Heaven"; likely referring to vital celestial energy or "pneuma"</note>—being instead immoderately cold and dry in substance and directly opposed to our hot and moist nature (as shown by how it flees from fire and oil, its enemies)—the Philosophers' Stone was temperate from the beginning. Now, it is exactly so: a very fine oil, full of heavenly spirits. For these three reasons, it is not only most friendly and similar to our nature, but also a deadly enemy and the direct opposite of all poisons.

## That the Philosophers' Stone is able to turn all base metals into silver and gold.

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK III.

For the effect, we should take Gold for *Riches* and strive to show that the "Son of Gold" <note>A term for the Philosopher's Stone or the perfected alchemical substance</note> is able to turn any metal into gold. We should do this not as sparingly, slowly, and with such difficulty as we did before through those inferior methods of *Binding* and *Coloring*—though even a little success would serve our purpose if it didn't waste so much time and effort—but as fully and plentifully as any of our *Masters* <note>original: "Men"; referring to established alchemical authorities</note> claim, to the amazement of the *World*. They set down no certain sum or limit, but I will do so, because I am speaking to those eager to hear, and because I do not like to speak vaguely, but prefer to have a certain mark at which to aim and direct all my words.

<margin>
(a) Francesco Sansovino, *On the Government of Kingdoms*, Book 6, at the end.
(b) Plato in the *Phaedrus*.
</margin>

Then let us say: by this great skill of HERMES <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy</note>, and with a little *Labor* and *Expense*, we may spend as much as the greatest monarch of the *World* and match the Sultan of Turkey's revenue—even if it be fifteen million pounds sterling, as I find (a) credibly reported. Indeed, let us be bold and not hide our faces for shame of the *Subject*, as (b) Socrates did when he spoke of *Love*. The truth can be sworn to before GOD and Man and will prove itself in the end, even though it is my luck to be constantly opposed by men of our own profession, the *Foster-children of HERMES*.

But why do I call them that? Although Paracelsus <note>A famous Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541)</note>, whom we discussed recently, was clearly one, his student *Dorne* <note>Gerard Dorn, a 16th-century philosopher and editor of Paracelsus</note>, who now enters the discussion, is clearly excluded from that group. This man, I say, to excuse his own ignorance, has learned a new trick for interpreting HERMES's riddle.

He is to turn metals in two ways: first, as a seed, if a man chooses to sow him upon them; and then, after his birth, by *nourishment*, or turning them into his own nature. Of these I will treat separately: and first of seed, which cannot be denied unto gold, if all <margin>(d) William Gilbert, in *On the Magnet*, book 5, chapter 12, proves extensively that all elements and things composed of elements are alive. But because it is a thing upon which almost the entire frame of my speech leans, and yet is much in doubt and hardly believed among the learned, let us take it up again. # Chapter II. The Way to Bliss.

<margin>(g) See Francesco Giorgio of Venice, *On the Harmony of the Whole World*, Song 2, Volume 1, Chapter 6; and the *Discourses* of Mattioli in the 5th book of Dioscorides, in the preface; and Peder Sørensen the Dane, *The Idea of Philosophical Medicine*, Chapter 2; and the *Commentary on the Secret Book of the Philosophers' Stone*, Volume 4, page 759; and Tommaso Campanella, *On the Sense of Things*, Book 3, Chapter 13. (h) The same is said by Cardano, *On Subtlety*, Book 5, page 210. (i) *On Ancient and New Metals*, Book 1; and *On the Journey* by Rutilius Claudius Namatianus the Gaul, in Onofrio Panvinio's *Description of the City of Rome*, Book 1; and Georg Fabricius, *Treatise on Thermal Waters and Metals*, Chapter 5; and Georg Fabricius, *Observations on Metallic Matters*, pages 10, 12.</margin>

But what if (g) *minerals*, by feeding, increase and grow just as *plants* or *living creatures* <note>original: "wights"</note> do? *Miners* have good experience of this when they see the minerals, through those regular and constant cycles, so dangerously expel their *waste products* <note>original: "leavings"</note>. *Agricola* <note>Georgius Agricola (1494–1555), a German scholar known as the "father of mineralogy."</note> says (h) that *saltpeter*, after it has lost its taste and strength through draining, will—if it is laid out in the *open weather*—grow, ripen, and recover its power and strength again within five or six years. The (i) same man tells of one *lead mine* and two others of *iron* which, after they are dug out and emptied, ripen and grow to be full again within a few years; one of these does so every tenth year.

<margin>(k) Cardano, *On Subtlety*, Book 9, page 239; and St. Albans <note>Francis Bacon</note>, *Sylva Sylvarum*, Century 8, Experiment 97; and Georgius Agricola, *On the Origin and Causes of Subterranean Things*, Book 5. (l) Book 8, *On the Nature of Fossils*.</margin>

Even if someone, through the clever maneuvers of a crafty *wit*, might try to dismiss these examples, they shall never be able to explain away the next case concerning *lead*. After lead has been taken out of its proper *womb*—where it was bred, nourished, and fashioned into the form required for our use—if it is then laid in a moist place underground, it will increase and grow both in weight and size. This is confirmed (k) by many respected *authors*, and even by (k) *Galen's* own testimony. Although Galen's authority might be considered "light" in other contexts, it carries great weight in this matter because his evidence goes so much against his own philosophical cause. Furthermore, note what (l) *G. Agricola* reports: that the same growth has been found to occur even on the tops of houses, and he shows where and how the proof was obtained.

<margin>(m) *Book on the Generation of Natural Things*, Volume 6, page 107. See Alessandro Alessandri, *Genial Days*, Book 4, Chapter 9; and Scaliger, *Exercise* 102; and B. Fulgosus, Book 1, Chapter 6, in Simone Maiolo, *Dies Caniculares*, Colloquy 19, on metals.</margin>

But to come to the very point, (m) *Paracelsus* says that *gold* buried in good *soil* that faces *East*, and is well-nourished with *pigeon's dung* and *urine*, will do the same. Certainly, I dare not reject his testimony in this *matter*, because the other examples that came before seem to...
