Then, if it is so certain that **Gold** has **life**, there is no alternative but that it shall also beget its **like**, if **Philosophy** and common proof are accepted. But they (n) will say that nothing does so if it lacks **seed**, as many **living creatures** <note>original: "Wights"</note> and **Plants** do, and all **Minerals**. No man says so who knows what **seed** is. (o) **Seed** is no gross thing that may be seen with **eyes**, but a fine and hot **Heavenly breath**, which we call **Life** and **Soul**. With this, not only the common soul of the **World**, but also **living creatures**—yes, and even perfect (p) **living creatures** sometimes—beget offspring without the company and sensation of that frothy stuff and shell <note>The author refers to physical semen as merely a "shell" for the spiritual seed.</note>, as I said above. Yet, most commonly, **Nature** utilizes the help and protection of that body called **Seed**. This was proved to be not only a branch and part slipped from the whole body, but sometimes the whole itself—as occurs by nature in the four elements <note>original: "beginnings"</note>, in **Minerals**, and in seedless **Plants** and **living creatures**; and by **Skill** <note>i.e., Alchemy or Art</note> in all things. Therefore, **Minerals** and all things have their **Seed**, and their whole body is their **Seed**.

Then, just as by **Nature** they are wholly sown and die, and rise again increased according to the custom of **Nature** (otherwise, (q) things under the moon <note>Sublunary things, or the earthly realm subject to change and decay.</note> would prove mortal), even so they will do above ground if we can by skill use them according to their nature. We may do this as well as **Nature** can, if we could espy her footsteps—which are not impossible to see, as I could show you quickly if I might a little unwind the depths <note>original: "bottome"</note> of **secrets** and lay them open. But I must take heed.

Then, as the **seed** of **Plants** and **living creatures** rises again much increased in quantity and size—because it draws to itself and turns into its own **Nature** much of the natural stuff...

The material and ground that lies around a seed serves to corrupt it <note>In this context, "corrupt" refers to the breaking down of a seed so it can germinate.</note>. Even so, if you <margin>(r) See Aurelius Augurellus, *Chrysopoeia*, Book 2. *Theatrum Chemicum*, Volume 3, page 214.</margin> make the **metals** a ground fit to receive and corrupt the seed of **gold**, it will, after its due time, rise again, turning them—or much of them—into its own **nature**.

Now Dr. Dorne <note>Gerard Dorn (c. 1530–1584), a Belgian philosopher and defender of Paracelsus.</note> may see, if he is not blinded, that this is no matter of riddles, but a plain and certain truth. It is grounded upon the open and daily course of **nature**, which I was not the first to notice (just as he noticed the subtle falsehood). Rather, the entire troop of the wise **Egyptians** saw and taught this before me. Yes, even some of those who sit in darkness <note>Likely referring to those who have not fully embraced the "light" of alchemy.</note>, such as those worthy **physicians** <term>original: leaches</term> whose aid we cited before: **Ficino**, **Fernel**, and **Cardanus**. The first two are especially notable because they bore goodwill toward the truth of this **science**. But **Cardanus**, as a man who neither knew nor loved it, falters a little; for while he held it as certain that **minerals** and all things had life, and were nourished, and grew and increased, he buried the third point in silence.

But let us not press this point too much here, because this is not the true **Son of Gold** and **Stone** of **HERMES** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>, but a lesser skill and a lower way to **riches**, which would have been more appropriate to follow in the **Second Book**. How then does the **PHILOSOPHER'S STONE**, and the natural Son of Gold itself, turn base **metals** into **gold**? For that was the second thing to be handled in this section.

When this **child** <note>An alchemical metaphor for the Philosopher's Stone or the perfected substance.</note> is born, keep him in his heat, which is his **life**, and give him his due and natural food of **metals**. If he is alive and able to be nourished, he must necessarily digest, change, and turn them into his own **nature**. This is done much more easily than **lead** was previously able to turn strange food and digest it while in a cold place and in a crude, hard fashion.

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK III.

And as I showed above, the change of *natural things* when they meet in combat is either total or partial. That is, they either *consume* one another to erase the other's identity entirely—turning it into its own self-same *nature*—or, through mixture, the forces of both are equally broken and dulled. Even so, in this great and skillful transformation, we may order the matter and match the two combatants—that is, the *meat* and the *feeder*, the *material* and the *agent*—with such proportion that one shall either gain the *victory* and eat up the other entirely, or both will be equally weakened and altered.

<margin>(s) See Pietro Bono of Ferrara, *The Precious Pearl*, chapter 3. *Theatrum Chemicum*, Volume 5, page 679.</margin>

To be plain: if we give (s) this mighty *child* and *son of gold* <note>A common alchemical term for the Philosopher's Stone or the perfected medicinal gold.</note> only a little *food* (the exact quantity I leave to your discretion), he will be able to turn it thoroughly into his own self-same *nature*, and thereby improve himself and increase his own mass and quantity. But if you wish to make *gold*—which is your final goal and purpose—you must match your *medicine* <note>The "medicine" refers to the transformative alchemical agent or tincture.</note> with a great deal of material, perhaps a hundred times as much or thereabouts (your own eyes shall guide you). In that case, both shall work equally upon each other, and neither shall be changed completely. Instead, they will create a *middle* substance between the two, which will be *gold*, if you wish, or whatever you desire, according to your proportions. 

And if you do not yet understand, observe how (the comparison is somewhat humble, but fitting and often used by our *men* <note>Referring to fellow alchemists or "philosophers."</note>) they make a sharp and strong *medicine*, <margin>(t) Denis Zachaire, *Theatrum Chemicum*, Volume 1, page 810; and Pietro Bono, *The Precious Pearl*, chapter 9, *Theatrum Chemicum*, Volume 5, page 677.</margin> (t) called *leaven* <note>Sourdough starter or yeast.</note>, from the best processed *flour*, which is *dough*; and another such medicine from *milk*, well-mixed in a calf's stomach, named <margin>(u) Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli, *Chrysopoeia*, book 2. *Theatrum Chemicum*, Volume 3, page 216.</margin> (u) *rennet*. By matching these with the correct proportion of *flour* and *milk*, they turn them into the intermediate states of *dough* and *curds*. Nothing is more fitting than this; observe it well.

<summary>The author explains that alchemical transmutation depends on the proportion of the "medicine" to the base material, using the domestic metaphors of leaven in dough and rennet in milk to illustrate how a small amount of agent transforms a larger mass.</summary>

It is then able easily to overcome *iron*—a similar and nearly weaker thing (for what is so near as *iron* to *copper*?)—and turn it into its own old, humble, and middle nature.

# Chapter III. The Way to Bliss.

Being of the same nature still with exalted <margin>(u) And this is to make the thick rare and to lighten the heavy, by which every subtle thing is worthier than the coarse, just as every rare thing is worthier than the thick. <note>original Latin: "Et hoc est spissum rarefacere & alleviare ponderosum, ex quo omne subtile dignius est gresso, sicut omne rarum spisso." Avicenna, Theatrum Chemicum, vol. 4, p. 991.</note></margin> (u) *Lead* and *Quicksilver* <note>mercury</note>, two great softeners and looseners of hard bodies, is able to make the stubborn *metals* retire and yield into the middle state of *Lead*, and this into *Quicksilver*.

Now then we see that *Art* <note>human skill or alchemy</note> has reached and overtaken all the natural changes of *minerals*; why may she not, by the same pattern, devise more of herself—as is the manner of good *workmen*—and go beyond *Nature*, turning the foul *metals* into fine *Silver* and *Gold*? She has a great advantage over *Nature*. First, her *patterns*; then, her help in working; and lastly, the light and instruction of a *divine wit* and *understanding*. Wherefore it is no marvel if all *wise men* have said that she *surpasses Nature*.

Although it is uncertain whether *Nature* has such a *Golden Medicine* <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note> hidden in her bosom or not—as well as those medicines of *Copper*, *Stone*, and such like—yet this is sure: that by the "bastard" <note>indirect or artificial</note> way of binding (as we have heard before), she turns *Lead* and *Tin*, and perhaps *Copper* too, but surely *Quicksilver* and *Silver* into *Gold*. Then I say it is a sign of a weak and shallow *wit* if *Art* cannot, by these patterns aforesaid, devise further to turn other *metals* into *Silver* and *Gold*. Is it any more to do than to exalt and raise *Silver* and *Gold* (but this <margin>(w) See Isaac Hollandus, Mineral Works, book 1, chapter 16. Theatrum Chemicum, vol. 3, p. 312. <summary>The author argues that alchemy (Art) can surpass natural processes by using divine intellect to refine base metals into gold and silver, following the patterns observed in nature.</summary>

The reason is so plain and obvious that I must consider anyone who cannot understand it to be less than a child. Now, pay close attention: by logic, if the *workman* <note>The "workman" refers to the active medicinal agent or the Philosopher's Stone itself.</note> is significantly stronger than the *material* <note>original: "stuffe"</note>, he will, through trial, transform things that are unlike and contrary just as well as things that are similar and friendly—though perhaps not as easily.

<margin>(x) *On the Transformation of Metals, chapter 1. Theatrum Chemicum, volume 1, page 539. And Paracelsus, on the Philosophy of Heat, or the Sixth Book of the Hunt, page 291. And Andreas Libavius, Alchymia, book 2, tract 1, on the Magistery, chapter 24.*
(y) *Richard the Englishman, Corrections. Theatrum Chemicum, volume 2, page 425. And Tauladanus, Observations on Braceschus, chapter 7, page 350. And The Rosary of the Philosophers, The Golden Art, volume 2, pages 337, 338.*</margin>
There is proof of *stony juices* <note>Mineral-rich liquids or petrifying agents.</note> turning all sorts of things—even *metals* themselves (x)—into *stone*, as has been discovered by the remaining impressions. There are also accounts of *antimony* and *copper* turned into *lead* and *copper*; of the ripening of *mineral mines* of *lead* and *gold*; and of substances consuming *dung* and *urine* and similar exchanges mentioned previously. Because of this, I am led to believe that a very vigorous and strong *medicine* would be able to change other things besides *metals*—especially minerals—into gold. (y) Some of our *experts* <note>original: "men"</note> say no, because other substances lack the foundation of *quicksilver* <note>mercury</note>, which is the bond of friendship and unity. I admit it is very difficult compared to the standard method, and yet I maintain it is possible.

<margin>(z) *Marsilio Ficino, On Obtaining Life from the Heavens, book 3, chapter 3, page 175.*
* *Jean Fernel, On the Hidden Causes of Things, book 2, chapter 17.*</margin>
*And thus you have seen the ability of Hermes' Medicine to turn base metals into gold by three different ways. First, as it is sown and rises again to be made into a medicine, which I call "begetting." Second, by changing the small amount of food given to it into its own nature, to make it increase and grow in quantity and size, which I term "nourishment." And lastly, by changing the great abundance of material with which we pair it—meeting it halfway in the intermediate nature of gold—which is the best transformation and the goal of our purpose. I may well call this "mixture," though (z) Ficino and * Fernel name it "begetting" as well. It is indeed a kind of begetting; but because it does not follow the natural way, let that go, and let us keep to our order.*

## CHAP. III.

### *That the Philosopher's Stone will turn base metals with as much advantage as we desire.*

But how shall our *Son of Gold* <note>original: "Son of Gold"; a common alchemical term for the Philosopher's Stone or the perfected metallic seed</note> be able to subdue and turn so much of base *metals* with so little expense and labor, and so great a return and gain as we have promised? It is for three causes: First, for the fitness and readiness of the material to be changed; and then for the great abundance and strength of the changing *workman* <note>The "workman" refers to the active principle or catalyst within the alchemical medicine</note> (to send away the lightest still first and foremost); and lastly, for his increase in store and quantity, which may be made either by sowing or nourishing the *Son of Gold* without end and number—starting first with sowing.

There are several ways of sowing and making this our *Medicine*: One is an excellent way, but a bare, naked, and lonely way; because if *Gold* can be made open and fit to be worked—as is necessary for a *seed* within himself—the less contamination there is of unclean material, the more excellent and mighty will he rise again. This way has been taken sometimes by deep and hardworking *Wits* <note>original: "Wits"; meaning scholars or ingenious philosophers</note>, but very seldom, because it is very hard, long, and irksome, and therefore we will leave it also. But I leave it chiefly because it goes against my purpose mentioned above; for if he be sown alone, he cannot be used in an increased amount, whereas we desire to augment his heap and quantity. Then there are two kinds of grounds, and yet both are of one kind,

which we may put unto him to corrupt him easily, and raise him again with increase in quantity. Consider for yourself what increase in store one grain of corn will yield within a few times sowing. When I had a little leisure, I did once calculate what one grain—by the increase of *fifty* (which happens often)—would arise to in seven times sowing, and I wearied myself in an endless matter. It was a greater *sum* than any man would <margin>78,125,000,000,000.</margin> think; I have forgotten it, so you who have leisure may calculate it. Because of the closeness of the material and the power of the agent, one part may be able to transform a hundred; then we shall supply and overtake all the lack and hindrance. Let us see.

<margin>(l) What if all things were even subject to one single form? What prevents metals from differing in appearance to the common eye? From these, whatever has emerged as more impure, when cast into their earthly hearts, will by art more quickly cast off all plague, etc. <note>original Latin: Quid si cuncta etiam sint uni subdita formæ... And first, let us speak again of the material, because it is the shorter and easier matter. The straight affinity and nearness of the *metals* to one another we have revealed above (l), when we found them all to be one thing, differing only by certain "hang-bys" <note>term: "hang-bys" refers to accidental or external qualities rather than the essential nature</note> of cleanness, fineness, closeness, and color. We also found that they were all made at first of *quicksilver* <note>mercury</note>, a foul and greasy thing by comparison, and then were greatly grimed and bespotted again by that foul, earthly *brimstone* <note>sulfur</note> which afterwards came upon them. Because of this, they were all gross and poorly colored, open and subject to fire and other spoiling enemies, until—by long, gentle, and natural *concoction*—all the foul and gross material was cleansed and refined. Thus, the metal was made ready to take on good color (as we see in *plants* and all things) and to gather itself up closely together. Likewise, it became weighty because of the high amount of fine material in a narrow space (whereas the heaviness of <margin>(m) Avicenna, *Tractatulus* (as found in the *Theatrum Chemicum*); Albertus Magnus, book 8, chapter 2.</margin> *lead* and *quicksilver* flows from their rawness). ...great closeness, left behind, to cause division and dissolution—which is destruction—nor is there any greasy substance (the fuel of fire) remaining.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Therefore, we see the close relationship of *metals* and the ease with which they can be changed into one another. This is especially true if we work upon silver, which is half *gold* already. Silver lacks nothing of *gold* except either a long, gentle concoction <note>original: "concoction"; a process of ripening or purifying through heat</note> or, in its place (because we cannot wait), a strong and fierce heat equivalent to it. This process first cleanses out all that gross and greasy *substance* and then brings the proper color upon it.

<margin>
(n) Those who have proposed a fictitious first matter and a shadowy foundation of nature. <note>original: "Qui materiam primam fictitiam et umbratile naturae fundamentum posuerunt." Anonymous, Enchiridion Physicae Restitutae, Canon 13.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So, I cannot help but wonder at those *men*—if they are truly learned—who, in their criticism of this unknown *art*, claim the *substance* is unfit to be changed. They say that *metals*, being of various kinds and natures, cannot be transformed until they are first reduced back into that original *substance* <note>The "Materia Prima" or first matter from which all things are made</note> from which they were first created and fashioned. They argue that we do not achieve this simply by melting them, and that such a reduction is not easy <note>original: "eath"</note> to perform.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is a sign that they either never knew, or at that time did not remember, the *nature* of a *metal* or of the first *substance*. Perhaps they mean the *Grecian* (n) theory of a supposed first matter—an empty and naked *substance* without shape, yet capable of receiving all shapes. This is even that middle (o) state of a thing that lasts only a moment when it is passing from one form to another through the process of making and marring (which our *men*, following *Hippocrates*, rightly call "changing"). If I yielded and quickly granted—along with *Geber*, *Arnold*, *Lully*, and many more learned *men* on our side—that in that very violent work of changing, the *metal* was significantly altered and broken down, even into *dust* of another fashion, then it was quite marred and stripped of its old *nature* and being, and passed even through the midst of that naked *substance*...

unto another kind and fashion: I think I might drive them to "blow the seek" <note>original: "blow the seek"; a phrase meaning to be at a loss or searching in vain</note>, as they say, and they would not know what to answer.

But if they mean—as it seems they do—that we should not melt our **metal**, but bring it back again unto its nearest beginning and material, **quicksilver** <note>mercury</note>, and then put our shape and form upon it (according to the natural sowing of **gold** upon its base ground mentioned above), they are deceived, not knowing the nature of **metals**. For they are not of different <margin>(p) Petrus Bonus of Ferrara, *The Precious Pearl*, chapter 20. *Theatrum Chemicum*, volume 3, page 760.</margin> kinds and beings, as they claim, but are all one thing, differing only by degrees of "baking," like various loaves made from the same dough. It would be madness, if any of them <margin>(q) Dionysius Zacharias, *Theatrum Chemicum*, volume 3, page 797.</margin> lacked baking, to lead it backward <note>original: "lead him back"; to revert the substance to a raw state</note>, or to mar and spoil its form, rather than to bake it better in that same form and being. And so did **Nature** in the ground: in baking **quicksilver** or **lead** into **gold**, she went forward and not backward with the **matter**. Nay, why do I go so far with them? They never noticed the nature of their own words which they use in their own **philosophy**, where "changing" is only a moving or shifting of those "hang-bys" called **accidents** <term>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential qualities (like color or weight) that can change while the essential "substance" remains the same.</term>, while the form, kind, and being of the thing remain.

Then, if the material is so fit, let us see what the worker <note>the alchemical agent or "medicine"</note> is—not in what has been done already, but in its force and power. Its **strength** and **power** are seen in two things: **purging** and **coloring**. For first, it must mightily show itself in purging and driving out all the gross greasiness of the material; and then, when all is fine, clear, and dense, it ought to stretch itself at large and spread far forth in color upon it. For although long and gentle purging by **concoction** <note>the process of ripening or "digesting" a substance through heat</note> breeds and brings good color by itself, yet this our short and violent **heat** proportioned...

# Chapter III. The Way to Bliss.

The process mentioned does not act in that way (as I showed above in the discourse of *binding and coloring*), but must necessarily bring *Color* with it already *coined* <note>original: "Coyned"; meaning already prepared or established</note>.

So, when he purges the *material* <note>original: "stuff"</note> secretly, he does not simply draw out the foul and gross matter and then depart from the work entirely, as the foul-purging *Binders* did. Instead, being a clean and fine substance like the *nature* of a *living being* <note>original: "wight"; an archaic term for a person or sentient creature</note>, he purges by *Digestion* and *Expulsion*. He drives out the foul and dissimilar parts as *waste* <note>original: "Leavings"</note>, while taking and embodying the fine and clean parts into himself for his *food and nourishment*.

Then let us see how this work of *Purging* is performed, for that is everything; the *Color* depends upon this, and both are accomplished as one, as we shall hear in the conclusion of this treatise. If nothing purges except *Heat* through concoction <note>The process of heating a substance to mature or purify it</note>, and this heat must always be measured according to the need and requirement of the *work* at hand; and if we must scour a hundred times as much *material* in the space of one, two, or three hours at most (for that is the required task), then we would have need of a marvelously fiery *Medicine*—scarce to be found within the compass of the world and *Nature*—in addition to great outward *heat* to prick it forward. It must show itself a hundred times fiercer than a *Binder*, which was scarcely able, in a longer time and with stronger heat, to scour and purge even one part of that same *material*.

This is a marvelously difficult point; I must whet my *thoughts*, my *memory*, and all the *weapons of my wit* for this matter. If we search everywhere and rifle through all the *corners of nature* <note>original: "Kind"</note>, we shall find no *fire* in the world so hot and fierce as *Lightning*, which is able to kill *plants* and *living beings*, melt *metals*, and perform other such

We may find many more such strange deeds done by that most violent **fire**. Therefore, our fiery **workman** <note>The alchemist or the alchemical agent.</note>, if he is tasked—as he is—to work wonders as great as these, must be as fierce and vehement as the fire of **lightning**, as it is also sometimes called in our **philosophy**.

Let us match these two together and see how they can agree, so that when all things are closely examined and, as it were, struck together, the light of **truth** may at last appear and shine forth from that **comparison**. Let us, as **Tully** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note> says and does, launch and row a little easily at the first setting out, before we hoist up the sail.

...quench a little **fire**, yet a strong (x) **fire** consumes him and puts him out of being: ) because, I say, like the **firefly** he does live and flourish in the **fire**: whereas, indeed, (y) **gold**, like all other **metals**, is cold and watery, far from the nature of **fire**. And yet it is not the outward appearance of the body alone that makes a fiery nature, but sometimes the inward quality performs the work of **fire** (if we speak broadly, as is the common custom). And so the **starfish** in the sea (z) burns everything she touches; and a cold spring in (a) **Slavonia** <note>A historical region in modern-day Croatia.</note> sets on fire any **cloth** spread upon it; and to come nearer, by such a fiery force does the water of the **Styx** in **Thessaly** <note>The author refers to a legendary spring in Greece believed to have corrosive properties, named after the river of the underworld.</note> pierce through any **vessel** except for a **horse's hoof**.

But now that we have come into the deep, let us hoist up our sails and speak more properly and **philosophically**, and more to the point; let us, I say, hear the **nature** of **fire** and how it comes to be. **Fire**, as they define it—and as we shall find if we observe its **origin**—is a very **hot** and **dry substance**. The first cause of **fire** is **motion**, gathering and driving much dry **matter** into a narrow space; by stirring and striving for its life and **existence**, this matter is made even more dense, fine, and hot than its **nature** will allow or suffer. And so it breaks out at last and is turned into another larger, thinner, drier, and hotter **nature** called **fire**. From this, the great underground **fires** in **Etna**, **Hecla**, and many other places grow and spring at first, when the **cold** drives a heap of **hot earthly breaths** and **vapors** either into a tight circle or along through narrow and rough places, rubbing and wringing out **fire**, which the natural fatness <note>original: "fatness"; here meaning the oily or combustible richness of the soil.</note> of the ground feeds forever.

<margin>(b) Pliny, Book 2, Chapter 37. And Cardano, *On Subtlety*, Book 2, page 55.</margin>
So the *Star*, called (b) the *Hellen-star* <note>original: "Hellen-star"; likely referring to St. Elmo's Fire, often associated in antiquity with Helen of Troy or her brothers Castor and Pollux</note>, which lights upon the tackle of a *Ship* (a very dangerous sign) and upon falling melts *Copper Vessels*, etc., comes from a heap of such *Vapors* carried up by crosswinds. Similarly, by rubbing *Millstones*, *Flints*, and such materials, we see *Fire* arise in the same manner; and this is the way all *Fire* is produced—others flow from this single source, constantly sowing one another, as it were. 

But if the *substance* <note>original: "stuff"</note> of this *Fire* is tough and hard, and then, once it is transformed into *Fire*, it is moved rapidly again, it becomes—for these two reasons—a marvelously hot, fierce, and violent *fire*. From this springs all the force of *Lightnings*; for lightning is nothing else but a heap of thick and *Sulfurous* <note>original: "Brimstony"</note> *vapors* (as some reasonably hold) which are beaten together closely by the coldness of the *Cloud*. Now, being suddenly turned into a larger and thinner *Element* than it was before (when it was *Earth* and *Water*), its old place will not hold it. Thus, by the force of *Nature* striving for room and liberty, it tears the *Clouds* in that manner which we hear in *Thunder*, and finally bursts out at a great and swift pace, as we see in *Lightning*. This swiftness, combined with the toughness of the finely-wrought *substance*, creates a violence greater than all other *Fires* in the world.

Now, regarding the *Son of Gold* <note>A metaphorical term for the Philosopher's Stone or the perfected metallic seed</note> and the *Medicine of Hermes* <note>The Alchemical Elixir</note>: what kind of *Fire* is it, since it cannot be such an *Elemental*, extremely hot, and dry *Fire*? For this medicine is temperate and has all its qualities in balance, with none working more than the others. And yet, indeed, because of the fine and tough (and therefore) mighty *Body* in which these qualities are seated, they work together in equality much more

# Chapter III. — The Way to Bliss

<margin>The power of the Heavenly fire.</margin>
This fire works more forcibly than extremely imbalanced, cold, and dry *poisons* can work alone. Just as fast—and even faster—than those poisons devour and destroy temperate bodies, these spirits overthrow their opposites. I showed before what kind of *fire* this is, and how it is more stuffed with *heavenly spirits* than all other things. Thus, it is a *heavenly fire*, which is much more effectual in power and mightier in action than common fire because of its exceeding subtlety. It is able to pierce through *rocks* and all things, whereas common fire is quickly stopped.

<margin>How the fire is liberated from the body of gold.</margin>
You might say, "I admit this, if that *heavenly fire* were active, free, and at full liberty; but it is fast bound up in a hard *body*." Then I will tell you the *reasoning*; bend your wits to it. *Gold* at first was fully freighted with the most piercing fire in the *world*. *Art* <note>original: "Art" — here meaning alchemy or the skilled application of chemical processes</note> then came and worked it into a most fine, flowing *oil*, and so unbound the fire and set it at full liberty. It is not as free, indeed, as it is in *heaven*, but as free as it can be in an *earthly body*. It is closely crowded together—which intensifies *heat*, as we see in a *burning-glass* <note>a magnifying lens used to concentrate sunlight to start a fire</note>—upon a most strange and mighty *body*, far above all things in the world. Lastly, with a violent outward *fire*, Art sent all these parts away to work together. 

Judge then, you who have judgment, whether it were not likely to stir itself as lustily as *lightning*? Compare them: the *heat* of these hot *spirits* is just as great; and even if it were not, their surpassing subtlety would easily make up the difference and make them equal. Indeed, perhaps when they are drawn and carried up close together, they might even surpass lightning. Certainly, the exceeding toughness of the *body*—as we see in *iron* and other metals—augments its heat greatly and carries it far beyond common limits.

<summary>The author argues that alchemical "Art" can liberate a powerful, piercing heavenly fire from the physical body of gold, creating a substance more potent than lightning or common fire. This process involves refining gold into a "fine flowing oil" to release its internal heat and spiritual power.</summary>

Now for the pace, it is much swifter, as driven by a much stronger *Mover* <note>original: "Mover"; referring to the primary force or catalyst of the reaction</note>, even as much as a *founder's fire* <note>The intense heat used by a metal-caster or blacksmith</note> surpasses in strength the top of a thick *cloud*. Therefore, because the *fire* is stronger and has the helps of *Body* and *Motion* far more favorable, the *fire* of the "Son of Gold" <note>An alchemical term for the refined essence or Elixir derived from gold</note> must necessarily surpass *lightning* in power and wonderful working. 

Then bethink yourself: with what ease and speed <margin>(c) See *The Spagyric Palladium*, chapter 21, pages 277–278.</margin> such a fiery *Medicine* <note>The alchemical Elixir</note> would likely pierce and break through, sift and search all about, and so scour and cleanse a great mass of foul *metals*? Fasten and bend your minds upon it: we see how a weak, watery, and earthly *breath* in a narrow place—within a *cloud*, a *grenade* <note>original: "Gramide"</note>, or a *gun* (all of which is but <margin>(d) Cardan, *On Subtlety*, book 21, page 704.</margin> *thunder*)—stirs itself and works marvelous deeds because it is so suddenly turned into a larger *element* <note>i.e., gas or vapor</note> and lacks room. 

What may we think then of the heaps of those rich <note>original: "fat"</note> vapors of *Heaven*, and of that most strong golden body, closely crouched together in a little room? Imagine when they are, in a narrow *vessel*, drawn out and spread abroad at large by a mighty *fire*, and thereby constantly pricked and egged forward. (For as long as the *fire* holds, they cannot be still, nor draw themselves back in again.) What thing in the sturdiest *metal* can be able to withstand them? How easily shall they cast down all that comes in their way, and break and bruise all to powder? The same strength, without the force of qualities; but I think it is rather because of its stronger, similar *Nature* seeking to devour them. Otherwise, it would pierce your hand, leather, and other such soft things, which it leaves untouched as being unlike itself and foreign. As for the qualities of *Quicksilver* <note>original: "Quick silver"; mercury</note>, it is a question of what they are and which one dominates. Some judge it to be very *cold*; some, on the other hand, consider it marvelously *hot* (such as *Paracelsus* <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</note> for one); some say it is most *moist*, and others *dry*. 

But, as it apparently possesses all these qualities, I deem it *temperate*, like the element that has sprung from it and is most like it—I mean *Gold*—though perhaps the qualities in mercury are not as equally balanced as they are in gold. But let the cause be what it will (as I do not like to dwell upon uncertain matters), the great *Spirit of Metals*, after it is first wrought into *Gold* and then into its "Son," our *Medicine* <note>The Philosopher's Stone or the Elixir.</note>, shall reasonably be a hundred times stronger and more able to perform its work, both for *Soul* and *Body*. 

Indeed, *Antimony* and *Lead* are much coarser than *Quicksilver*, and yet we see how they rend, tear, and consume base *metals* even to nothing. But what shall we say of *Plants*? There is as great a difference in sharpness and the ability to pierce between plants and *minerals* as there is between a *thorn* and a *needle*. And yet, as mentioned above, the gentle plant of the *Vine* and the mild *Dew of Heaven* yielded the material for a corrosive water <note>original: "eating water"; likely referring to a strong acid or solvent like aqua fortis.</note> capable, within three or four distillations, of devouring and dissolving *metals*.

What remains then? Yes, many things; I heard them whisper that although this *Stone* of ours has such thundering power, yet it might not serve our purpose, because it might consume all the *Metal* (as is the custom and forceful habit of such fierce things) without regard or choice for any specific part or portion. Therefore, in times past, they called it (σαρκοφάγος) *Flesh-eater* <note>original: "sarkophagos"; the origin of the word "sarcophagus."</note>, and made *Tombs* from it for the dead, and *Boots* for *Gouty men* <note>It was believed that the stone's properties could "eat away" the humors causing gout.</note>. But weigh this one example and our artificial *Stone* together: why may it not also have its choice, and save a specific part during this great waste and spoiling? They do not know why. And yet, there are many deep, hidden, and causeless properties in the bosom of *species* and *nature* <note>original: "kinde and nature"</note> which no man's wit is able to reach or see into; the *World* is full of them, even when *Art* is open and all its ways are known.

Furthermore, nothing eats and devours all the *material* <note>original: "stuffe"</note> which it overcomes; it only takes as much as is similar to itself and capable of being transformed. The rest it leaves behind as strange and untouchable. Indeed, in this way and no other does *lightning* and all *fire* eat and consume the *material* it subdues, turning the *air* and water into *fire* and leaving the *earth* and *ashes* behind. Is it not clear enough that all things seek their likes and shun their *contraries*? Yet, because these *mineral matters* have always been very strange and unfamiliar to the *Greeks*, I will set down one or two of the clearer examples. Why does *copperas water* <note>original: "Coppress water"; likely a reference to nitric acid or a sulfate solution used in refining</note> part and draw away *silver* from *gold*? It is because *copperas* is like *copper*, and copper is like *silver*. For as (p) *lead* is to *gold*, so is copper to *silver*; they are both very like one another in both weight and softness, and therefore they are considered *leprous gold* and *silver* <note>A common alchemical term for base metals that resemble precious ones but lack their "purity."</note>. 

To test this, try both together: when you have so parted *gold* and *silver*, cast in plates of *lead* and *copper*. The lead will cleave to the *gold*, and the copper (q) will cleave to the *silver*. But *silver* is more like *silver* than it is like *copper*; therefore, to part *silver* from *copper*, the *miners* are accustomed to season a lump of *lead* with a little *silver*. That softens the work and makes it ready; then, one part of *silver* draws the other part unto itself. 

Furthermore, observe *quicksilver* <note>term: mercury</note> (r). As she is strange in all things, so in this she is very wonderful. *Quicksilver*, I say—the *grandmother* of our *medicine* <note>The "medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the transformative alchemical agent.</note> and the spring of all her goodness—will quickly receive and swallow, in either heat or cold, her near friend and very similar, clean, fine, and temperate body of *gold*. (Therefore, as the one is termed unripe *gold*, so the other is called ripe *quicksilver*.) Meanwhile, she refuses the rest and keeps her distance, viewing them as foul, gross, and unlike her *nature*. 

The *miners* also have revealed this secret to us through their practice, when they part *gold* from the rest of the materials mashed together in a dust heap. Therefore, when this fine and clean body, *quicksilver*, is made by *nature* and *art* to be much finer and clearer—and again as much more piercing and *spiritual* and able to perform its task—how much more readily will she run to her like and devour it? # Chapter III. The Way to Bliss.

To close everything up: remember what I said, and what is most true and certain, that **Gold** is the densest and most full of fine, large-spreading material of anything else in the world. In this regard, it surpasses the wonderful gift of **Silver** a hundredfold. It is so remarkable that one **ounce** of **Gold**, even by the blunt skill of the **hammer**, may be drawn out and made to stretch over ten acres of ground.

Consider this one point well, and everything shall be plain and easy—I mean to those who are learned, for these are not matters for dull and confused wits to contemplate. If this spreading **metal** is then made into a fine, flowing **oil** and drawn out at length, and laid out very thinly by a vehement heat of **fire**, how far do you reasonably think it will spread? Such a vision might quickly dazzle the eye of the **understanding**; therefore, let us illustrate the matter using a metaphor, as **Plato** often does.

Imagine the difference in **fineness** and color between the **Sun** <note>The "Sun" is the alchemical symbol for Gold.</note> of **Gold** and **Silver** (if you choose to transform him, as I instructed you) to be like the difference between very fine red **sarcenet** <note>A fine, soft silk fabric.</note> and coarse white **sackcloth**. Imagine the silk is tightly packed together inside a **walnut shell**, while the sackcloth is packed as hard as possible into a very round **pot** the size of a quart—a size that would hold a hundred **walnuts**. 

You can see the bulk of both; and if you weigh them, one will prove to be a hundred times the weight of the other. But if you draw them out and spread them abroad one upon another, one shall overtake, match, and fit the other on all sides. Now, one is very coarse and large, and the other fine and small, as is apparent by their threads; yet the small may be just as strong as the large, as we commonly see in a little **gall** <note>A bitter substance, often used to mean something highly concentrated or potent.</note>, **poison**, etc. Then, when these two encounter one another (as we must suppose they will), they shall, by necessity, hurt and

# The Way to Bliss. Book III.

They change each other equally; and so, the exceeding *fine* and *gross* mingled together make a middle thread. The extreme *red* and *white* colors, carried within their bodies, also take on a *yellow* middle ground. Even so, you must imagine that when a hundred *ounces* of *silver* and one *ounce* of our *Medicine* are both beaten by the *fire* and driven out to their full length and furthest thinness, every part overtakes, fits, and reaches the other. Because the small part is as strong as the large part, they strive until one overcomes, consumes, and turns the other, so that neither shall be quite erased. Instead, both are equally changed and mingled into a third *middle thing* <note>original: "Mean thing"</note>, both in fineness and color, which is *gold*. For the *Medicine* is as far above gold as gold is beyond *silver*, both in fineness and color, and all other properties whatsoever.

And so you see the matter of color is also settled, which I kept for this section, and which seems like a wonder in some *men's* sight. I hope you will not ask me how *gold* obtained this high red and unnatural <note>original: "unkindly"</note> color, unless you are ignorant of how all such *attachments* <note>original: "Hang-bies"; referring to secondary qualities or accidents of a substance</note> shift and change up and down without hurting the thing that carries them. You must also realize <margin>(v) *Pietro Bono of Ferrara, The Precious Pearl, chapter 2. Theatrum Chemicum, Volume 5, pages 622, 623.*</margin> that by a natural <note>original: "kindly"</note> course—whereby all soft and alterable things, when gently and softly boiled, grow first *black*, then *white*, next *yellow*, and lastly *red*, where they remain at the peak of color—we see our seed of gold changed and drawn up into this new, unusual color.

And thus you have at last all the reasoning which I saw, or at least thought good to commit to writing, for the truth of *Hermes* or the *Philosopher's Stone* and *Medicines*. This explains why it is the ready way to bring all *men* to all the *bliss* and *happiness* in the *world*; that is, to *long life*, *health*, *youth*, and *riches*.

*Wisdom and Virtue; it is now time to sit down and take our rest.*

### CHAPTER IV.

#### *That Gold may be wrought into such a fine oil as we speak of.*

<margin>(w) Paracelsus, Book on Long Life, Volume 6, page 114.</margin>
<margin>(x) Sendivogius, Treatise on Sulfur, chapter on Fire, page 33.</margin>

But I think I hear them mutter among themselves that there has not been a single reason given yet—not even one. They argue this because everything stands upon a feigned and supposed foundation; and since that foundation is nothing, everything built upon it must inevitably come to nothing. 

For just as (<note>original: "w"</note>) Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</note> concludes in his supposed *Paradise*—at the end of his *High* opinions—that if it were possible to be created by any labor of *wisdom*, it would undoubtedly prove to be a notable place for *Long Life* and *Health*; even so might one think of this *Stone of Gold*. If any *Art* or skill were able to create it, it would without doubt work those wonders mentioned before. 

But his *Paradise* is impossible to create (if he means literally what he says, and not the *Philosophers' Stone* <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting immortality.</note>, to which his words might be interpreted) unless he could enclose himself in a place free, first, from the contagion and force of the outward *Earth, water, and weather*—and therefore even from the *Fire of Heaven* and *Light* also. Secondly, (<note>original: "x"</note>) it would require a place where all the Beginnings were in their pure and naked *Nature*, which they call a *Fifth nature* <note>original: "Quinta Essentia" or Quintessence; the pure, celestial element beyond the four earthly elements.</note>, which exists nowhere except in *Heaven*, and which would be a miracle even to conceive of. And lastly, it would be impossible unless he could live without *Food* and his *Excrement* <note>original: "Leavings"</note>, which both the *learned and unlearned* consider ridiculous to imagine.

In the same way, it is considered just as difficult and unlikely in common opinion that *Gold* may be spoiled and brought to nothing (as it must be first) and then restored and raised to such dig—

nity; because just as *Heaven* is forever one and unchangeable—since within it all *Beginnings* <note>The primary elements or first principles.</note> are weighed so evenly and securely tied together in full harmony, unable to ever clash or be loosened—in the same way, *Gold* is so dense and firm. Because of the secure and equal mixture of its fine *earth* and *water* <note>In alchemy, gold was thought to be composed of highly purified elemental earth and water.</note>, no force (y) of nature—neither *earth, air, nor water*—can harm it. Not even *fire*, even when it is aided by *lead, antimony*, or any such fierce and hot *stomach* <note>term: stomach; here meaning a corrosive substance or intense heat capable of "digesting" or dissolving metals</note> that easily consumes all other things, will ever affect it. What should be said to this?

Although *I* confess that this is the main foundation and support of the entire work and structure, I did not simply assume it or take it for granted as if *I* were dealing with *Geometry*. Instead, I left it to be proved in the most appropriate place. As for that supposed *Paradise*, it is difficult to judge because the author only glanced at the subject and then left it as something unlawful to be told. Nevertheless, a *Man* may imagine in his thoughts just as well as that author did (for *I* suspect he had not actually tested it) what might be achieved and what *Nature* will permit.

# Chapter IV. *The Way to Bliss.*

It is easily possible, as *I* will reveal to you as far as my permission will allow me—which has been large indeed, and must be, because *I* made a large promise at first (perhaps too rashly, though with good intentions) which must be fulfilled and performed.

<margin>(b) *Aristotle, Physics, Book 8, Chapter 10. See also Hannibal Rossel of Calabria in his commentary on Hermes Trismegistus's Pymander, Book 1, commentary 16, question 1, chapter 2.*</margin>

(b) *Aristotle* says like a wise *philosopher* that *nature* makes her *creatures* and *subjects* apt to move and rest—that is, changeable. And again, he says that a *body* that is finite cannot be without end and everlasting. Therefore, when *heaven* ever moves and *earth* ever rests, it is beyond the compass of *nature* and springs from a more *divine* cause. 

If his *rule* be true, as it is most certain, then *gold*—a thing that is neither infinite nor an extraordinary and divine *work*, but made by the ordinary hand of *nature* <note>original: "Kind"</note> as we heard above—must necessarily decay and perish eventually; it cannot last forever. And if *nature* can dissolve it, much more shall she perform it with the help of *art* <note>original: "Art," referring here to alchemy or technical skill</note>. 

What was said previously regarding *fire* and its helpers is irrelevant. Why do they improve *gold*? Only because they remove its *enemies* <note>impurities or corrosive elements</note> when *nature* had secretly set them about the gold to destroy it. In this way, even a simple *stick*, as I said above, may be saved from decay if protected. But let *nature* have her way underground, or let skill be applied above ground, and they shall cause its enemies in time to spoil and consume it. We cannot tell (as they say in the *country-style*)...

<summary>The author argues that gold is a natural, finite substance and therefore subject to decay and dissolution, refuting the idea that it is inherently eternal while suggesting that alchemical "Art" can manipulate these natural processes.</summary>

it may be a divine and not a natural work, for we see it is everlasting. Go on then, let it be so: I will overtake them by that argument as well. For just as we know—which *Aristotle* did not—that both *Heaven* and *Earth* may be, and once must be, marred and changed by the same divine cause that made them both; so we may think that *Gold*, even if it were a divine work, might fall to decay and perish by a similar skill that follows the divine *Pattern*.

But what is that divine *Pattern*? And how shall *men* be like unto *God*? It is even by the goodness of God, who has (as I said above) left this *Pattern* open in all places and easy to be seen by those who seek to be like the main *Pattern* <note>The "main Pattern" refers to the *Imago Dei*, or the image of God in which humanity was created.</note> after which we were all made. And this, as *Hermes* (c) says, is a gentle and clever separation, by which he declares both the great work <note>The creation of the universe (Macrocosm).</note> and this, our little work <note>The creation of the Philosopher's Stone or the human body (Microcosm).</note>, were made and woven—and so are to be marred and unwoven again. This is to secretly show us that no great and skillful work is performed by such rude and smith-like violence as you speak of—for as the poet says, (d) "Force void of counsel falls by its own weight" <note>original: *consilii expers mole ruit sua*</note>—but rather by gentle skill and wisdom. 

We may see this plainly and fittingly by looking at a thing that is, in its virtue and price (I mean in worldly estimation), most near unto Gold: the noble and untamed (e) *Diamond*. When the diamond comes into the smith's hands, it will yield neither to fire nor hammer; rather, it will break the hammer before it will break itself. It will not so much as become hot (as *Pliny* says), and certainly will not be hurt (as they all grant) by the fire. And yet, by the gentle means (g) of lion's or goat's blood—though these are "hot" bloods, the one by its nature and the other by the disease of a continual fever—you may so soften and subdue this stout and noble stone that it will yield to be handled at your pleasure.

# Chapter IV. The Way to Bliss.

Indeed, by the flowing tears of molten *lead* (a substance not as hot as it might be), gold will completely relent and melt along with it. Even so, we may judge of *Gold*: that although the more roughly it is handled, the less it yields—as is the nature of stubborn things—there is a gentle and heavenly skill, and a way to soften it and make it willingly yield and go to corruption <note>original: "Corrrruption"; in alchemy, "corruption" refers to the necessary breakdown or "death" of a metal before it can be reborn as the Philosopher's Stone</note>. However, this method, like the other, is not common or known publicly, and there is no reason it should be.

<margin>(b) Proverbs 22:2. Ecclesiastes 33:16 & 42:24. & Francesco Giorgio, *The Harmony of the World*, Song 2, Volume 4, Chapter 9.</margin>
But why do we need to fly with *Aristotle* to any divine shelter? Just as *Gold* was made by the common course of Nature <note>original: "Kind"</note>, and must die and perish the same way, so this skill of ours does not need to be fetched from any hidden or divine secret (whatever our *Alchemists* <note>original: "Men"</note> say to keep away the unworthy). Instead, it comes from a plain *Art*, following the ordinary and daily steps of *Nature* in all her natural works and *changes*. Therefore, mark and chew my words well, and *I* will open the whole *Art* unto you.

<margin>The whole universe is necessarily composed of opposites. <note>original Greek: ἐξ ἐναντίων ἐξ ἀνάγκης τὸ τὸ πᾶν</note> Plotinus, *Enneads* 1, book 8. & Prosper of Aquitaine, *Short Work on the Providence of God*, page 522. & Michael Sendivogius, *On Sulphur*, chapter on Fire, page 31. & Paracelsus, *On the Generation of Natural Things*, book 3, volume 6, page 208.</margin>
(b) *GOD*, because he intended for none of these lower creatures to be eternal (as was said before), first sowed the four seeds of strife in the *world*—one to fight with and destroy the other. And if that would not suffice, as it will not here, he made those things that spring from them of the same *nature*. There is nothing in the *world* (i) that does not have its match, either similar or contrary, able to combat it and devour it. But a *similar* thing eats up and consumes its like with more ease and more naturally than a contrary thing does, because of their nearness and agreement.

<margin>(i) Pliny, book 8, chapter 21. & Aegidius de Vadis, *Theatrum Chemicum*, Volume 2, page 98.</margin>
Therefore, if Nature intends to spoil *Gold* and make it perish—because it is such a strong thing—she takes the nearest and most natural way: she sets a "strange like" <note>This refers to a substance that shares a similar nature with gold (likely mercury or a specific solvent) but acts as a predator to dissolve it.</note> upon it to eat it up and consume it. What more should *I* say, or how could I say it more plainly? You know the thing...

<margin>(t) Ep. &c. regarding the philosopher's stone. Theatrum Chemicum volume 5, page 894. Artephius's secret book, page 170.</margin>
Let then the (t) dew of this *starry blood* <note>original: "Starry blood"; likely referring to a celestial or refined mercurial essence</note> beat about the womb, and your *seed* shall *rejoice and prosper—yet so much the better and sooner also, if that blood be whole and sound,* (u) *and consisting of all its parts.* <margin>(v) Praise of the holy Harmony of imperfect chemistry, decade 1, page 114.</margin> Therefore, it is no marvel if the *world* misses this *happy stone* <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>, when they think to make it above the *ground*. I say they must either climb up to *heaven*, or go down deep within the *earth*, for there and nowhere else is this natural <note>original: "kindly"; meaning natural or innate to its kind</note> *heat*.

<margin>(w) Basil Valentine, key 5, page 38.</margin>
*Living creatures* <note>original: "Wights"</note> are heated with *blood*, and *plants* with *earth*, but (w) *minerals with a heavenly breath*. To be short: because *men* are too heavy to mount up to *heaven*, you must go down to the midst of the *earth* and put your *seed* into its *mine* again, so that it may take that *influence of heaven* equally round about it.

Muse and reflect well upon my words, you who are fit and skilled in *nature*, for this is a very *natural heat*, and yet here all the world is blinded. Indeed, (x) *if a man could read little and think much upon the ways of nature, he might discover this art more quickly by a sudden impulse of the mind than find it by reason or hard labor.* <note>original Latin: "Ut perquirenti mentis impetu occurrat citiùs, quàm ratione aut sudore inveniatur."</note> <margin>(x) Hermetic Secrets of Philosophy, Canon 36.</margin>

---

What remains now? We have the entire method to break down and spoil our *gold*, and that was the main difficulty, I believe. For if it is once put down so naturally, it will surely rise again, or else all *nature* will fail and lose her established custom. And if it rises, it shall rise always increased tenfold in *power* <note>original: "Vertue"</note>; I mean, if it is not degraded as the *seeds* of *plants* and *living creatures* are, and as the *seed* of *gold* was by that inferior method mentioned above, using the *ground* that corrupts it.

So if a poisoned *plant* or *living creature* is rotted in a *glass*,

<summary>The author explains that the Philosopher's Stone requires a specific "natural heat" found only deep within the earth or in the heavens, arguing that the "seed" of gold must be returned to its mineral source to be refined. He emphasizes that deep contemplation of nature is more effective than excessive reading or physical labor in discovering these alchemical secrets.</summary>

<keywords>Alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Natural Heat, Seed of Gold, Basil Valentine, Artephius, Mineral Transformation, Hermetic Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the refinement of substances through alchemical corruption and heat, leading to the creation of a "Heavenly Nature" or Elixir.</meta>

<margin>(y) Girolamo Cardano, *On Subtlety*, Book 18, page 662.</margin>
<margin>(z) Anonymous, *Counsel on the Marriage of the Sun and Moon, or of the Three Words*, part 3, *Theatrum Chemicum*, Volume 5, page 563.</margin>

She will rise again as a most *venomous beast*, and perhaps a *cockatrice* <note>A mythical serpent-king said to kill with a glance; here used as a metaphor for a substance of intense, concentrated power</note>, for that is her *offspring*. In the same way, if you corrupt a good *plant*, it will turn into (y) a *worm* or something similar, but with greatly increased *virtue* <note>original: "vertue"; meaning its inherent medicinal or transformative power</note>. What is the reason for this? It is because the same temperament and proportion of qualities increases in power as the *body* is refined. When the gross material that hinders its action is stripped away from the obstacles of the *body*, (z) all the qualities shall be raised equally. They shall work mightily, devouring and drawing things to their own *nature* more than anything else. This happens because they are not only free and in their clean, naked *nature*, but are also seated upon a most subtle and tough *body* that is able to pierce, divide, and subdue all things.

Again, both *metals* and *stones* are finer and better the more *heat* they have (as is seen in hotter *countries*). Therefore, the more often they are brought back to their *first matter* <note>The original, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made</note> and baked with temperate *heat*, the more they increase in goodness.

And if a substance is brought to such a temperate fineness—that is, to such a *Heavenly Nature*—then it no longer keeps the nature of a metal in respect to any quality, except for the durability of the *body*. Nor does it retain the nature of any other gross food or medicine. Therefore, it cannot be an *enemy* to our *nature*, nor does it require ordinary digestion in our *body*. Instead, it straightway flies out, as I said before, and scours most swiftly through all parts of the *body* by extraordinary means and passages, just as *nature* herself does. Thus, it joins with our *first moisture* <note>The "radical moisture" or vital fluid believed to sustain life and youth</note> and performs all other good deeds belonging to this BLISS of the *body*, in such a way—and even better—than what I have shown you regarding a *fifth-nature* <note>original: "fifth-nature" or Quintessence; the purest, most transparent essence of a thing</note>.

And so *Erastus* <note>Thomas Erastus (1524–1583), a Swiss physician and theologian known for his fierce opposition to Paracelsian alchemy</note> and all other slanderous mouths may—

<summary>The author explains how alchemical refinement through "corruption" and heat releases the inner power of a substance, transforming it into a "Heavenly Nature" capable of healing the human body instantly. This refined essence, or Quintessence, is argued to be superior to ordinary medicine and capable of merging with the body's vital moisture.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the third book of "The Way to Bliss," serving as the author's final defense of his work and a closing benediction.</meta>

# The Way to Bliss. BOOK III.

Now begin again, for there is not a word spoken to any purpose, because everything rests upon a false and unknown foundation. A *wise man* would first have known the *nature* of the thing he speaks of, if he did not intend to provoke *laughter* in those who hear him and understand the matter.

But indeed, these *railers* <note>original: "Railers"; those who complain or use abusive language</note> are safe enough, because these things are so hidden and unknown to the *world* that no *man*, except one of their own *household*, can detect or challenge them. Therefore, I took in hand this difficult and dangerous labor—which all of our *ancestors* have refused—both so that these critics might be ashamed of their wrongful slanders, and so that the *wise* and *well-disposed* might see and profit from the *truth* of so great a *blessing*.

<margin>Even the worthy Homer nods off sometimes. <note>original: "Indignior quandoque (id est quandocunque) dormitat Homerus." Horace, Ars Poetica.</note></margin>

If they ever find it, let them thank *GOD* and use it, as no doubt they will, to do good to good *men*. If *I* have slipped in *words* or the *truth* of the matter, let them consider how common such errors are among *men*, and weigh the good and bad together. Otherwise, *Homer* himself, when he slips now and then, could never escape criticism; and yet he was dealing with an easy subject (for a *man may invent fictions forever*) and had *Orpheus* and *Musaeus* <note>Legendary Greek poets and musicians</note>, I think, before him. But you see the difficulty of this *material*, although you do not see my *pattern* <note>original: "Pattern"; likely referring to the divine or alchemical source text</note> because, to my knowledge, it is not to be seen anywhere in the *world*. But what do I care? These *men* whom I respect will take all things well, and as for the rest, I passed them by long ago without concern.

-> THE END. <-

-> *All glory be ever and only to him that is, that was, and that is to come. Amen, Amen, Amen.* <-

<summary>The author concludes his treatise by defending his work against ignorant critics and dedicating the labor to the benefit of wise men and the glory of God.</summary>
